VECTOR LIFTINGS FOR PRODUCTS OF PROBABILITY SPACES AND MEASURABLE MODIFICATIONS OF STOCHASTIC PROCESSES Thanks: The research of the first author was supported by NSERC Thanks: The research of the second author was partially supported by the University of Piraeus Research Center.
M. R. Burke, N. D. Macheras, W. Strauss

Abstract
. We investigate the properties of linear primitive liftings ρ : ℒ p ​ ( μ ) → ℒ p ​ ( μ ) \rho\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu) for probability spaces ( X , Σ , μ ) (X,\varSigma,\mu) , which are linear maps selecting a representative from each class for almost everywhere equality. We call them vector liftings. They have the advantage over liftings or linear liftings that they exist for all p ∈ [ 0 , ∞ ] p\in[0,\infty] , not only for p = ∞ p=\infty . Their relationship to products is still not clear, but we establish existence of (strong) product vector liftings for products of two factors. The vector liftings which are 2 2 -marginals with respect to a suitable product yielded a characterization of stochastic processes having a measurable modification modelled on one discovered by Musiał, and led to a proof of the characterization that does not use liftings. The improvement relies on results on extending a measure by null sets that apply when the family of new null sets is an ideal of a σ \sigma -algebra larger than the domain of the measure, but not necessarily an ideal of the power set. These allow us to reduce or eliminate completeness assumptions on the measures from several of our results.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 28A51; Secondary 28A35, 54B10, 60G05, 60A10, 60B05.

1.
Introduction

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be a measure space,
0\leq p\leq\infty
. By a vector lifting for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
, we mean a linear map
\rho\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
mapping each constant function to itself, and satisfying
\rho(f)=_{\mu}f
and
f=_{\mu}g
implies
\rho(f)=\rho(g)
for all
f,g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
(not to be confused with linear liftings which also have to be order-preserving).
Vector liftings for
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mu)
have been known to experts in the theory of liftings at least since the sixties of the last century but were left undeveloped. They were considered trivial in the times of the predominance of functional analysis, where the interest was in positive linear and multiplicative linear liftings instead (compare
[
18
]
) and there seemed to be no evidence for getting interesting results, when applying it to the topics we deal with in this paper. Not even an extension of the definition to spaces
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
,
0\leq p<\infty
was considered, where the vector lifting is a substitute for the non-existent positive linear lifting.
We investigate the notion of (strong) vector liftings on (topological) measure spaces, focusing on how vector liftings for products relate to vector liftings for the factors.

Many of our results concern extensions of measures by adding null sets. We give a general framework (Definition
2.1
) for doing this by considering triples
(X,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{I})
, where
\mathcal{A}
is a
\sigma
-algebra of subsets gof
X
, and
\mathcal{I}
is a
\sigma
-ideal of a
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}
with
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
. We develop in Section
3
properties of extensions of measures by null sets, focusing on products where we add ideals which are skew products. These allow us to prove versions of facts about complete ideals in the absence of completeness.

Section
4
establishes basic properties of vector liftings. We also show that for any diffuse probability space
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
, and
0\leq p<\infty
, there is no selector for the measure classes
\varphi\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
which is order-preserving (Proposition
4.6
) generalizing the result from
[
18
]
that there is no linear lifting for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
when
1\leq p<\infty
.

We investigate vector liftings for product spaces. In particular we prove Theorem
5.15
which implies the following.
If
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
and
(X\times{Y},\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
are topological probability spaces, where
\upsilon
extends the product measure
\mu\otimes\nu
and satisfies Fubini’s theorem with respect to the factors (in either order), and if each of
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
has a strong vector lifting, then for every pair of strong vector liftings
\gamma
for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
\eta
for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
there exists a strong vector lifting
\pi
for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
so that
\pi(f\otimes g)=\gamma(f)\eta(g)
for all
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
,
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
, and the vertical and horizontal sections of
\pi(f)
all belong to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
and
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
, respectively.
As part of the proof, we need to show that for any separately continuous function
h\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
, if
h
is equal almost everywhere to a sum
\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\otimes g_{i}
where
f_{i}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
,
g_{i}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
, then the functions
f_{i}
and
g_{i}
in this representation can be taken to be continuous (see Corollary
5.6
).

We also examine the notion of marginals on
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
,
p\in[0,\infty]
, see Definition
6.1
. We give some conditions equivalent to the existence of
2
-marginals in an
\mathcal{L}^{p}
setting (Corollary
6.24
). For the existence of vector marginals we prove in particular the following result (Corollary
6.35
).
Given two complete
\tau
-additive topological probability spaces
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
and their
\tau
-additive product
(X\times{Y},\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
, and two strong vector liftings
\lambda
and
\eta
on
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
, respectively, there exists a product strong vector lifting
\varphi
on
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}})
, where
\mathfrak{N}
is the
\sigma
-ideal of all right nil null subsets of
X\times{Y}
(see Definition
2.11
) and
\upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}
is defined as in Lemma
3.8
, which is a 2-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}})
.

In Section
7
, we apply the results of Section
6
to the measurable
\mathcal{L}^{p}
modifications of stochastic processes, see Definition
7.4
.
It is seen, that for a primitive lifting
\rho
for
\mathcal{L}^{p}
of the underlying probability space, that the
\rho
-modification of a measurable
\mathcal{L}^{p}
stochastic process is always measurable is equivalent to
\rho
being a marginal (Theorem
7.5
) and, in case of modifications respecting continuous functions to
\rho
being a strong marginal (Corollary
7.7
). The result does not apply to (linear) liftings unless
p=\infty
, since they typically do not exist.

Cohn
[
8
]
, Theorem 3 (see also Chung and Doob
[
7
]
) has given a necessary and sufficient condition for a process to have a measurable modification. The result of Cohn has been extended in
[
5
]
, Theorem 8.8. Hoffmann-Jørgensen
[
17
]
has given another necessary and sufficient condition, which only depends on the 2-dimensional marginal distributions of the process. Musiał
[
24
]
, Proposition 4.1 states another characterization of processes possessing a measurable modification. Theorem
7.11
is inspired by that statement. See Corollary
7.13
for the specialization to probability measures, and Remark
7.16
for how our result relates to the one in
[
24
]
. The proof of Theorem
7.11
involves no applications of liftings. One of the characterizations applies without completeness assumptions on the measures and the others apply with only minimal completeness assumptions. Remark
7.15
indicates how the result could be obtained with a suitable
2
-marginal.

In Section
8
, we investigate the following problem: Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,T,\nu)
be probability spaces endowed with vector liftings
\gamma
and
\delta
, respectively. Can we define a vector lifting on the product space by means of a Fubini type formula

(\gamma\odot\delta)(f)(x,y)=\gamma([\delta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y})(x)\;\mbox{if}\;[\delta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu),\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;(\gamma\odot\delta)(f)(x,y)=0\;\mbox{otherwise}

where
(\delta_{\bullet}(f))(x,y):=\delta(f_{x})(y)
if
f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
and
(\delta_{\bullet}(f))(x,y):=0
otherwise, for all
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu\widehat{\otimes}\nu)
and
(x,y)\in{X}\times{Y}
? We single out classes of marginals vector liftings or linear liftings
\gamma
and
\delta
which admit a positive solution, see Proposition
8.20
and Theorem
8.23
, respectively. Corresponding results for densities and category densities are given in
[
27
]
and
[
1
]
, respectively.

2.
Preliminaries

{\mathbb{N}}
,
{\mathbb{R}}
, and
{\mathbb{C}}
stand for the natural, real, and complex numbers, respectively,
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}:={{\mathbb{R}}}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}
. We write
\mathbb{K}
to mean
{\mathbb{R}}
or
{\mathbb{C}}
, except in sections 3 and 4, where
\mathbb{K}
is by default an arbitrary field. For a vector space
X
over
\mathbb{K}
, by a basis for
X
we mean a basis in the indexed sense, i.e., a family
\langle x_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}
of elements of
X
such that for each
x\in X
there is a unique indexed family
\langle a_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}
of elements of
\mathbb{K}
having finite support (
a_{i}=0
except for finitely many
i
) such that
x=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}x_{i}
. When it is not important to name the index set, we will also write
\langle x_{i}\rangle
,
\langle a_{i}\rangle
for these indexed families.

By
\mathcal{P}(X)
we denote the set of all subsets of a set
X
ordered by inclusion and we write
M^{c}:=X\setminus{M}
for
M\in\mathcal{P}(X)
.
Y^{X}
is the space of all functions from
X
into
Y
.
For
f,g:X\to\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
we abbreviate
\{x\in X:f(x)<g(x)\}
as
\{f<g\}
, and similarly for other equality or inequality relations.

Let
X
be a set. If
\mathcal{I}
is an ideal of an algebra
\mathcal{A}
on
X
, we write
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}:=\{E\subseteq{X}:\exists A\in\mathcal{I},E\subseteq{A}\}
for the completion of
\mathcal{I}
. For
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)
,
\sigma(\mathcal{C})
denotes the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathcal{C}
.

For a given probability space
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
we denote by
(\varSigma,\mu)_{0}
, or by
\varSigma_{0}
for simplicity, the
\sigma
-ideal
\{E\in\varSigma:\mu(E)=0\}
. If
A\in\varSigma
we write
\varSigma\cap A
for the
\varSigma
-algebra of all
E\cap A
with
E\in\varSigma
. We denote by
(\mathfrak{A}_{\mu},\bar{\mu})
the
measure algebra of
a measure space
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
, where
\mathfrak{A}_{\mu}=\varSigma/\varSigma_{0}
is the space of all equivalence classes
A^{\bullet}
for
A\in\varSigma
modulo the
\sigma
-ideal
\varSigma_{0}
and
\bar{\mu}(A^{\bullet}):=\mu(A)
for
A\in\varSigma
.
The completion of
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
will be written
(X,\widehat{\varSigma},\widehat{\mu})
.
For topological spaces
(X,\mathfrak{T})
we write
\mathfrak{B}(X)

for its Borel
\sigma
-field over
X
. We write
C(X,{\mathbb{K}})
for the family of all continuous
{\mathbb{K}}
-valued functions on
X
. We usually write simply
C(X)
, letting the context determine
{\mathbb{K}}
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be a probability space and
{\mathfrak{S}}
a topology on
X
with
{\mathfrak{S}}\subseteq\varSigma
. We say
(X,{\mathfrak{S}},\varSigma,\mu)
is a
topological probability space
.
This space is
\tau
-additive
if for each upwards directed
\mathfrak{U}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}
,
\mu(\bigcup\mathfrak{U})=\sup\{\mu(U):U\in\mathfrak{U}\}
, and it is

Radon
if
\mathfrak{S}
is Hausdorff and
\mu
is a complete measure which is inner regular with respect to the compact sets (and therefore
\tau
-additive).

For
\tau
-additive topological probability spaces
(X,{\mathfrak{S}},\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
, their
\tau
-additive product
is the unique topological probability space
(X\times Y,\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
such that
\upsilon
is
\tau
-additive, extends the product measure, and is the completion of its restriction to

\sigma((\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T})\cup(\varSigma\otimes T))
.

For contexts where we wish to adjoint a family
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)
to the null ideal of a measure on
X
, a suitable type of family in our setting is an ideal
\mathcal{I}
of an algebra larger than the algebra of measurable sets. We introduce the following definition,

Definition 2.1
.

We write
\mathbf{T}
for the class of all triples
(X,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{I})
where

(i)

\mathcal{A}
is an algebra of subsets of
X
.

(ii)

\mathcal{I}
is an ideal of an algebra
\mathcal{B}
of subsets of
X
such that
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
.

We write
\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
for the class of all triples
(X,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{I})
in
\mathbf{T}
for which
\mathcal{I}
is closed under countable unions and
\mathcal{A}
is a
\sigma
-algebra.

Remark 2.2
.

(a) In
[
2
]
, we characterized the families
\mathcal{I}
which are ideals of some algebra of subsets of
X
, and identified the largest algebra
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
of which
\mathcal{I}
is an ideal as follows (
[
2
]
, Proposition 2.2):

\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)
is an ideal of some algebra of subsets of
X
if and only if
\emptyset\in\mathcal{I}
and for
A,B\in\mathcal{I}
we have
A\cup B\in\mathcal{I}
and
A\setminus B\in\mathcal{I}
; when this holds, there is a largest algebra
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
of which
\mathcal{I}
is an ideal given by
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}:=\{E\subseteq X:\forall A\in\mathcal{I}\,(E\cap A\in\mathcal{I})\}
.

(b) We can similarly characterize what it means for
\mathcal{I}
to be a
\sigma
-ideal of some
\sigma
-algebra of subsets of
X
: it means that
\mathcal{I}
is an ideal of some algebra of subsets of
X
, and is closed under countable unions. Note that if
\mathcal{I}
is closed under countable unions then so is
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
, as is clear from its definition.
Hence if
\mathcal{I}
is a
\sigma
-ideal of an algebra
\mathcal{B}
then
\mathcal{I}
is also a
\sigma
-ideal of a
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
(namely
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
). Note that in this case,
\mathcal{I}
is closed under countable intersections since
A_{n}\in\mathcal{I}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, implies
A:=\bigcap_{n}A_{n}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
A\subseteq A_{1}
, so
A\in\mathcal{I}
.

(c) In terms of these notions, (ii) could be restated equivalently by saying that
\mathcal{I}
is an ideal of some algebra of subsets of
X
, and
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
.

Example 2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an algebra of subsets of
X
.

(a) When
\mathcal{I}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{P}(X)
then
(X,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}
.

(b) When
\mathcal{I}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{A}
, then
(X,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}
.

(c) When
(X,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is a subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}
, then
(X,\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}
.

(d) When
(X,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}
,
\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{A}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{A}
, and
(X,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{A})\in\mathbf{T}
.

These examples have obvious analogs with
\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
in the place of
\mathbf{T}
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}
. For sets
A,B\in\mathcal{A}
, write
A\subseteq_{\mathcal{I}}B
if
A\setminus B\in\mathcal{I}
,
A=_{\mathcal{I}}B
if
A\,\triangle\,B\in\mathcal{I}
. For
\mathcal{A}
-measurable functions
f,g\colon X\to\ \overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
, write
f\leq_{\mathcal{I}}g
if
\{f>g\}\in\mathcal{I}
, and
f=_{\mathcal{I}}g
if
\{f\not=g\}\in\mathcal{I}
.

When
(X,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{I})
is
(X,\varSigma,\varSigma_{0})
for a measure space
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
, we write the subscript as
\mu
instead of
\varSigma_{0}
, i.e.,
A\subseteq_{\mu}B
,
f=_{\mu}g
, and so on.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}
. We make the following definitions.

(i)

\mathcal{L}^{0}({\mathbb{K}},\varSigma):=\{f\in{\mathbb{K}}^{X}:f\ \text{is $\varSigma$-measurable}\}
.

(ii)

\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({\mathbb{K}},\varSigma,\mathcal{I}):=\{f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}({\mathbb{K}},\varSigma):\|f\|_{\infty}<\infty\}
, where
\|f\|_{\infty}:=\inf\{M\geq{0}:|f|\leq_{\mathcal{I}}M\}
.

(iii)

\mathcal{N}({\mathbb{K}},\varSigma,\mathcal{I}):=\{f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}({\mathbb{K}},\varSigma):f=_{\mathcal{I}}0\}
.

In (b) and (c), we get equivalent definitions if we replace
\mathcal{I}
by the ideal
\mathcal{I}\cap\varSigma
of
\varSigma
. In (b), by standard arguments we have the formulas
\|af\|_{\infty}=|a|\|f\|_{\infty}
,
\|f+g\|_{\infty}\leq\|f\|_{\infty}+\|g\|_{\infty}
,
\|fg\|_{\infty}\leq\|f\|_{\infty}\|g\|_{\infty}
, and thus
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({\mathbb{K}},\varSigma,\mathcal{I})
is a subalgebra of
\mathcal{L}^{0}({\mathbb{K}},\varSigma)
.

We call
\mathcal{N}({\mathbb{K}},\varSigma,\mathcal{I})
the
null ideal
for both
\mathcal{L}^{0}({\mathbb{K}},\varSigma)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({\mathbb{K}},\varSigma,\mathcal{I})
. Note that a function
f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}({\mathbb{K}},\varSigma)
satisfying
f=_{\mathcal{I}}0
belongs to
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({\mathbb{K}},\varSigma,\mathcal{I})
.
When
\mathbb{K}
is clear from the context, we omit it. So
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\varSigma)
means
\mathcal{L}^{0}({\mathbb{K}},\varSigma)
, and similarly for
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\varSigma,\mathcal{I})
,
\mathcal{N}(\varSigma,\mathcal{I})
.

For a measure space
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
, with
\mathcal{I}=\varSigma_{0}
the sets defined above are the standard notions of
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
when
p=0,\infty
. For the case
0<p<\infty
, we make the standard definition

(iv)

\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mathbb{K},\mu)=\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu):=\{f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu):\int|f|^{p}\,d\mu<\infty\}
.

The null ideal for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
for all
p\in[0,\infty]
is
\mathcal{N}(\mu):=\{f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu):f=_{\mu}0\}
.
If
r_{p}\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)\to{L}^{p}(\mu)
is the quotient map, then when the value of
p
is clear from the context, we write
f^{\bullet}
for
r_{p}(f)=f+\mathcal{N}(\mu)\in L^{p}(\mu)
, and for
V\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
we write
V^{\bullet}=r_{p}[V]=\{f^{\bullet}:f\in V\}
.

Definition 2.6
.

For a given set
X
,
{\mathcal{D}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}(X)
, and
\xi\in{\mathcal{P}}(X)^{\mathcal{D}}
we consider the following properties holding for all
A,B\in\mathcal{D}
.

(N)

\emptyset\in{\mathcal{D}}
and
\xi(\emptyset)=\emptyset
,

(E)

X\in{\mathcal{D}}
and
\xi(X)=X
,

(M)

A\subseteq B
implies
\xi(A)\subseteq\xi(B)
,

(V)

A^{c}\in\mathcal{D}
and
\xi(A)\cap\xi(A^{c})=\emptyset
,

(
\vartheta
)

A\cap B\in{\mathcal{D}}
and
\xi(A\cap B)=\xi(A)\cap\xi(B)
.

(C)

A^{c}\in\mathcal{D}
and
\xi(A^{c})=[\xi(A)]^{c}
.

(\Lambda)

\xi
satisfies
(\vartheta)
and
(C)
.

If
X
is a topological space, we say
\xi
is
strong
, if

U\subseteq\xi(U)
for every open set
U
belonging to
\mathcal{D}
.
For
(X,{\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{I}})\in\mathbf{T}
we consider for
\xi:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{P}(X)
the following properties holding for all
A,B\in\mathcal{D}
.

(
I{1}
)

\xi(A)\in\mathcal{D}
and
\xi(A)=_{\mathcal{I}}A
,

(
I2
)

A=_{\mathcal{I}}B
implies
\xi(A)=\xi(B)
,

When
(X,\mathcal{D},\mathcal{I})=(X,\varSigma,\varSigma_{0})
for a measure space
(X,\Sigma,\mu)
, write
(L1)
,
(L2)
instead of
(I1)
,
(I2)
. Also, write
\Lambda(\mu)
(resp.
\vartheta(\mu)
) for the set of all
\xi\in{\mathcal{P}}(X)^{\varSigma}
satisfying the conditions
(N)
,
(E)
, and
(\Lambda)
(resp.
(N)
,
(E)
, and
(\vartheta)
).

Analogously to the properties
(I1)
and
(I2)
for set maps, for maps on functions we define the following properties.

Definition 2.7
.

(a) Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be a measure space,
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu)
a linear subspace containing the constant functions.
We consider for
\xi\colon\mathcal{M}\to{\mathbb{K}}^{X}
the following conditions holding for all
f,g\in\mathcal{M}
.

(
l1
)

\xi(f)\in\mathcal{M}
and
\xi(f)=_{\mu}f
.

(
l2
)

f=_{\mu}g
implies
\xi(f)=\xi(g)
.

We say
\xi
is a
primitive lifting
for
\mathcal{M}
if
\xi
fixes the constant functions and satisfies
(l1)
and
(l2)
. We say
\xi
is a
vector lifting
if
\xi
is a linear primitive lifting.
We denote by
P(\mu,\mathcal{M})
, or just
P(\mu)
, the set of all primitive liftings for
\mathcal{M}
, and by
V(\mu,\mathcal{M})
, or just
V(\mu)
, the set of all vector liftings (when
\mathcal{M}
is a subspace).
When
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
, we write
P^{p}(\mu)
instead of
P(\mu,\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu))
and we write
V^{p}(\mu)
instead of
V(\mu,\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu))
.
If it is necessary to specify
{\mathbb{K}}
, we write
P^{p}_{\mathbb{K}}(\mu)
,
V^{p}_{\mathbb{K}}(\mu)
.
If
X
is also a topological space, we say
\xi
is
strong
if
\xi(f)=f
for each
f\in C(X)\cap\mathcal{M}
.

(b)
When
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{R}}
, a vector lifting which is order-preserving (
f\leq g
implies
\xi(f)\leq\xi(g)
) is called a
linear lifting
for
\mathcal{M}
.
If
\mathcal{M}
is closed under multiplication, a vector lifting which is multiplicative (
\xi(fg)=\xi(f)\xi(g)
) is called a
lifting
for
\mathcal{M}
.
Denote by
\mathfrak{G}(\mu)
and
\Lambda^{\infty}(\mu)
the family of all linear liftings for
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mu)
and of all liftings for
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mu)
, respectively. It is well-known and easy to check that
\Lambda^{\infty}(\mu)\subseteq\mathfrak{G}(\mu)
.

There is a bijective correspondence between liftings
\rho\in\Lambda(\mu)
and liftings
\rho^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\mu)

determined by the relation
\rho^{\prime}(\chi_{A})=\chi_{\rho(A)}
for all
A\in\varSigma
(see
[
18
]
, pages 35–36). We generally denote them both by the same symbol, writing for example
\rho(\chi_{A})=\chi_{\rho(A)}
.

Definition 2.8
.

(Cf.
[
4
]
, page 6)
Let
X
and
Y
be sets. For
E\subseteq X\times Y
, write
E_{x}:=\{y\in Y:(x,y)\in E\}
and
E^{y}:=\{x\in X:(x,y)\in E\}
(
x\in X
,
y\in Y
).

(a) When discussing families of subsets of
X\times Y
constructed from families
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)
and
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Y)
, by
\mathcal{S}\,\dot{\times}\,Y
we will mean
\{S\times Y:S\in\mathcal{S}\}
, and similarly
X\,\dot{\times}\,\mathcal{T}:=\{X\times T:T\in\mathcal{T}\}
.

(b) The downward closures of

\mathcal{S}\,\dot{\times}\,Y
and
X\,\dot{\times}\,\mathcal{T}
in
\mathcal{P}(X\times Y)
will be denoted

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y

\displaystyle:=\{F\subseteq X\times Y:F\subseteq S\times Y\ \text{for some}\ S\in\mathcal{S}\},

\displaystyle X\,\widehat{\times}\,\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle:=\{F\subseteq X\times Y:F\subseteq X\times T\ \text{for some}\ T\in\mathcal{T}\}.

These are ideals when
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{T}
are ideals.

(c) For
\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{T}_{x})_{x\in X}
a collection of families
\mathcal{T}_{x}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Y)
indexed by
X
, define
the
skew product

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\ltimes\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle:=\{E\subseteq X\times Y:\exists N_{E}\in\mathcal{S}\;\forall x\notin N_{E}\;(E_{x}\in\mathcal{T}_{x})\}.

If the indexed family is constant,
\mathcal{T}_{x}=\mathcal{U}
for all
x\in X
, we write the skew product as:
1
1

1

In this general set-theoretic setting, the notation
\mathcal{S}\ltimes\mathcal{U}
would be ambiguous if
\mathcal{U}
were both a family of subsets of
Y
and a collection indexed by
X
of families of subsets of
Y
. In our intended uses the context always makes the intended meaning clear.

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\ltimes\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle:=\{E\subseteq X\times Y:\exists N_{E}\in\mathcal{S}\;\forall x\notin N_{E}\;(E_{x}\in\mathcal{U})\}.

In this setting, interchanging the roles of the two coordinates, we also define

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\rtimes\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle:=\{E\subseteq X\times Y:\exists M_{E}\in\mathcal{U}\;\forall y\notin M_{E}\;(E^{y}\in\mathcal{S})\}.

The following simple observation is useful. We leave its straightforward verification to the reader.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)
be nonempty,

\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{T}_{x})_{x\in X}
, where
\mathcal{T}_{x}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Y)
for each
x\in X
.

(i)

If
\mathcal{S}
is upwards directed and each
\mathcal{T}_{x}
is an ideal of a subalgebra
\mathcal{B}_{x}
of
\mathcal{P}(Y)
then
\mathcal{S}\ltimes\mathcal{T}
is an ideal of the subalgebra
\mathcal{S}\ltimes\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{P}(X\times Y)
, where
\mathcal{B}=(\mathcal{B}_{x})_{x\in X}
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{S}
is upwards
\sigma
-directed and each
\mathcal{T}_{x}
is a
\sigma
-ideal of a
\sigma
-subalgebra
\mathcal{B}_{x}
of
\mathcal{P}(Y)
then
\mathcal{S}\ltimes\mathcal{T}
is a
\sigma
-ideal of the
\sigma
-subalgebra
\mathcal{S}\ltimes\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{P}(X\times Y)
, where
\mathcal{B}=(\mathcal{B}_{x})_{x\in X}
.

(
\mathcal{S}
is upwards (
\sigma
-)directed if every finite (countable) subset of
\mathcal{S}
has an upper bound in
(\mathcal{S},\subseteq)
.)

We make the following standard definition.

Definition 2.10
.

Given probability spaces
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
and
(X,\varSigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
, we say that
(X,\varSigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
is an
extension of
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
by null sets
, or just that
\mu^{\prime}
is an
extension of
\mu
by null sets
, if
\varSigma\subseteq\varSigma^{\prime}
,
\mu^{\prime}\upharpoonright\varSigma=\mu
, and for all
E^{\prime}\in\varSigma^{\prime}
there exists an
E\in\varSigma
such that
\mu^{\prime}(E^{\prime}\,\triangle\,E)=0
.
Equivalently,
\mu^{\prime}
is an extension of
\mu
by null sets if and only if
\mu^{\prime}
extends
\mu
and
\varSigma^{\prime}=\sigma(\varSigma\cup\varSigma^{\prime}_{0})
.

The equivalence in the definition is standard. It is also
Corollary
3.5
.

Definition 2.11
.

(Cf.
[
4
]
, Definition 2.8)
Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
be probability spaces. Let
T:=(T_{x})_{x\in X}
,
T_{0}:=(T_{x,0})_{x\in X}
(
T_{x,0}=
the null ideal of
\nu_{x}
), and
\nu:=(\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
,
\widehat{\nu}:=(\widehat{\nu}_{x})_{x\in X}
.

(a)
\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\nu):=\varSigma_{0}\ltimes T_{0}
is the family of
right nil null sets
. It is a
\sigma
-ideal of
\varSigma_{0}\ltimes T
.

When all
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})
are equal to a single space
(Y,T,\nu)
(
T_{0}
now denoting the null ideal of
\nu
), the roles of the two coordinates can be interchanged, giving the
\sigma
-ideals
\overline{\mathfrak{N}}(\mu,\nu):=\varSigma_{0}\rtimes T_{0}
(
left nil null sets
) and

\mathfrak{N}_{2}(\mu,\nu):=\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\nu)\cap\overline{\mathfrak{N}}(\mu,\nu)
(
(two-sided) nil null sets
) of the
\sigma
-algebras
\varSigma\rtimes T_{0}
,
\varSigma_{0}\ltimes T\cap\varSigma\rtimes T_{0}
, respectively.

(b) The families
\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\widehat{\nu})
,
\overline{\mathfrak{N}}(\widehat{\mu},\nu)
,
\mathfrak{N}_{2}(\widehat{\mu},\widehat{\nu})
we denote simply by
\mathfrak{N}
,
\overline{\mathfrak{N}}
,
\mathfrak{N}_{2}
, respectively. They are
\sigma
-ideals of
\mathcal{P}(X\times Y)
, the latter two being defined only when all spaces
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})
are equal to a single space
(Y,T,\nu)
.

(c)

\mu\ltimes T
is the family of all
E\subseteq{X\times{Y}}
for which there exists an
N\in\varSigma_{0}
with
E_{x}\in T_{x}
when
x\in N^{c}
, and
x\mapsto\nu_{x}(E_{x})
is
\varSigma
-measurable on
N^{c}
. It is a Dynkin class (
[
14
]
, 136A, 452A) on which we define
(\mu\ltimes\nu)(E):=\int_{N^{c}}\nu(E_{x})\,d\mu(x)
for every
E\in\mu\ltimes T
.

In the case of a single space
(Y,T,\nu)
, we can interchange the roles of the two coordinates and we get similarly a Dynkin class
\varSigma\rtimes\nu
on which we define
(\mu\rtimes\nu)(E)
in the obvious way.

(d) For a
\sigma
-ideal
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
,
\mu\ltimes_{\mathcal{I}}T
has the same definition as
\mu\ltimes T
, but with
N\in\varSigma_{0}
replaced by
N\in\mathcal{I}
.

Definition 2.12
.

Given a triple
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
of probability spaces,
we define the following conditions, where
(X\times Y,\varSigma\otimes T,\mu\otimes\nu)
is the ordinary product space,
\varSigma\otimes T
being the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the rectangles
A\times B
(
A\in\varSigma
,
B\in T
), and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
is a
\sigma
-ideal.

{[}P_{0}{]}
:

\varSigma\otimes{T}\subseteq\Upsilon

{[}P_{1}{]}
:

[P_{0}]
and
\upsilon\upharpoonright\varSigma\otimes{T}=\mu\otimes\nu
, i.e.,
\upsilon
extends the product measure
\mu\otimes\nu

{[}P_{2}{]}
:

[P_{1}]
and
\forall E^{\prime}\in\Upsilon

\exists E\in\varSigma\otimes T
(
\upsilon(E^{\prime}\,\triangle\,E)=0
), i.e.,
\upsilon
extends
\mu\otimes\nu
by null sets.

{[}C{]}
:

\Upsilon\subseteq\mu\ltimes T
and
\upsilon=\mu\ltimes\nu\upharpoonright\Upsilon

{[}C{]_{\mathcal{I}}}
:

\Upsilon\subseteq\mu\ltimes_{\mathcal{I}}T
and
\upsilon=\mu\ltimes\nu\upharpoonright\Upsilon

{[}\overline{C}{]}
:

\Upsilon\subseteq\Sigma\rtimes{\nu}
and
\upsilon=\mu\rtimes\nu\upharpoonright\Upsilon

The properties
[C]
and
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
we define also if
(Y,T,\nu)
is replaced by a family
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
, using the same definitions as above, taking
T:=(T_{x})_{x\in X}
,
\nu:=(\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
.
[C]
is the same as
[C]_{\varSigma_{0}}
.

Remark 2.13
.

(a) Note that
[P_{0}]+[C]
implies
[P_{1}]
.

(b)
[P_{0}]
,
[P_{1}]
,
[P_{2}]
,
[C]
,
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
,
[\overline{C}]
are properties of the triple
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
, but when the factors are clear from the context, we will sometimes say that they are properties of
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
or just
\upsilon
. Similarly for
[C]
and
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
when
(Y,T,\nu)
is replaced by a family
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
.

(c) If we regard
\nu_{x}
as a measure on
X\times Y
supported by the fiber
\{x\}\times Y
of the projection map
\pi\colon X\times Y\to X
, then in the language of
[
14
]
, 452E, property
[C]
in the case of a family
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
says that
\nu=(\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
is a
disintegration
of
\upsilon
over
X
which is strongly consistent with
\pi
.
2
2

2

Note that in
[
14
]
, functions being integrated are only assumed to be defined on a co-negligible set. See
[
11
]
, 122K.

The following proposition states that it is enough to verify
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
on the sets in a
\pi
-system generating
\Upsilon
. Cf.
[
14
]
, 452A (a).

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
, and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces. Let
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\Upsilon
be a
\pi
-system with
\sigma(\mathcal{W})=\Upsilon
. Let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
be a
\sigma
-ideal and assume that
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
holds for sets
E\in\mathcal{W}
, i.e., for each
E\in\mathcal{W}
, there is an
I\in\mathcal{I}
such that for all
x\in I^{c}
,
E_{x}\in T_{x}
,
x\mapsto\nu_{x}(E_{x})
is
\varSigma
-measurable on
I^{c}
, and
\upsilon(E)=\int_{I^{c}}\nu_{x}(E_{x})\,d\mu(x)
. Then
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
holds.

Proof.

The sets
E
satisfying
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
form a Dynkin system.
∎

Example 2.15
.

Suppose
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
,
(X\times Y,\varSigma\otimes T,\upsilon)
are probability spaces so that
(\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
is a
product regular conditional probability
(
product r.c.p.
for short) on
T
for
\upsilon
with respect to
\mu
(see
[
34
]
, Definition 1.1), i.e., the following two conditions are satisfied.

(D1)

for every
B\in T
, the map
x\mapsto\nu_{x}(B)
is
\varSigma
-measurable;

(D2)

\upsilon(A\times{B})=\int_{A}\nu_{x}(B)\,d\mu(x)
for
A\in\varSigma
and
B\in T
.

(Note that by (D2),
\upsilon(A\times{Y})=\mu(A)
for every
A\in\varSigma
.)
Then
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
,
(X\times Y,\varSigma\otimes T,\upsilon)
satisfies
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
with
\mathcal{I}=\{\emptyset\}
.

Proof.

By (D1) and (D2), we get that
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
holds for the rectangles
A\times B
,
A\in\varSigma
,
B\in T
. Since these form a
\pi
-system generating
\varSigma\otimes T
,
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
holds by Proposition
2.14
.
∎

The following two propositions use a standard machine for converting Fubini type properties for sets into similar properties for functions. (Cf.
[
14
]
, 452A, 452B, 452F.) We will frequently require Proposition
2.19
(iv) and (iv)
⟂
and will use them without reference,
3
3

3

Many of our results on products
X\times Y
have another version obtained by interchanging the role of the two factors. Borrowing a notational device from
[
25
]
, we sometimes denote this other version by putting the symbol
⟂
after the number of the theorem.

saying only that they hold “by
[C]
” and “by
[\overline{C}]
,” respectively (the latter applying only in the case of a single space
(Y,T,\nu)
).

Proposition 2.16
.

Let
X
be a set equipped with
\mathcal{I}
, an upward
\sigma
-directed family of subsets of
X
. Let
(Y,T_{x})_{x\in X}
, and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon)
be measurable spaces such that
\Upsilon\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
, where
T=(T_{x})_{x\in X}
. Let
h\colon X\times Y\to Z
be a
\Upsilon
-measurable function, where
Z
is a separable metrizable space.

(i)

There is an
I\in\mathcal{I}
such that
h_{x}
is
T_{x}
-measurable when
x\in I^{c}
.

(ii)

When
Z=\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
or
{\mathbb{C}}
, if
J=(J_{x})_{x\in X}
,
J_{x}
a
\sigma
-ideal of
T_{x}
, and
\mathcal{K}
is a
\sigma
-ideal of
\Upsilon
satisfying
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes J
, we can also require

\|h_{x}\|_{\infty}\leq\|h\|_{\infty}
for
x\in I^{c}
, where the first norm is computed with respect to
J_{x}
, and the second with respect to
\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

(i) A
\Upsilon
-measurable simple function
h
has this property since there is an
I\in\mathcal{I}
such that
x\in I^{c}
implies
[h^{-1}(F)]_{x}\in T_{x}
for each of the (finitely many) preimage sets
h^{-1}(F)
. Since every
\Upsilon
-measurable function
h
is a pointwise limit
h=\lim_{n\to\infty}h_{n}
of
\Upsilon
-measurable simple functions
h_{n}
,
4
4

4

Let
d
be a totally bounded metric for
Z
and let
\mathcal{B}_{n}
be a partition of
Z
into Borel sets of diameter at most
1/n
. For
E\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
, choose any point
x_{E}\in E
and define
h_{n}(p)=x_{E}
if and only if
p\in h^{-1}(E)
. Then the
h_{n}
are
\Upsilon
-measurable simple functions and
h=\lim_{n\to\infty}h_{n}
.

with say
[h_{n}]_{x}

T_{x}
-measurable for
x\in I_{n}^{c}
,
I_{n}\in\mathcal{I}
, we get that for
x\in I^{c}
, where
I\in\mathcal{I}
contains
\bigcup_{n}I_{n}
,
h_{x}=\lim_{n\to\infty}[h_{n}]_{x}
is a pointwise limit of
T_{x}
-measurable functions and hence
T_{x}
-measurable.

(ii) For some
E\in\mathcal{K}
,
|h(x,y)|\leq\|h\|_{\infty}
when
(x,y)\in E^{c}
. By (i) and
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes J
, there is an
I\in\mathcal{I}
such that when
x\in I^{c}
,
h_{x}
is
T_{x}
-measurable and
E_{x}\in J_{x}
and therefore
\|h_{x}\|_{\infty}\leq\|h\|_{\infty}
.
∎

Definition 2.17
.

For
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X\times Y)
, and
T=(T_{x})_{x\in X}
, where
T_{x}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Y)
, let
\mathcal{S}[T]
be defined by

\mathcal{S}[T]:=\mathcal{S}\cap(\{\emptyset\}\ltimes T)=\{E\in\mathcal{S}:E_{x}\in T_{x}\ \text{\rm for all $x\in X$}\}.

Corollary 2.18
.

Let
X
be a set,
(Y,T_{x})
,
x\in X
, and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon)
measurable spaces,
Z
a separable metrizable space,
h\colon X\times Y\to Z
. Let
T=(T_{x})_{x\in X}
.
Then
h
is
\Upsilon[T]
-measurable if and only if
h
is
\Upsilon
-measurable and
h_{x}
is
T_{x}
-measurable for all
x\in X
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Apply Proposition
2.16
(i) taking
\Upsilon
to be
\Upsilon[T]
and taking
\mathcal{I}=\{\emptyset\}
.

(\Leftarrow)
For
E\subseteq Z
Borel, we have
h^{-1}(E)\in\Upsilon
and for all
x\in X
,
[h^{-1}(E)]_{x}=h_{x}^{-1}(E)\in T_{x}
. Thus,
h^{-1}(E)\in\Upsilon[T]
.
∎

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
, and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces. Let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
be a
\sigma
-ideal such that
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
holds. Let
h\colon X\times Y\to\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
or
h\colon X\times Y\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a
\Upsilon
-measurable function. Let
0\leq p\leq\infty
.

(i)

If
h(X\times Y)\subseteq[0,\infty]
, there is an
I\in\mathcal{I}
such that each section
h_{x}
is
T_{x}
-measurable for
x\in I^{c}
,
x\mapsto\int h_{x}\,d\nu
is
\varSigma
-measurable on
I^{c}
, and
\int h\,d\upsilon=\int_{I^{c}}\int h_{x}\,d\nu_{x}\,d\mu(x)
.

(ii)

There are
I\in\mathcal{I}
and
N\in\varSigma_{0}
with
I\subseteq N
such that
h_{x}
is
T_{x}
-measurable when
x\in I^{c}
,
x\mapsto\|h_{x}\|_{\infty}
is
\varSigma
-measurable on
I^{c}
, and
\|h_{x}\|_{\infty}\leq\|h\|_{\infty}
when
x\in N^{c}
.

(iii)

If
h
is
\upsilon
-integrable, then there is an
N\in\varSigma_{0}
such that
h_{x}
is
\nu_{x}
-integrable for
x\in N^{c}
,
x\mapsto\int h_{x}\,d\nu_{x}
is
\mu
-integrable on
N^{c}
, and
\int h\,d\upsilon=\int_{N^{c}}\int h_{x}\,d\nu_{x}\,d\mu(x)
.

(iv)

If
h\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
, then
there is an
N\in\varSigma_{0}
such that
h_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
when
x\in N^{c}
. If
p=0
, we can take
N\in\mathcal{I}
.

(v)

[C]
holds for the spaces
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,\widehat{T}_{x},\widehat{\nu}_{x})_{x\in X}
, and
(X\times Y,\widehat{\Upsilon},\widehat{\upsilon})
.

Remark 2.20
.

From
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
we get
[C]
, so
\Upsilon_{0}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}\ltimes T_{0}
holds. But note than in general we will not have
\Upsilon_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{0}
—consider the usual Borel measure on the unit square with
\mathcal{I}=\{\emptyset\}
.

Proof.

(i) The family
\mathcal{F}
of nonnegative
\Upsilon
-measurable functions
h\colon X\times Y\to\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
such that for some
I\in\mathcal{I}
, the section
h_{x}
is
T_{x}
-measurable for
x\in I^{c}
,
x\mapsto\int h_{x}\,d\nu_{x}
is
\varSigma
-measurable on
I^{c}
, and
\int h\,d\upsilon=\int_{I^{c}}\int h_{x}\,d\nu_{x}\,d\mu(x)
contains the characteristic functions of elements of
\Upsilon
by assumption. Since
\mathcal{F}
is closed under taking linear combinations with nonnegative coefficients and limits of increasing sequences, it contains all nonnegative
\Upsilon
-measurable functions.

(ii) The first part follows by Proposition
2.16
(i) and gives
I\in\mathcal{I}
. For the second part, for each
r\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, let
E_{r}=\{|h|>r\}
. Then for
x\in X
,
[E_{r}]_{x}=\{|h_{x}|>r\}
. By
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
, there is an
I_{r}\in\mathcal{I}
,
I\subseteq I_{r}
, such that for
x\in I_{r}^{c}
,
[E_{r}]_{x}\in T_{x}
and
x\mapsto\nu_{x}[E_{r}]_{x}
is
\varSigma
-measurable on
I_{r}^{c}
. Let
I_{0}=\bigcup_{r}I_{r}\in\mathcal{I}
. On
I_{0}^{c}
, each of the maps
x\mapsto\nu_{x}[E_{r}]_{x}
is
\varSigma
-measurable, and therefore for any
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\{x\in I_{0}^{c}:\|h_{x}\|_{\infty}>c\}=\{x\in I_{0}^{c}:\nu_{x}[E_{r}]_{x}>0
for some
r>c\}\in\varSigma
. Thus,
x\mapsto\|h_{x}\|_{\infty}
is
\varSigma
-measurable on
I_{0}^{c}
. The last part follows by Proposition
2.16
(ii), taking
\mathcal{I}=\varSigma_{0}
,
J=T_{0}:=(T_{x,0})_{x\in X}
,
T_{x,0}
the null ideal of
\nu_{x}
,
\mathcal{K}=\Upsilon_{0}
.

(iii) If
h
is real-valued and
\upsilon
-integrable, then corresponding to the positive and negative parts
h^{+}
,
h^{-}
, since
h^{\pm}\in\mathcal{F}
, we get
I\in\mathcal{I}
, which we can take to be the same for both parts, as in the definition of
\mathcal{F}
. Since
\int h^{\pm}\,d\upsilon=\int_{I^{c}}\int h_{x}^{\pm}\,d\nu_{x}\,d\mu(x)
is finite, by enlarging
I
to a set
N\in\varSigma_{0}
, we get that the integrals
\int h_{x}^{\pm}\,d\nu_{x}
are finite when
x\in N^{c}
. We then get that the sections
h_{x}
are
\nu_{x}
-integrable for
x\in N^{c}
,
x\mapsto\int h_{x}\,d\nu_{x}=\int h_{x}^{+}\,d\nu_{x}-\int h_{x}^{-}\,d\nu_{x}
is
\mu
-integrable on
N^{c}
, and
\int h\,d\upsilon=\int h^{+}\,d\upsilon-\int h^{-}\,d\upsilon=\int_{N^{c}}\int h_{x}^{+}\,d\nu_{x}\,d\mu(x)-\int_{N^{c}}\int h^{-}_{x}\,d\nu_{x}\,d\mu(x)=\int_{N^{c}}\int h_{x}\,d\nu_{x}\,d\mu(x)
. If
h
is complex-valued, apply the real-valued case to the real and imaginary parts.

(iv) The cases
p=0
and
p=\infty
are covered by (ii). The case
0<p<\infty
is covered by (iii) applied to
|h|^{p}
.

(v) First consider
E\in\widehat{\Upsilon}
with
\widehat{\upsilon}(E)=0
. There is an
F\in\Upsilon
such that
E\subseteq F
and
\upsilon(F)=0
. By
\Upsilon_{0}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}\ltimes T_{0}
, there is an
N\in\varSigma_{0}
such that when
x\in N^{c}
,
\nu_{x}(F_{x})=0
and therefore
\widehat{\nu}_{x}(E_{x})=0
.

For an arbitrary
E\in\widehat{\Upsilon}
, there is a
G\in\Upsilon
such that
P:=E\,\triangle\,G
satisfies
\widehat{\upsilon}(P)=0
. By
[C]
and the previous paragraph, there is an
N\in\varSigma_{0}
such that when
x\in N^{c}
,
G_{x}\in T_{x}
,
\widehat{\nu}_{x}(P_{x})=0
, and
x\mapsto\nu_{x}(G_{x})
is
\varSigma
-measurable on
N^{c}

with
\int_{N^{c}}\nu_{x}(G_{x})\,d\mu(x)=\upsilon(G)
.
It follows that when
x\in N^{c}
,
E_{x}=G_{x}\,\triangle\,P_{x}\in\widehat{T}_{x}
,

x\mapsto\widehat{\nu}_{x}(E_{x})=\nu_{x}(G_{x})
is
\varSigma
-measurable on
N^{c}
, and
\int_{N^{c}}\widehat{\nu}_{x}(E_{x})\,d\mu(x)=\int_{N^{c}}\nu_{x}(G_{x})\,d\mu(x)=\upsilon(G)=\widehat{\upsilon}(E)
.
∎

Corollary 2.21
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces. For functions
h\colon X\times Y\to{\mathbb{R}}
, and
N\in\varSigma_{0}
,
M\in T_{0}
, define
h_{N},h_{M}\colon X\times Y\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

\displaystyle\text{$h_{N}(x,y)=h(x,y)$ when $x\in N^{c}$, $h_{N}(x,y)=0$ when $x\in N$},

\displaystyle\text{$h^{M}(x,y)=h(x,y)$ when $y\in M^{c}$, $h^{M}(x,y)=0$ when $y\in M$}.

(i)

Assume
\varSigma_{0}\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
. If
h\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
satisfies that for some
N\in\varSigma_{0}
,
h_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
when
x\notin N
, then
h_{N}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
,
h_{N}=_{\upsilon}h
, for all
x\in X
,
[h_{N}]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
, and for all
y\in Y
,
[h_{N}]^{y}=_{\mu}[h]^{y}
.

(ii)

Assume we have a single space
(Y,T,\nu)
, and that in
(i)
we have moreover that
X\,\dot{\times}\,T_{0}\subseteq\Upsilon
and for some
M\in T_{0}
,
h^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
when
y\notin M
. Then
(h_{N})^{M}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
,
(h_{N})^{M}=_{\upsilon}h
and for all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
,
[(h_{N})^{M}]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
and
[(h_{N})^{M}]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
.

Remark 2.22
.

If we have
[C]
in (i), or
[C]
and
[\overline{C}]
in (ii), these applies in particular to
h\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
by Proposition
2.19
(iv) and (iv)
⟂
, and then the functions
h_{N}
in (i), and
(h_{N})^{M}
in (ii), belong to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
as well since they are
=_{\upsilon}h
.

Proof.

(i) is mostly obvious, we just need to note that we have

[h_{N}]^{y}(x)=[h]^{y}(x)
when
x\in N^{c}
and
[h_{N}]^{y}(x)=0
when
x\in N
, so
[h_{N}]^{y}=_{\mu}[h]^{y}
for all
y\in Y
.

(ii) From the last part of (i) we get that
[h_{N}]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
when
y\notin M
. Then apply (i)
⟂
to
h_{N}
getting

(h_{N})^{M}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
,
(h_{N})^{M}=_{\upsilon}h_{N}=_{\upsilon}h
and for all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
,
[(h_{N})^{M}]_{x}=_{\nu}[h_{N}]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
and
[(h_{N})^{M}]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
.
∎

3.
Extensions by null sets

The proofs of the next two lemmas follow standard ideas and we leave most of them as exercises for the reader (cf.
[
16
]
, I and II, and
[
9
]
, II, Aufgabe 6.2). The proofs are largely a matter of checking that standard arguments go through with the assumption that
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}
, instead of the stronger assumption that
\mathcal{I}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{P}(X)
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}
.

(i)

The algebra generated by
\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I}
is

\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}:=\{A\,\triangle\,I:A\in\varSigma,\,I\in\mathcal{I}\}
.

\mathcal{I}
is an ideal of
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
. If
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
then
\sigma(\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I})=\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
.

(ii)

Suppose
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
.
Let
f\colon X\to\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
be
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
-measurable. Then there is a function
g\colon X\to\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
which is
\varSigma
-measurable and is such that
f=_{\mathcal{I}}g
.
The same statement holds with
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
replaced by any standard Borel space.

Remark 3.2
.

(a) Since
\varSigma
and
\mathcal{I}
are closed under symmetric difference, and the symmetric difference operation is commutative and associative,
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
is simply the closure of
\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I}
under symmetric difference. Example
3.6
shows that it is not true in general for a
\sigma
-algebra
\varSigma
of subsets of
X
and a
\sigma
-ideal
\mathcal{I}
of some algebra on
X
that
\sigma(\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I})=\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
. Nevertheless, if
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is the ideal of
\sigma(\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I})
generated by
\mathcal{I}
, then
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I}^{\prime})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
, so
\sigma(\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I})=\sigma(\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I}^{\prime})=\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\{A\,\triangle\,I:A\in\varSigma,\,I\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\}
.

(b) The assumption
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
is not a necessary condition for the equality
\sigma(\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I})=\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
, i.e., the latter does not imply that
\mathcal{I}
is an ideal of
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
, as shown by Example
3.7
.

Proof.

(i) We begin by checking that
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
is the algebra of subsets of
X
generated by
\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I}
. Clearly an algebra containing
\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I}
must contain
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
as well, and by taking either
I
or
A
to be empty in
A\,\triangle\,I
, we see that
\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
. There remains to check that
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
is closed under complementation and unions. This follows from

(A\,\triangle\,I)^{c}=X\,\triangle\,(A\,\triangle\,I)=(X\,\triangle\,A)\,\triangle\,I=A^{c}\,\triangle\,I

and from the fact that for
A_{1},A_{2}\in\varSigma
,
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathcal{I}
, letting
E:=(A_{1}\,\triangle\,I_{1})\cup(A_{2}\,\triangle\,I_{2})
, we have

I:=E\,\triangle\,(A_{1}\cup A_{2})\subseteq(I_{1}\cup I_{2}).

Since
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
belong to
\varSigma\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
, we get
I\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
, so
I\in\mathcal{I}
since
\mathcal{I}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
. Therefore
E=(A_{1}\cup A_{2})\,\triangle\,I\in\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
.
Since
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
is a subalgebra of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
\mathcal{I}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
, it follows readily that
\mathcal{I}
is an ideal of
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
. We leave for the reader the verification that
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
is a
\sigma
-algebra when
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
.

We sketch a proof for (ii). For
r\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, write
\{f<r\}=A_{r}\,\triangle\,I_{r}
where
A_{r}\in\varSigma
and
I_{r}\in\mathcal{I}
. The function
g
defined by
g(x)=\inf\{r\in{\mathbb{Q}}:x\in A_{r}\}
is
\varSigma
-measurable because
\{g<r\}=\bigcup_{s<r}A_{s}
. We have
f=_{\mathcal{I}}g
because
f(x)=g(x)
when
x\notin\bigcup_{r\in{\mathbb{Q}}}I_{r}
.
Now suppose
f
takes values in a standard Borel space
Y
. Since
Y
is Borel-isomorphic to a Borel subset of
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
(
[
19
]
, Proposition 12.1 and Corollary 15.2), we may assume
Y
is a Borel subset of
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
. Then we get
g
as above.
The set
E:=g^{-1}(\overline{R}\setminus Y)
belongs to
\varSigma
since
g
is
\varSigma
-measurable, and belongs to
\mathcal{I}
since
f=_{\mathcal{I}}g
. By redefining
g
on
E
to be identically equal to a fixed value
y_{0}\in Y
,
5
5

5

If
Y=\emptyset
then
X=\emptyset
since
f\colon X\to Y
, so
g
and
f
are both the empty function.

we arrange that
g
takes values in
Y
.
∎

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
(X,\varSigma)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon)
be measurable spaces,
\mathcal{I}
an upwards
\sigma
-directed family of subsets of
X
. Given
(Y,T_{x},J_{x})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
,
x\in X
, write
J=(J_{x})_{x\in X}
,
T_{J}=((T_{x})_{J_{x}}:x\in X)
. If
\Upsilon\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{J}
then
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\mathcal{I}\ltimes J)\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
.

Proof.

\mathcal{I}\ltimes J
is a
\sigma
-ideal of the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{J}
by (ii) of Proposition
2.9
.
∎

The following example will be useful in section
8
.

Example 3.4
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces so that
[C]
and
[\overline{C}]
hold and
X\,\dot{\times}\,T\subseteq\Upsilon
. Assume that
X
is an atom, i.e.,
\mu
is
\{0,1\}
-valued. Write
\pi_{Y}\colon X\times Y\to Y
for the projection onto
Y
. Then
6
6

6

Part (i) is essentially
[
3
]
, Proposition 5.2 (iii). The statement there assumes
\upsilon
,
\mu
and
\nu
are complete but no completeness is needed. For clarity, we repeat the argument.

(i)

(X\,\dot{\times}\,T)_{\Upsilon_{0}}=\Upsilon

(ii)

For each
f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
, there are
w\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)
and
N_{f}\in T_{0}
such that
f=_{\upsilon}g
, where
g=w\circ\pi_{Y}
, and
y\notin N_{f}
implies
f^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu)
and
f^{y}=_{\mu}w(y)
.

(iii)

For each
E\in\Upsilon
, there are
A\in T
and
N_{E}\in T_{0}
such that
E=_{\upsilon}X\times A
and
y\notin N_{E}
implies
E^{y}\in\varSigma
and
\mu(E^{y})=\chi_{A}(y)
.

Proof.

(i) The inclusion
(X\,\dot{\times}\,T)_{\Upsilon_{0}}\subseteq\Upsilon
is clear. For the reverse inclusion, let
E\in\Upsilon
. By
[\overline{C}]
, there is an
N_{E}\in T_{0}
such that
y\in N_{E}^{c}
implies
E^{y}\in\varSigma
, and the function
y\mapsto\mu(E^{y})
is
T
-measurable on
N_{E}^{c}
. The set
A:=\{y\in N_{E}^{c}:\mu(E^{y})=1\}
belongs to
T
and hence
X\times A\in\Upsilon
since
X\,\dot{\times}\,T\subseteq\Upsilon
. When
y\notin A\cup N_{E}
, we have
\mu(E^{y})=0
, so by
[\overline{C}]
,
E=_{\upsilon}X\times A
. It follows that
E\in(X\,\dot{\times}\,T)_{\Upsilon_{0}}
.

(ii) We have
(X\times Y,X\,\dot{\times}\,T,\Upsilon_{0})\in T_{\sigma}
. Since
f
is
\Upsilon
-measurable and (i) holds, by Lemma
3.1
(ii) there is a
(X\,\dot{\times}\,T)
-measurable function
g\colon X\times Y\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that
f=_{\upsilon}g
.
Using (i) of Proposition
2.16
with
\mathcal{I}=\{\emptyset\}
and
X\,\dot{\times}\,T
in the role of
\Upsilon
, we see that
g_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)
for all
x\in X
. Using (i)
⟂
of the same proposition with
\mathcal{I}=\{\emptyset\}
and
X\,\dot{\times}\,T
in the role of
\Upsilon
again, and taking
\{\emptyset,X\}
in the role of
T
, we see that for all
y\in Y
,
g^{y}
is
\{\emptyset,X\}
-measurable, so
g^{y}
is constant with a value
w(y)
. Then
w\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)
since
g_{x}=w
for all
x\in X
.

(iii) In (ii), take
f=\chi_{E}
. In the proof of (ii), Lemma
3.1
(ii) allows us to take
g
to be
\{0,1\}
-valued since
f
is
\{0,1\}
-valued. Then
w
is
\{0,1\}
-valued, so
w=\chi_{A}
for some
A\in T
and
g(x,y)=w(y)=\chi_{A}(y)=\chi_{X\times A}(x,y)
. The rest of (iii) follows from corresponding parts of (ii).
∎

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
and
(X,\varSigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
be probability spaces. Then
\mu^{\prime}
is an extension of
\mu
by null sets if and only if
\mu^{\prime}
extends
\mu
and
\varSigma^{\prime}=\sigma(\varSigma\cup\varSigma^{\prime}_{0})
.

Proof.

The forward implication is easy to see. For the converse, note that with
\mathcal{I}:=\varSigma^{\prime}_{0}
, we have
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
and use the fact from Lemma
3.1
(i) that
\sigma(\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I})=\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
.
∎

Example 3.6
.

There are a probability space
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
on a finite set
X
, and
\mathcal{I}
, an ideal of some algebra on
X
, such that
\mu
has an extension to a measure
\mu^{\prime}
whose null ideal contains
\mathcal{I}
but
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
is not an algebra of subsets of
X
.

Consider the probability space
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
given by

X=\{1,2,3,4\},\ \varSigma=\{\emptyset,\{1,2\},\{3,4\},X\},\ \mu\{1,2\}=\mu\{3,4\}=1/2.

Let
\mathcal{I}=\{\emptyset,\{1,3\}\}
. The algebra generated by
\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I}
is
\varSigma^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}(X)
, and
\mu
extends to
\mu^{\prime}
on
\varSigma^{\prime}
having
\mathcal{I}
in its null ideal by setting
\mu^{\prime}\{1\}=\mu^{\prime}\{3\}=0
,
\mu^{\prime}\{2\}=\mu^{\prime}\{4\}=1/2
.
However, the number of elements of
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
,
A\,\triangle\,I
with
A\in\varSigma
and
I\in\mathcal{I}
, is at most
|\varSigma|\cdot|\mathcal{I}|=8
,
7
7

7

It is easily checked that the elements
A\,\triangle\,I
consist precisely of the sets of even cardinality.

so
\varSigma^{\prime}\not=\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
.
It follows that
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\notin\mathbf{T}
, which we can also see directly since
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
consists of the four sets disjoint from
\{1,3\}
and their complements, so
\varSigma\not\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
.

Example 3.7
.

There are a probability space
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
on a finite set
X
, and
\mathcal{I}
, an ideal of some algebra on
X
, such that
\mu
has an extension to a measure
\mu^{\prime}
with domain
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
whose null ideal contains
\mathcal{I}
but
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\notin\mathbf{T}
.

We begin with the trivial probability space
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
having
X=\{a,b,c,d,e\}
,
\varSigma=\{\emptyset,X\}
. Define

\mathcal{I}=\{\emptyset,\{a,b,c\},\{a,b\},\{c\}\},\ \ \mathcal{J}=\{\emptyset,\{b,c,d\},\{b\},\{c,d\}\}.

It is readily seen that each of
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
is closed under union and difference, and hence is an ideal of some algebra on
X
. Trivially
\varSigma\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
, so by Lemma
3.1
(i),
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
is the algebra generated by
\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I}
. Its atoms are
\{a,b\},\{c\},\{d,e\}
, and
\mu
extends to a probability measure
\mu^{\prime}
on this algebra having
\mathcal{I}
in its null ideal by setting
\mu^{\prime}\{d,e\}=1
. This space
(X,\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}},\mu^{\prime})
, together with the ideal
\mathcal{J}
, is our example, as we now verify.

The closure of
\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{J}
under symmetric difference is
\mathcal{P}(X)
.
8
8

8

Since disjoint unions are symmetric differences, it is enough to show why the closure
\mathcal{S}
of
\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{J}
under symmetric difference contain the relevant singletons. We have
\{c\},\{b\}\in\mathcal{S}
, and then
\{a,b\}\,\triangle\,\{b\}=\{a\}\in\mathcal{S}
and
\{c,d\}\,\triangle\,\{c\}=\{d\}\in\mathcal{S}
. Then
\{e\}=X\,\triangle\,\{a,b,c,d\}=X\,\triangle\,\{a\}\,\triangle\,\{b\}\,\triangle\,\{c\}\,\triangle\,\{d\}\in\mathcal{S}
.

Hence the algebra
\varSigma^{\prime\prime}
generated by the union
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}\cup\mathcal{J}
, as well as the closure
(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{\mathcal{J}}
of the union under symmetric difference, are both equal to
\mathcal{P}(X)
.
\mu^{\prime\prime}
extends to a measure on
\varSigma^{\prime\prime}
having
\mathcal{J}
in its null ideal by setting
\mu^{\prime\prime}\{e\}=1
. However,
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}\not\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{J}}
since taking
I=\{a,b,c\}\in\mathcal{I}
and
J=\{b,c,d\}\in\mathcal{J}
, we see that
I\cap J=\{b,c\}
which does not belong to
\mathcal{J}
.
Note that
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
are interchanged by the bijection
a\leftrightarrow d
,
b\leftrightarrow c
,
e\leftrightarrow e
, so we could have interchanged the roles of
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}
and let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be a finitely additive measure space
(
with
\mu
valued in
[0,\infty])
such that
\varSigma\cap\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
. We get a well-defined finitely additive measure
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}
on
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
extending
\mu
by setting
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}(A\,\triangle\,I)=\mu A
for
A\in\varSigma
,
I\in\mathcal{I}
.
If
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
and
\mu
is a measure, then
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}
is a measure.

The null ideal
(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{0}
of
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}
has the following properties.

(i)

(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{0}=\{A\,\triangle\,I:A\in\varSigma_{0},\,I\in\mathcal{I}\}
.

(ii)

(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{0}=\mathcal{I}
if and only if
\varSigma\cap\mathcal{I}=\varSigma_{0}
if and only if
\varSigma_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{I}
.

(iii)

(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{0}=\{A\cup I:A\in\varSigma_{0},\,I\in\mathcal{I}\}
if and only if
A\setminus I\in\varSigma
whenever
A\in\varSigma_{0}
,
I\in\mathcal{I}
.

The measure algebras of
\mu
and
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}
are isomorphic
(
by mapping
E^{\bullet}\in\mathfrak{A}_{\mu}
to
E^{\bullet}\in\mathfrak{A}_{\mu_{\mathcal{I}}}
for
E\in\varSigma)
.

Note that
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}
is an extension of
\mu
by null sets since it extends
\mu
,
\mathcal{I}
is contained in its null ideal, and its domain is
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
.
As pointed out earlier, it is possible that
\mathcal{I}
is an ideal of some algebra on
X
and
\mu
can be extended to a measure having
\mathcal{I}
in its null ideal but
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\not\in T
. (See Example
3.7
and cf. also Corollary
3.13
.) We reserve the notation
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}
for the case where
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in T
. We state this for the countably additive setting as a definition.

Definition 3.9
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be a measure space. When
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in T_{\sigma}
and
\varSigma\cap\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
we define a measure
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}
on
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}:=\{A\,\triangle\,I:A\in\varSigma,\,I\in\mathcal{I}\}
by
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}(A\,\triangle\,I)=\mu A
for
A\in\varSigma
,
I\in\mathcal{I}
.

Proof of Lemma
3.8
.

We check that
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}
is a finitely additive measure and leave the rest as an exercise for the reader. If
A\,\triangle\,I=B\,\triangle\,J
(where
A,B\in\varSigma
,
I,J\in\mathcal{I}
) then composing both sides with
B\,\triangle\,I
gives
A\,\triangle\,B=I\,\triangle\,J\in\varSigma\cap\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
, so
\mu(A\,\triangle\,B)=0
and hence
\mu(A)=\mu(A\cap B)=\mu(B)
. Thus
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}
is well-defined. When
A\in\varSigma
we have
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}A=\mu_{\mathcal{I}}(A\,\triangle\,\emptyset)=\mu A
, so
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}
extends
\mu
. To prove additivity of
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}
, suppose
A_{1}\,\triangle\,I_{1}
and
A_{2}\,\triangle\,I_{2}
are disjoint, where
A_{1},A_{2}\in\varSigma
,
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathcal{I}
. Then
A_{1}\setminus I_{1}
and
A_{2}\setminus\,I_{2}
are disjoint and therefore

A_{1}\cap A_{2}\subseteq I_{1}\cup I_{2},

which gives
A_{1}\cap A_{2}\in\varSigma\cap\mathcal{I}
and hence
\mu(A_{1}\cap A_{2})=0
. As in the proof of Lemma
3.1
, for some
I\in\mathcal{I}
we have
E:=\bigcup_{n}A_{n}\,\triangle\,I_{n}=(\bigcup_{n}A_{n})\,\triangle\,I
.
It follows that

\mu_{\mathcal{I}}\Bigl(\bigcup_{n}A_{n}\,\triangle\,I_{n}\Bigr)=\mu_{\mathcal{I}}\Bigl(\Bigl(\bigcup_{n}A_{n}\Bigr)\,\triangle\,I\Bigr)=\mu\Bigl(\bigcup_{n}A_{n}\Bigr)=\sum_{n}\mu(A_{n})=\sum_{n}\mu_{\mathcal{I}}(A_{n}\,\triangle\,I_{n})\qed

Corollary 3.10
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be a measure space and let
\mathcal{I}
be a
\sigma
-ideal of
\mathcal{P}(X)
with
\varSigma\cap\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
. If either
\mu
is complete or
\varSigma_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{I}
then
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}
is complete.

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
(X,\varSigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
be an extension of a probability space
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
by null sets. Then letting
\mathcal{I}=\varSigma^{\prime}_{0}
we have
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
and
(X,\varSigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})=(X,\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}},\mu_{\mathcal{I}})
.

Proof.

By Corollary
3.5
,
\varSigma^{\prime}=\sigma(\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I})=\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
.
∎

Corollary 3.12
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be a measure space,
(X,\varSigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
an extension by null sets. For any
p\in[0,\infty]
,
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu^{\prime})=\{f+g:f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu),\;g\in\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\prime})\}
.

When
p=0
or
p=\infty
, a similar formula holds for any
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
, namely,

\mathcal{L}^{0}(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})=\{f+g:f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\varSigma),\;g\in\mathcal{N}(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{I})\}
and

\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{I})=\{f+g:f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\varSigma,\mathcal{I}),\;g\in\mathcal{N}(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{I})\}
. The proofs are similar.

Proof.

The inclusion from right to left is clear. For the other direction, suppose
h\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu^{\prime})
. Get
f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\varSigma)
from Lemma
3.1
(ii) (taking
\mathcal{I}=\varSigma^{\prime}_{0}
) with
f=_{\mu^{\prime}}h
. When
p=0
, just take
g=h-f
. When
p=\infty
, additionally note that
\|h\|_{\infty}=\|f\|_{\infty}
. When
0<p<\infty
, if
h\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu^{\prime})
then
\int|h|^{p}\,d\mu^{\prime}=\int|f|^{p}\,d\mu^{\prime}=\int|f|^{p}\,d\mu
, so
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
.
∎

For the following corollary, cf.
[
30
]
, Proposition 39 page 325.

Corollary 3.13
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be a measure space.
Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)
. There is a measure space
(X,\varSigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
with
\varSigma\subseteq\varSigma^{\prime}
and
\mu^{\prime}
extending
\mu
such that
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\varSigma^{\prime}_{0}
if and only if
E\subseteq\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}F_{n}
for
E\in\varSigma
and
\{F_{n}:n\in{\mathbb{N}}\}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
implies
\mu(E)=0
.
When this holds, if
\mathcal{I}
is the
\sigma
-ideal of
\mathcal{S}:=\sigma(\varSigma\cup\mathcal{F})
generated by
\mathcal{F}
, then
\mathcal{S}=\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
and
\mu^{\prime}\upharpoonright\mathcal{S}=\mu_{\mathcal{I}}
.

Thus we can take
\mu^{\prime}
to be an extension of
\mu
by null sets, and every such extension extends
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}
.

Proof.

If the extension
\mu^{\prime}
exists then clearly the condition is satisfied. Conversely, if the condition is satisfied then let
\mathcal{K}
be the
\sigma
-ideal of
\mathcal{P}(X)
generated by
\mathcal{F}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{K}
is the collection of all subsets of
X
covered by a sequence of elements of
\mathcal{F}
. The assumption gives
\mathcal{K}\cap\varSigma\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
and therefore the extension
\mu^{\prime}
exists, specifically we can take
\mu^{\prime}=\mu_{\mathcal{K}}
.

For the last sentence, since
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}^{\prime}
, we have
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma^{\prime}_{0}
. We also have
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
since
\mathcal{I}
is a
\sigma
-ideal of
\mathcal{S}
and
\varSigma\subseteq\mathcal{S}
. Using Lemma
3.1
(i) we get
\mathcal{S}=\sigma(\varSigma\cup\mathcal{I})=\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
, and given
A\,\triangle\,I\in\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
, where
A\in\varSigma
,
I\in\mathcal{I}
, we have
\mu^{\prime}(A\,\triangle\,I)=\mu^{\prime}(A)=\mu(A)=\mu_{\mathcal{I}}(A\,\triangle\,I)
.
∎

From the characterization of an ideal-of-some-algebra in Remark
2.2
(a), it is clear that given any collection
\mathcal{C}
of subsets of
X
, there is a smallest ideal-of-some-algebra containing
\mathcal{C}
, namely the intersection of all ideals-of-some-algebra containing
\mathcal{C}
. In particular, given two ideals-of-some-algebra
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
on
X
, there is a smallest ideal-of-some-algebra
\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}
containing
\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{J}
.
Similarly, there is a smallest
\sigma
-ideal-of-some-algebra
\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J}
containing
\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{J}
.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}
each be an ideal of some algebra on
X
. Writing

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{I\cap J:I\in\mathcal{I},\,J\in\mathcal{J}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{I\setminus J:I\in\mathcal{I},\,J\in\mathcal{J}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{3}

\displaystyle=\{J\setminus I:I\in\mathcal{I},\,J\in\mathcal{J}\},

we have that
\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}
consists of the finite unions
\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}E_{i}
of sets
E_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}\cup\mathcal{A}_{3}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be the collection of all finite unions
\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}E_{i}
of sets
E_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}\cup\mathcal{A}_{3}
. Clearly
\mathcal{S}
is contained in any ideal-of-some-algebra which contains
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
since any ideal-of-some-algebra is closed under differences, finite unions, and intersections. Hence
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}
.
\mathcal{S}
contains
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{2}
and
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{3}
(consider sets
I\setminus\emptyset
,
J\setminus\emptyset
). There remains to show that
\mathcal{S}
is an ideal-of-some-algebra. It is clearly closed under finite unions. For closure under differences, first note the following.

Claim 3.15
.

A\setminus B\in\mathcal{S}
when
A,B\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}\cup\mathcal{A}_{3}
.

In what follows,
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathcal{I}
,
J_{1},J_{2}\in\mathcal{J}
, and we write intersections as products. If
A
and
B
both belong to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, say
A=I_{1}J_{1}
and
B=I_{2}J_{2}
, then

A\setminus B=(I_{1}J_{1})\setminus(I_{2}J_{2})=I_{1}J_{1}(I_{2}^{c}\cup J_{2}^{c})=I_{1}I_{2}^{c}J_{1}\cup I_{1}J_{1}J_{2}^{c}\in\mathcal{S}

since it is the union of two elements of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. If
A=I_{1}J_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
,
B=I_{2}\setminus J_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
, then

A\setminus B=(I_{1}J_{1})\setminus(I_{2}\cap J_{2}^{c})=I_{1}J_{1}(I_{2}^{c}\cup J_{2})=I_{1}I_{2}^{c}J_{1}\cup I_{1}J_{1}J_{2}\in\mathcal{S}

since it is again the union of two elements of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. Similarly if
A\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
,
B\in\mathcal{A}_{3}
.

If
A=I_{1}\setminus J_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
,
B=I_{2}J_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
, then

A\setminus B=(I_{1}J_{1}^{c})\setminus(I_{2}J_{2})=I_{1}J_{1}^{c}(I_{2}^{c}\cup J_{2}^{c})=I_{1}I_{2}^{c}J_{1}^{c}\cup I_{1}J_{1}^{c}J_{2}^{c}=I_{1}I_{2}^{c}J_{1}^{c}\cup I_{1}(J_{1}\cup J_{2})^{c}\in\mathcal{S}

since it is the union of two elements of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
. Similarly if
A\in\mathcal{A}_{3}
,
B\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

If
A=I_{1}\setminus J_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
,
B=I_{2}\setminus J_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
, then

(I_{1}J^{c}_{1})\setminus(I_{2}J_{2}^{c})=I_{1}J_{1}^{c}(I_{2}^{c}\cup J_{2})=I_{1}I_{2}^{c}J_{1}^{c}\cup I_{1}J_{1}^{c}J_{2}\in\mathcal{S}

since it is the union of an element of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
and an element of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. Similarly if
A,B\in\mathcal{A}_{3}
.

If
A=I_{1}\setminus J_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
,
B=J_{2}\setminus I_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{3}
, then

(I_{1}J^{c}_{1})\setminus(I_{2}^{c}J_{2})=I_{1}J_{1}^{c}(I_{2}\cup J_{2}^{c})=I_{1}I_{2}J_{1}^{c}\cup I_{1}J_{1}^{c}J_{2}^{c}=I_{1}I_{2}J_{1}^{c}\cup I_{1}(J_{1}\cup J_{2})^{c}\in\mathcal{S}

since it is the union of two elements of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
. Similarly if
A\in\mathcal{A}_{3}
,
B\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

From the claim, we then get that
A\setminus B\in\mathcal{S}
when
A\in\mathcal{S}
,
B\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}\cup\mathcal{A}_{3}
since writing
A=\bigcup_{i}A_{i}
gives
A\setminus B=\bigcup_{i}(A_{i}\setminus B)\in\mathcal{S}
. Then for
A,B\in\mathcal{S}
, writing
B=\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}B_{j}
we have by induction on
m
that
A\setminus B=(A\setminus(\bigcup_{j=1}^{m-1}B_{j}))\setminus B_{m}
belongs to
\mathcal{S}
.
∎

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
X
be a set,
\varSigma
an algebra of subsets of
X
, and let each of
\mathcal{I}
,
\mathcal{J}
be an ideal-of-some-algebra on
X
.

(i)

If
\mathcal{J}
is the
\sigma
-ideal-of-some-algebra generated by
\mathcal{I}
, then
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{J}}
.

(ii)

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{J}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J}}
.

(iii)

If
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})
,
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{J})\in\mathbf{T}
, then
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J})\in\mathbf{T}
.

(iv)

If
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})
,
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{J})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
, then
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
.

(v)

If
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}
and
(X,\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{J})\in\mathbf{T}
then
(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{\mathcal{J}}=\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}}
.

(vi)

If
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
and
(X,\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{J})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
then
(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{\mathcal{J}}=\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J}}

Proof.

For any
E\subseteq X
and any collection
\mathcal{S}
of subsets of
X
, write

\mathcal{C}(E,\mathcal{S})=\{K\subseteq X:E\cap K\in\mathcal{S}\}

and note that
\mathcal{S}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{2}
implies
\mathcal{C}(E,\mathcal{S}_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{C}(E,\mathcal{S}_{2})
.
From the formulas
E\cap\bigcup_{i}K_{i}=\bigcup_{i}E\cap K_{i}
and
E\cap(K_{1}\setminus K_{2})=(E\cap K_{1})\setminus(E\cap K_{2})
, we see that if
\mathcal{S}
is an ideal of some algebra then so is
\mathcal{C}(E,\mathcal{S})
, and if
\mathcal{S}
is a
\sigma
-ideal of some algebra then so is
\mathcal{C}(E,\mathcal{S})
. The property that
E\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
can be written
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(E,\mathcal{I})
.

(i) If
E\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
then
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(E,\mathcal{I})\subseteq\mathcal{C}(E,\mathcal{J})
. Since
\mathcal{C}(E,\mathcal{J})
is a
\sigma
-ideal of some algebra on
X
, we get
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(E,\mathcal{J})
, so
E\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{J}}
.

(ii) Let
E\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{J}}
. Then
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(E,\mathcal{I})
and
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(E,\mathcal{J})
, so
\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(E,\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J})
. Since
\mathcal{C}(E,\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J})
is an ideal of some algebra on
X
, we get
\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(E,\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J})
, so
E\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}}
. The second inclusion follows from (i).

(iii), (iv) From
\varSigma\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
\varSigma\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{J}}
we get
\varSigma\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{J}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J}}
.

(v) By (iii) and Lemma
3.1
(i),
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}}
is the algebra of subsets of
X
generated by
\varSigma\cup(\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J})
. Since
(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{\mathcal{J}}
is an algebra containing
\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{J}
, it is in particular an ideal-of-some-algebra containing
\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{J}
, so it contains
\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}
and hence
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}}\subseteq(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{\mathcal{J}}
. Similarly, since
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}}
is an algebra containing
\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{J}
, it contains
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
and hence also contains
(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{\mathcal{J}}
.

(vi) Same proof as for (v), replacing
\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}
by
\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J}
and saying
\sigma
-algebra and
\sigma
-ideal-of-some-algebra instead of algebra and ideal-of-some-algebra.
∎

With regards to (i), we note that in general the inclusion
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{J}
between two ideals of some algebra on
X
does not imply any inclusion relationship between
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{J}}
.

Example 3.17
.

(a) If
X=\{a,b,c,d\}
,
\mathcal{I}=\{\emptyset,\{a,b\}\}
, and
\mathcal{J}
is the algebra with atoms
\{a\}
,
\{b\}
,
\{c,d\}
(and so in particular is an ideal of some algebra), then
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{J}
but
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{J}}
are not comparable as can be seen by considering the singletons
\{a\}
and
\{c\}
. (b) If
\mathcal{I}
is any ideal of some algebra with
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}\not=\mathcal{P}(X)
and
\mathcal{J}
is its completion, then
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{J}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
is a proper subalgebra of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{J}}=\mathcal{P}(X)
. (c) If
\mathcal{J}
is any ideal of some algebra with
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{J}}\not=\mathcal{P}(X)
and
\mathcal{I}=\{\emptyset\}
, then
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{J}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{J}}
is a proper subalgebra of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}=\mathcal{P}(X)
.

The next example shows that the second inclusion in (ii) can be proper,
and so in particular we can have
\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}\not=\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J}
, even when
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
are
\sigma
-ideals of some algebra. When
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
are complete
\sigma
-ideals, then
\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}=\{A\cup B:A\in\mathcal{I},\,B\in\mathcal{J}\}
is a
\sigma
-ideal. The same fact and the same formula for
\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}
hold if
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}
are
\sigma
-ideals-of-some-algebra with
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(E)
and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(F)
for some disjoint sets
E
and
F
.

Example 3.18
.

Let
X={\mathbb{N}}\times{\mathbb{N}}
,
\mathcal{I}=\{A\times{\mathbb{N}}:A\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}\}
, and
\mathcal{J}=\{{\mathbb{N}}\times B:B\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}\}
. Then
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
are
\sigma
-algebras of subsets of
X
, and hence each is a
\sigma
-ideal-of-some-algebra on
X
. Since
X\in\mathcal{I}
, the ideal-of-some-algebra
\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}
generated by
\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{J}
is also the subalgebra of
\mathcal{P}(X)
generated by
\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{J}
. Each rectangle
A\times B
belongs to this algebra, and the elements of
\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{J}
are all rectangles, so
\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}
is the algebra of all finite unions of rectangles. On the other hand, since
X
is countable and
\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}
contains all singletons,
\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{P}(X)
. For the diagonal
D=\{(n,n):n\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
we have
D\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J}}=\mathcal{P}(X)
but
D\notin\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}}
because
X\in\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}
while
D\cap X=D
does not belong to
\mathcal{I}\vee\mathcal{J}
since it is not a finite union of rectangles.

The following proposition is a version of Proposition 2.3 of
[
4
]
in our present setting.

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be a measure space. Suppose that
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
,
(X,\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{J})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
,
\varSigma\cap\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
and
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}\cap\mathcal{J}\subseteq(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{0}
so that the measures
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}
and
(\mu_{\mathcal{I}})_{\mathcal{J}}
are defined. We have

(i)

The domain of
(\mu_{\mathcal{I}})_{\mathcal{J}}
is

(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{\mathcal{J}}=\{A\,\triangle\,I\,\triangle\,J:A\in\varSigma_{0},\,I\in\mathcal{I},\,J\in\mathcal{J}\}
.

(ii)

(\mu_{\mathcal{I}})_{\mathcal{J}}(A\,\triangle\,I\,\triangle\,J)=\mu(A)
for
A\in\varSigma
,
I\in\mathcal{I}
,
J\in\mathcal{J}
.

(iii)

(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
and
\varSigma\cap(\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J})\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
, so that
\mu_{\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J}}
is defined.

(iv)

(\mu_{\mathcal{I}})_{\mathcal{J}}=\mu_{\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J}}
.

(v)

\varSigma\cap\mathcal{J}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
and
\varSigma_{\mathcal{J}}\cap\mathcal{I}\subseteq(\varSigma_{\mathcal{J}})_{0}
.

(vi)

If
(X,\varSigma_{\mathcal{J}},\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
then
(\mu_{\mathcal{J}})_{\mathcal{I}}
is also defined and

(\mu_{\mathcal{I}})_{\mathcal{J}}=(\mu_{\mathcal{J}})_{\mathcal{I}}
.

Note that
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}\cap\mathcal{J}\subseteq(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{0}
does not follow from the weaker assumption that
\varSigma\cap\mathcal{J}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
as shown by the probability space
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
with
X=\{a,b\}
,
\varSigma=\{\emptyset,X\}
taking
\mathcal{I}=\{\emptyset,\{a\}\}
,
\mathcal{J}=\{\emptyset,\{b\}\}
. The following example shows that the assumption that
(X,\varSigma_{\mathcal{J}},\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}
in (vi) can fail.

Example 3.20
.

There are a probability space
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
on a finite set
X
, and
\mathcal{I}
,
\mathcal{J}
, ideals-of-some-algebra on
X
, such that the hypotheses of Proposition
3.19
hold but they fail if we interchange
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
.
Specifically,
(X,\varSigma_{\mathcal{J}},\mathcal{I})\notin\mathbf{T}
.

Consider the trivial probability space
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
given by
X=\{1,2,3,4\}
,
\varSigma=\{\emptyset,X\}
. Let
\mathcal{I}=\{\emptyset,\{1,2\}\}
,
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{P}(\{1,3\})
.
We have
\varSigma\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{J}}=\mathcal{P}(X)
. Also
\mathcal{I}\cap\varSigma=\{\emptyset\}=\varSigma_{0}
and

\mathcal{J}\cap\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}=\mathcal{J}\cap\{\emptyset,\{1,2\},\{3,4\},X\}=\{\emptyset\}\subseteq(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{0}=\{\emptyset,\{1,2\}\}
.
However,
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
consists of all sets which are disjoint from
\{1,2\}
and their complements, so
\{1,3\}\notin\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
and therefore
\varSigma_{\mathcal{J}}\not\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{I}}
.

Proof.

By Definition
3.9
,
\mu_{\mathcal{I}}
and
(\mu_{\mathcal{I}})_{\mathcal{J}}
are defined and (i), (ii) hold.

(iii) Existence of
\mu_{\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J}}
follows from Lemma
3.8
again, using Proposition
3.16
(iv) and
\varSigma\cap(\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J})\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
which follows from the fact that
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
, and therefore also
\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J}
, are contained in the null ideal of
(\mu_{\mathcal{I}})_{\mathcal{J}}
which extends
\mu
.

(iv) The equality of the domains is from Proposition
3.16
(vi). Then (ii) gives
(\mu_{\mathcal{I}})_{\mathcal{J}}(A\,\triangle\,I\,\triangle\,J)=\mu(A)=\mu_{\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J}}(A\,\triangle\,I\,\triangle\,J)
since
I\,\triangle\,J\in\mathcal{I}\vee_{\sigma}\mathcal{J}
for
I\in\mathcal{I}
and
J\in\mathcal{J}
.

(v) This part is as in Proposition 2.3 of
[
4
]
. To verify
\varSigma\cap\mathcal{J}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
, let
A\in\varSigma\cap\mathcal{J}
. Then
A\in\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}\cap\mathcal{J}\subseteq(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{0}
, so
\mu(A)=\mu_{\mathcal{I}}(A)=0
, hence
A\in\varSigma_{0}
. To verify
\varSigma_{\mathcal{J}}\cap\mathcal{I}\subseteq(\varSigma_{\mathcal{J}})_{0}
, let
A\in\varSigma
,
J\in\mathcal{J}
satisfy
A\,\triangle\,J=:I\in\mathcal{I}
. Then
A\,\triangle\,I=J\in\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}\cap\mathcal{J}\subseteq(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{0}
, so
\mu_{\mathcal{J}}(A\,\triangle\,J)=\mu(A)=\mu_{\mathcal{I}}(A\,\triangle\,I)=0
, and thus
A\,\triangle\,J\in(\varSigma_{\mathcal{J}})_{0}
.

(vi) Clear from (v), as well as (ii) (or (iv)) and the same formula with
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
interchanged.
∎

Definition 3.21
.

Given sets
X
and
Y
, for
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)
define

\mathcal{I}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y:=\{F:F\subseteq I\times Y\ \text{for some}\ I\in\mathcal{I}\}
.
Similarly, for
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Y)
, define
X\,\widehat{\times}\,\mathcal{J}:=\{F:F\subseteq X\times J\ \text{for some}\ J\in\mathcal{J}\}
.

Propositions
3.22
and
3.25
are building on Proposition 2.10 and Remark 2.12 of
[
4
]
.

Proposition 3.22
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces, and let
(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})
and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\mathcal{I}^{\prime})
belong to
\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
, where
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y
, and
\varSigma\cap\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
.

(i)

If the triple
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
satisfies
[C]
then
\Upsilon\cap\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\Upsilon_{0}
, so that
\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
is defined, and the triple
(X,\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}},\mu_{\mathcal{I}})
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}},\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}})
satisfies
[C]
.

(ii)

If the triple
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
satisfies
[\overline{C}]
then
\Upsilon\cap\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\Upsilon_{0}
. If also for each
F\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
there is
Q\in T_{0}
with
F^{y}\in\mathcal{I}
when
y\notin Q
