, then the triple
(X,\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}},\mu_{\mathcal{I}})
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}},\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}})
satisfies
[\overline{C}]
.

Taking
\mathcal{I}=\varSigma_{0}
we get the following corollary which generalizes the observation in Remark 2.9 of
[
5
]
that the first part holds when
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{N}
.

Corollary 3.23
.

Given probability spaces
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
, if
[C]
holds for
\upsilon
, then it holds also for
\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
for any
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y
with
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\mathcal{I}^{\prime})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
.
The same is true with
[C]
replaced by
[\overline{C}]
, if for each
F\in\mathcal{I}
there is
Q\in T_{0}
with
F^{y}\in\varSigma_{0}
when
y\notin Q
.

To appreciate the need for the assumption on the
F^{y}
, consider the following example.

Example 3.24
.

The property
[\overline{C}]
fails for the triple
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}},\upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}})
, where
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,T,\nu)
are the Lebesgue measure space on
[0,1]
and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
is the Lebesgue measure space on
[0,1]^{2}
.

To see this, build a set
E\subseteq X\times Y
so that
E
is the graph of a function (and hence belongs to
\mathfrak{N}
) but for each
y\in Y
,
E^{y}
is a Bernstein set (and hence is not measurable). For example, see Exercise 5 page 28 of
[
20
]
which produces a family of disjoint Bernstein sets
E^{y}
,
y\in[0,1]
, then take
E=\bigcup_{y}E^{y}\times\{y\}
.
9
9

9

To do this directly, let
\langle(K_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}):\alpha<\mathfrak{c}\rangle
enumerate all pairs
(K,y)
consisting of an uncountable closed set
K\subseteq[0,1]
and a point
y\in[0,1]
. Recursively choose two distinct points
x_{\alpha},x^{\prime}_{\alpha}\in K_{\alpha}\setminus(\{x_{\beta}:\beta<\alpha\}\cup\{x^{\prime}_{\beta}:\beta<\alpha\})
and let
E=\{(x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha}):\alpha<\mathfrak{c}\}
.

Proof of Proposition
3.22
.

(i) For
\Upsilon\cap\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\Upsilon_{0}
, let
F\in\Upsilon\cap\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. We have
F\subseteq I\times Y
for some
I\in\mathcal{I}
.
For some
P\in\varSigma_{0}
, for
x\notin P
we have
F_{x}\in T
, and
x\mapsto\nu(F_{x})
is
\varSigma
-measurable on
P^{c}
. The set
A=\{x\in P^{c}:\nu(F_{x})>0\}
belongs to
\varSigma
and is contained in
I
. Thus
A\in\varSigma\cap\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
and therefore
\upsilon(F)=\int_{P^{c}}\nu(F_{x})\,d\mu(x)=0
, so
F\in\Upsilon_{0}
. The existence of
\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
then follows from Lemma
3.8
.

To prove
[C]
, let
L\in\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
. Write
L=A\,\triangle\,F
where
A\in\Upsilon
and
F\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
,
F\subseteq I\times Y
for some
I\in\mathcal{I}
.
For some
P\in\varSigma_{0}
, for
x\notin P
we have
A_{x}\in T
, and
x\mapsto\nu(A_{x})
is
\varSigma
-measurable on
P^{c}
. Then
P\cup I\in(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{0}
and when
x\notin P\cup I
we have
L_{x}=A_{x}\,\triangle\,F_{x}=A_{x}\in T
and
x\mapsto\nu(L_{x})=\nu(A_{x})
is
\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
-measurable on
(P\cup I)^{c}
. Moreover,
\int_{(P\cup I)^{c}}\nu(L_{x})\,d\mu_{\mathcal{I}}(x)=\int_{(P\cup I)^{c}}\nu(A_{x})\,d\mu_{\mathcal{I}}(x)=\int_{P^{c}}\nu(A_{x})\,d\mu_{\mathcal{I}}(x)=\int_{P^{c}}\nu(A_{x})\,d\mu(x)=\upsilon(A)=\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}(L)
.

(ii) For
\Upsilon\cap\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\Upsilon_{0}
, let
F\in\Upsilon\cap\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. We have
F\subseteq I\times Y
for some
I\in\mathcal{I}
.
For some
Q\in T_{0}
, for
y\notin Q
we have
F^{y}\in\varSigma
and
F^{y}\in\mathcal{I}
.
10
10

10

The assumption on the
F^{y}
is not needed here because
F^{y}\in\varSigma
and
F^{y}\subseteq I
together imply
F^{y}\in\mathcal{I}
. The assumption is used in an essential way later in the proof.

Thus
F^{y}\in\varSigma\cap\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
. It follows by
[\overline{C}]
that
\upsilon(F)=0
.

For
[\overline{C}]
, let
L\in\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
. Write
L=A\,\triangle\,F
where
A\in\Upsilon
,
F\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
,
F\subseteq I\times Y
for some
I\in\mathcal{I}
.
For some
Q\in T_{0}
, for
y\notin Q
we have
A^{y}\in\varSigma
,
y\mapsto\mu(A^{y})
is
T
-measurable on
Q^{c}
, and
F^{y}\in\mathcal{I}
. Then when
y\notin Q
we have
L^{y}=A^{y}\,\triangle\,F^{y}\in\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}}
, and
y\mapsto\mu_{\mathcal{I}}(L^{y})=\mu_{\mathcal{I}}(A^{y}\,\triangle\,F^{y})=\mu_{\mathcal{I}}(A^{y})=\mu(A^{y})
is
T
-measurable on
Q^{c}
. Moreover,
\int_{Q^{c}}\mu_{\mathcal{I}}(L^{y})\,d\nu(y)=\int_{Q^{c}}\mu(A^{y})\,d\nu(y)=\upsilon(A)=\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}(L)
. Thus the triple
(X,\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}},\mu_{\mathcal{I}})
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}},\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}})
satisfies
[\overline{C}]
.
∎

Proposition 3.25
.

Suppose the triple
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)

satisfies
[C]
and
[\overline{C}]
, and we are given the following.

(i)

(X,\varSigma,\mathcal{I})
and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\mathcal{I}^{\prime})
in
\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
, where
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y
, and
\varSigma\cap\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
.

(ii)

(Y,T,\mathcal{J})
and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\mathcal{J}^{\prime})
in
\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
, where
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\subseteq X\,\widehat{\times}\,\mathcal{J}
, and
T\cap\mathcal{J}\subseteq T_{0}
.

(iii)

F\cap G\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
when
F\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
,
G\in\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
.

Then
(X\times Y,\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
and
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\subseteq(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}})_{0}
so that
(\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}})_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
is defined, and symmetrically
(\upsilon_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}})_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
is defined and
(\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}})_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=(\upsilon_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}})_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
. If

(iv)

for each
F\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
there is
Q\in T_{0}
with
F^{y}\in\mathcal{I}
when
y\notin Q
, and

(v)

for each
G\in\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
there is
P\in\varSigma_{0}
with
G_{x}\in\mathcal{J}
when
x\notin P
,

then the triple
(X,\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}},\mu_{\mathcal{I}})
,
(Y,T_{\mathcal{J}},\nu_{\mathcal{J}})
,
(X\times Y,(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}})_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}},(\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}})_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}})
satisfies
[C]
and
[\overline{C}]
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.22
(i),
\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
is defined.

For
(X\times Y,\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
, let
A\in\Upsilon
,
F\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
,
G\in\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
. We want to show that
(A\,\triangle\,F)\cap G\in\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
. This follows from

(A\,\triangle\,F)\cap G=(A\cap G)\,\triangle\,(F\cap G)
and the fact that
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\mathcal{J}^{\prime})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
and assumption (c) holds.

To verify
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\subseteq(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}})_{0}
, suppose we have
A\in\Upsilon
,
F\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
,
F\subseteq I\times Y
for some
I\in\mathcal{I}
and suppose that
A\,\triangle\,F\subseteq X\times J
for some
J\in\mathcal{J}
. Thus,
A\subseteq(I\times Y)\cup(X\times J)
. By
[C]
, for some
P\in\varSigma_{0}
,
x\notin P
implies
[A]_{x}\in T
, and
x\mapsto\nu([A]_{x})
is
\varSigma
-measurable on
P^{c}
. When
x\notin P\cup I
, we have
[A]_{x}\subseteq J
, and hence (since
[A]_{x}\in T
)
[A]_{x}\in\mathcal{J}
, so
[A]_{x}\in T\cap\mathcal{J}\subseteq T_{0}
. Thus, the set
\{x\in P^{c}:\nu([A]_{x})>0\}
is contained in
I
and therefore belongs to
\varSigma\cap\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
. Thus, from
[C]
we get
\upsilon(A)=0
and hence
\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}(A\,\triangle\,F)=\upsilon(A)=0
.

The equality
(\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}})_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=(\upsilon_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}})_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
then follows from Proposition
3.19
.

Now suppose (d) and (e) hold. From Proposition
3.22
(i), we get that

(3.26)

(X,\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}},\mu_{\mathcal{I}}),\ (Y,T,\nu),\ (X\times Y,\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}},\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}})

satisfies
[C]
. Using this fact we can apply Proposition
3.22
(ii)
⟂
to (
3.26
), the other required assumptions being that
(Y,T,\mathcal{J})
and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})
belong to
\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
,
T\cap\mathcal{J}\subseteq T_{0}
, and for each
G\in\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
there is
P\in(\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}})_{0}
with
G_{x}\in\mathcal{J}
when
x\notin P
. These are covered by (b) and (e), or were already proven. We get the conclusion that

(3.27)

(X,\varSigma_{\mathcal{I}},\mu_{\mathcal{I}}),\ (Y,T_{\mathcal{J}},\nu_{\mathcal{J}}),\ (X\times Y,(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}})_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}},(\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}})_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}})

satisfies
[C]
.
Similarly, from Proposition
3.22
(ii), we get that (
3.26
) satisfies
[\overline{C}]
. Using this fact, we can apply Proposition
3.22
(i)
⟂
to this triple, the other required assumptions being that
(Y,T,\mathcal{J})
and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})
belong to
\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
, and
T\cap\mathcal{J}\subseteq T_{0}
. These are covered by (b), or were already proven. We conclude that (
3.27
) satisfies
[\overline{C}]
.
∎

Proposition 3.28
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
, and
(X\times{Y},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces. Write
T=(T_{x})_{x\in X}
,
T_{0}=(T_{x,0})_{x\in X}
, where
T_{x,0}
is the null ideal of
\nu_{x}
. Let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
be a
\sigma
-ideal of
\varSigma
.

(i)

If
[C]
holds and
\Upsilon\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
then
\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{0}}
satisfies
[C]
.

(ii)

If
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
holds then
\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{0}}
satisfies
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
.

Remark 3.29
.

It follows that the same conclusion holds for any smaller ideal of
\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
in the place of
\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{0}
.

Proof.

(i) Assume that
[C]
holds and
\Upsilon\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
.
We have
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{0})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
, since
\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{0}
is a
\sigma
-ideal of
\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
and
\Upsilon\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
.
We also have

(\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{0})\cap\Upsilon\subseteq(\varSigma_{0}\ltimes T_{0})\cap\Upsilon=\Upsilon_{0}.

Thus,
\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{0}}
is defined. To prove that
\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{0}}
satisfies
[C]
, let
A\in\Upsilon
,
F\in\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{0}
.
By
[C]
for
\upsilon
, by the definition of
\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{0}
, and by
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
, there is a
P\in\varSigma_{0}
such that for
x\in P^{c}
we have
A_{x}\in T_{x}
and
F_{x}\in T_{x,0}
, the map
x\mapsto\nu_{x}(A_{x})
is
\varSigma
-measurable on
P^{c}
, and
\upsilon(A)=\int_{P^{c}}\nu_{x}(A_{x})\,d\mu(x)
. Then for
x\in P^{c}
,
[A\,\triangle\,F]_{x}=A_{x}\,\triangle\,F_{x}\in T_{x}
,
x\mapsto\nu_{x}([A\,\triangle\,F]_{x})=\nu_{x}(A_{x})
is
\varSigma
-measurable on
P^{c}
, and
\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{0}}(A\,\triangle\,F)=\upsilon(A)=\int_{P^{c}}\nu_{x}(A_{x})\,d\mu(x)=\int_{P^{c}}\nu_{x}([A\,\triangle\,F]_{x})\,d\mu(x)
.

(ii) By
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
, the assumptions of (i) are satisfied, so
\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{0}}
is defined. The proof of
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
proceeds as in (i), this time choosing
P\in\mathcal{I}
.
∎

Example 3.30
.

If
[C]
holds for the triple
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
, then it is easy to see that
\Upsilon\cap\mathfrak{N}=\Upsilon_{0}
and
\Upsilon\cap\mathfrak{N}_{2}\subseteq\Upsilon_{0}
(with equality if
[\overline{C}]
also holds). Hence, for
\mathcal{K}=\mathfrak{N}_{2},\mathfrak{N}
the measures
\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}
are defined by Lemma
3.8
. The spaces
(X\times Y,\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}},\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
also satisfy
[C]
, and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}_{2}},\upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}_{2}})
satisfies
[\overline{C}]
if
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
does (by Proposition
3.28
and
3.28
⟂
).
In particular, the probability measure
\mu\otimes_{\mathcal{I}}\nu:=(\mu\otimes\nu)_{\mathcal{I}}
is the unique extension of
\mu\otimes\nu
onto
\varSigma\otimes_{\mathcal{I}}T:=(\varSigma\otimes T)_{\mathcal{I}}
having null ideal
\mathcal{I}
, where

\varSigma\otimes T\subseteq\varSigma\,\widehat{\otimes}\,T\subseteq\varSigma\otimes_{\mathfrak{N}_{2}}T\subseteq\varSigma\otimes_{\mathfrak{N}}T\subseteq\mu\ltimes T,

\mu\ltimes\nu\upharpoonright\varSigma\otimes_{\mathfrak{N}}T=\mu\otimes_{\mathfrak{N}}\nu,\ \mu\otimes_{\mathfrak{N}}\nu\upharpoonright\varSigma\otimes_{\mathfrak{N}_{2}}T=\mu\otimes_{\mathfrak{N}_{2}}\nu,\ \mu\otimes_{\mathfrak{N}_{2}}\nu\upharpoonright\varSigma\,\widehat{\otimes}\,T=\mu\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\nu,

(X\times Y,\varSigma\otimes_{\mathcal{I}}T,\mu\otimes_{\mathcal{I}}\nu)
is a complete probability space for

\mathcal{I}=\mathfrak{N}_{2},\mathfrak{N}
, and the measure algebras of

\mu\otimes_{\mathfrak{N}}\nu
,
\mu\otimes_{\mathfrak{N}_{2}}\nu
,
\mu\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\nu
, and
\mu{\otimes}\nu
are Boolean isomorphic.

4.
Vector liftings

The definition of vector lifting in Definition
2.7
can be given in purely linear algebraic terms, and we begin by taking that point of view.
As is well-known, every surjective linear map
r\colon X\to Z
of vector spaces has a linear right inverse which can be obtained by choosing a basis
\langle z_{i}\rangle
for
Z
, selecting any
x_{i}\in r^{-1}(z_{i})
and extending the map
z_{i}\mapsto x_{i}
by linearity to get a right inverse
s\colon Z\to X
. The right inverse can be given equivalently as the composition
sr\colon X\to X
,
s
being recoverable from
sr
by the formula
s(r(x))=(sr)(x)
. We are interested in the case where
Z=X/Y
is a quotient of
X
by a subspace
Y
.

Definition 4.1
.

Given a vector space
X
and a linear subspace
Y
of
X
write
x=_{Y}z
if
x-z\in Y
,
x^{\bullet}:=\{z\in X:x=_{Y}z\}
,

X/Y:=\{x^{\bullet}:x\in X\}
, and let
r_{Y}:x\in X\mapsto x^{\bullet}\in X/Y
be the quotient map.
A linear map
\xi:X\to X
is called a
vector lifting
for
X
mod
Y
, if for all
x,x_{1},x_{2}\in X
,
\xi(x)=_{Y}x
, and if
x_{1}=_{Y}x_{2}
then
\xi(x_{1})=\xi(x_{2})
.

W(X,Y)
denotes the set of all vector liftings for
X
mod
Y
.
Given, in addition, a linear subspace
C
of
X
a
\xi\in W(X,Y)
is called
C
-strong
, if
\xi(x)=x
for every
x\in C
.
W(X,Y,C)
denotes the set of all
C
-strong
\xi\in W(X,Y)
.

Remark 4.2
.

As recalled above, a vector lifting
\xi
can be given equivalently as a linear map
s\colon X/Y\to X
satisfying
s(a)\in a
for each
a\in X/Y
, the two versions being definable from each other by the formula
\xi(x)=s(x^{\bullet})
. In category theoretic terms, such an
s
is a section of
r_{Y}
(which is a retraction). (See
[
32
]
, Chapter III, Definitions 9.5.2.) We have
\xi=s\circ r_{Y}
.

Since the sections
s
are linear, they are determined by their values on a basis for
X/Y
. It will be convenient to state this as a proposition. As it is standard linear algebra, we omit the proof.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
X
be a vector space,
Y
a subspace of
X
. For each family
\langle x_{i}\rangle
of elements of
X
such that
\langle x_{i}^{\bullet}\rangle
is a basis for
X/Y
, there is a unique vector lifting
\xi\colon X\to X
such that
\xi(x_{i})=x_{i}
for each index
i
. Conversely, each vector lifting has this form for some basis
\langle x_{i}^{\bullet}\rangle
of
X/Y
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Given a vector space
X
, and linear subspaces
Y
and
C
of
X
, we have that
W(X,Y,C)\not=\emptyset
if and only the condition

(N_{C,Y})\;C\cap Y=\{0\}
is satisfied.

Proof.

\xi\in W(X,Y,C)
implies for
x\in C\cap Y
that

x=\xi(x)=\xi(0)=0
, so
(N_{C,Y})
holds.

Conversely, if
(N_{C,Y})
is satisfied then
C
and
C^{\bullet}=C/Y
are linearly isomorphic. Given a basis
\langle x_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}
for
C
, the basis
\langle x_{i}^{\bullet}\rangle_{i\in I}
for
C^{\bullet}
can be extended to a basis
\langle x_{i}^{\bullet}\rangle_{i\in J}
for
X/Y
. The unique lifting
\xi
satisfying
\xi(x_{i})=x_{i}
for all
i
is
C
-strong.
∎

We now specialize to subspaces of
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu)
, with
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{R}}
or
{\mathbb{C}}
.
Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be a measure space,
\mathcal{M}
a linear subspace of
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu)
containing the family
C
of constant functions. Letting
\mathcal{N}(\mu)
be the null ideal of
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu)
, the family
V(\mathcal{M})=V(\mu,\mathcal{M})
of vector liftings for
\mathcal{M}
as defined in Definition
2.7
are the elements of
W(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{N}(\mu),C)
.
Hence,
\gamma:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
is a vector lifting for
\mathcal{M}
if
\gamma
is linear, satisfies
(l1)
and
(l2)
, and
\gamma(1)=1
.

Remark 4.5
.

Note that when
X
is empty,
\mathcal{M}=\{\emptyset\}
contains only the empty function which serves as every constant function. The unique function
\gamma\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
is a vector lifting. Of course this case is no interest.

As mentioned in the introduction, if
\mu
is a diffuse measure, i.e.,
\mu
has no atoms, then there are no linear liftings for
\mathcal{L}^{p}({\mathbb{R}},\mu)
when
1\leq p<\infty
(
[
18
]
, Chap. IV, Theorem 6). The proof in
[
18
]
uses the continuity of positive linear functionals on
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}},\mu)
, but the argument can be modified to avoid linearity as the following proposition shows.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be any diffuse probability space, and let
0\leq p<\infty
. There is no order-preserving selector for
\mathcal{L}^{p}({\mathbb{R}},\mu)
, i.e., there is no function
\varphi\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}({\mathbb{R}},\mu)\to\mathcal{L}^{p}({\mathbb{R}},\mu)
satisfying for all
f,f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}({\mathbb{R}},\mu)
that
\varphi(f)=_{\mu}f
, and
f_{1}\leq_{\mu}f_{2}
implies
\varphi(f_{1})\leq\varphi(f_{2})
.

Proof.

For
0\leq p<q\leq\infty
, we have the inclusion
\mathcal{L}^{q}(\mu)\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
(
[
10
]
, Proposition 6.12). An order-preserving selector
\varphi
for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
as in the statement would yield one for
\mathcal{L}^{q}(\mu)
by restriction. Hence to prove nonexistence we may assume
p\geq 1
.

Suppose
\varphi
were such a function.
Fix for each
n
a partition
\mathcal{S}_{n}
of
X
into
n
sets of measure
1/n
(
[
12
]
215D) and set
\mathcal{S}=\bigcup_{n}\mathcal{S}_{n}
. Choose
x\in X
so
that
\varphi(f)(x)=f(x)
for each of the countably many functions
f
of the form

f=\sum_{n=1}^{N}a_{n}b_{n}^{1/p}f_{n},

where
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
a_{n},b_{n}
are positive rational numbers, and
f_{n}=\chi_{E_{n}}
with
E_{n}\in\mathcal{S}
.
For each
n
, choose
E_{n}\in\mathcal{S}
such that
x\in E_{n}
and

\mu(E_{n})\leq 1/n^{p}
. Let
f_{n}=\chi_{E_{n}}
. Then

\|f_{n}\|_{p}=\mu(E_{n})^{1/p}\leq\frac{1}{n}.

In
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}},\mu)
, the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-2}f_{n}/\|f_{n}\|_{p}
is absolutely summable and hence converges. Let
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}({\mathbb{R}},\mu)
be any function in the class of the limit. Define
g_{N}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{-2}f_{n}/\|f_{n}\|_{p}
and write
g=g_{N}+h_{N}
. The function
h_{N}
belongs to the class

\sum_{n=N+1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\frac{1}{\|f_{n}\|_{p}}f_{n}

in
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}},\mu)
and hence is almost everywhere nonnegative
since the positive cone is closed in the norm topology (see for example
[
31
]
, II 5.2 (ii))
and the partial sums of this series are nonnegative. It follows that
g\geq g_{N}
almost everywhere. By our choice of
x
,
\varphi(g_{N})(x)=g_{N}(x)
for all
N
.
We have for any
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\varphi(g)(x)\geq\varphi(g_{N})(x)=g_{N}(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\frac{1}{\|f_{n}\|_{p}}f_{n}(x)\geq\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{1}{n}\to\infty\ \text{as}\ N\to\infty,

contradiction.
∎

In contrast, vector liftings always exist for any probability space, except in trivial cases.

Proposition 4.7
.

A vector lifting for
\mathcal{M}
exists if and only if either
X=\emptyset
or
\mu(X)>0
.

Proof.

If
X
is nonempty but
\mu(X)=0
then no vector lifting
\gamma
exists, because the constant functions all belong to the same measure class, so they cannot all be fixed.
Conversely, if
X=\emptyset
then a vector lifting exists as pointed out in Remark
4.5
, and if
\mu(X)>0
, and
C
is the subspace of constant functions in
\mathcal{M}
, then a vector lifting for
\mathcal{M}
is a member of
W(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{N}(\mu),C)
which exists by Proposition
4.4
since nonzero constant functions are not in
\mathcal{N}(\mu)
.
∎

The following simple observation will be useful.

Remark 4.8
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be a probability space and let
\mathcal{M}
be a subspace of
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu)
.
If
\langle f_{i}^{\bullet}\rangle_{i\in I}
with each
f_{i}\in\mathcal{M}
is a linear basis for
\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}
, and we extend it to a basis for
L^{p}(\mu)
,
\langle f_{i}^{\bullet}\rangle_{i\in J}
, where
I\subseteq J
, then the vector lifting
\pi\in V^{p}(\mu)
satisfying
\pi(f_{i})=f_{i}
for
i\in J
also satisfies
\pi(f)\in\mathcal{M}
for all
f\in\mathcal{M}
.
(Writing
f^{\bullet}=\sum_{i}a_{i}f_{i}^{\bullet}
,
\langle a_{i}\rangle
of finite support in
\mathbb{K}
, we have
a_{i}=0
for
i\notin I
, so
\pi(f)=\sum_{i}a_{i}f_{i}\in\mathcal{M}
.)

For ease of reference later, we give some examples of the use of this remark.

Example 4.9
.

(a) Letting
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be the Lebesgue measure space on
X=[0,1]
, by the Lusin theorem, each class in
L^{0}(\mu)
contains a function of Baire class
1
(i.e., a pointwise limit of continuous functions). Since the Baire class
1
functions form a subspace, it follows that there is a vector lifting for
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu)
whose values are Baire class
1
functions.

(b) Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[C]
and
\varSigma_{0}\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
.
Every element
h\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
is equal modulo
\upsilon
to an element
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
satisfying
[h^{\prime}]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for all
x\in X
(Corollary
2.21
(i)). Since the family of such elements
h^{\prime}
is a subspace, it follows that there is a vector lifting
\pi\in V^{p}(\upsilon)
whose values satisfy
[\pi(f)]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for
x\in X
.

(c) If in (b) we also have
[\overline{C}]
and
X\,\dot{\times}\,T_{0}\subseteq\Upsilon
, then we get a vector lifting
\pi\in V^{p}(\upsilon)
whose values satisfy
[\pi(f)]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for
x\in X
and
[\pi(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for
y\in Y
(using Corollary
2.21
(ii)).

(d) Let
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
be a topological probability space.
If
\langle f_{i}^{\bullet}\rangle_{i\in I}
is a basis for
C^{p}(X)^{\bullet}
, with each
f_{i}
continuous, which we extend to a basis
\langle f_{i}^{\bullet}\rangle_{i\in J}
,
I\subseteq J
, for
L^{p}(\mu)
, then the vector lifting
\pi\in V^{p}(\mu)
given by
\pi(f_{i})=f_{i}
for
i\in J
satisfies that
\pi(f)
is continuous for each
f\in C^{p}(X)
. Under the condition
(N_{C,\mu})
defined next,
\langle f_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}
is a basis for
C^{p}(X)
and we get
\pi(f)=f
for each
f\in C^{p}(X)
.

Definition 4.10
.

Given a topological measure space
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
we put

C^{p}(X,{\mathbb{K}}):=C(X,{{\mathbb{K}}})\cap\mathcal{L}^{p}({\mathbb{K}},\mu).

As with the notation for
\mathcal{L}^{p}({\mathbb{K}},\mu)
, we will omit the mention of
{\mathbb{K}}
, writing simply
C^{p}(X)
. In principle, when
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{C}}
, we could consider either
C^{p}(X,{\mathbb{R}})
or
C^{p}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
inside of
\mathcal{L}^{p}({\mathbb{C}},\mu)
, but by
C^{p}(X)
we will always mean
C^{p}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
in this context. Note that
C^{0}(X)=C(X)
.

Recall from Definition
2.7
that we say
\eta\in V^{p}(\mu)
is strong when
\eta(f)=f
for every
f\in C^{p}(X)
. We write

V^{p}(\mathfrak{S},\mu)
for the set of all strong
\eta\in V^{p}(\mu)
and
(N_{C,\mu})
instead of
(N_{C^{p}(X),\mathcal{N}(\mu)})
. (We have
\mathcal{N}(\mu)\cap C^{p}(X)=\mathcal{N}(\mu)\cap C(X)
for any
p
, so the notation does not need to mention
p
.)

[formula]

\mathcal{N}(\mu)\cap C(X)=\{0\}.

Remark 4.11
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
be a topological measure space.

(i)

The property
[N_{\mathfrak{S},\varSigma_{0}}]\;\mathfrak{S}\cap\varSigma_{0}=\{\emptyset\}
implies the condition
(N_{C,\mu})
.

Proof.

For
h\in\mathcal{N}(\mu)\cap C(X)
, it follows
\{h\not=0\}\in\mathfrak{S}\cap\varSigma_{0}=\{\emptyset\}
, i.e.
h=0
.
∎

(ii)

If
(X,\mathfrak{S})
is Tychonoff, then
(N_{C,\mu})
implies
[N_{\mathfrak{S},\varSigma_{0}}]
.

Proof.

For
G\in\mathfrak{S}\cap\varSigma_{0}
, if
G\not=\emptyset
and
x_{0}\in G
then by the Tychonoff property, we can choose
0\leq h\in C(X)
with
0\leq h\leq 1
,
h(x_{0})=1
and
h(x)=0
for every
x\in G^{c}
. It follows

h^{-1}[(0,\infty)]\subseteq G\in\varSigma_{0}
so
h\in\mathcal{N}(\mu)
and by
(N_{C,\mu})
get
h=0
, contradicting
h(x_{0})=1
. Thus,
G=\emptyset
.
∎

(iii)

If
(N_{C,\mu})
holds, then for
f\in C(X)
,
\sup\{|f(x)|:x\in X\}\leq\|f\|_{\infty}
. In particular,
\|f\|_{\infty}<\infty
implies that
f
is bounded.

Proof.

We may assume that
f
is real-valued and that
M:=\|f\|_{\infty}<\infty
. We have
(f-M)^{+}\in C(X)
and
(f-M)^{+}=_{\mu}0
, so
(f-M)^{+}=0
by
(N_{C,\mu})
. This gives
f\leq M
. Similarly,
f\geq-M
.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
be a measure space,
(X,\varSigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
an extension by null sets. Corresponding to
\eta\in V^{p}(\mu)
, we have
\eta^{\prime}\in V^{p}(\mu^{\prime})
extending
\eta
defined by
\eta^{\prime}(f+g):=\eta(f)

for
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
,
g\in\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\prime})
.
If moreover
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
is a topological measure space and
\eta\in V^{p}(\mathfrak{S},\mu)
, then

\eta^{\prime}\in V^{p}(\mathfrak{S},\mu^{\prime})
.

Proof.

For the sufficiency of defining
\eta^{\prime}
on elements
f+g
as stated, see Corollary
3.12
. Let
f,f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
g,g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\prime})
.

If
f_{1}+g_{1}=_{\mu^{\prime}}f_{2}+g_{2}
then
f_{1}-f_{2}=_{\mu^{\prime}}g_{2}-g_{1}
. We have
\{f_{1}\not=f_{2}\}\in\varSigma
and
\mu^{\prime}\{f_{1}\not=f_{2}\}=0
, so
\{f_{1}\not=f_{2}\}\in\varSigma_{0}
since
\mu^{\prime}
extends
\mu
. Thus,
\eta(f_{1})=\eta(f_{2})
, showing that
\eta^{\prime}
is well-defined and satisfies
(l2)
.
We also have
\eta^{\prime}(f+g)=\eta(f)=_{\mu^{\prime}}f=_{\mu^{\prime}}f+g
, showing that
\eta^{\prime}
satisfies
(l1)
.

For linearity, we have
\eta^{\prime}((f_{1}+g_{1})+(f_{2}+g_{2}))=\eta^{\prime}\bigl((f_{1}+f_{2})+(g_{1}+g_{2})\bigr)=\eta(f_{1}+f_{2})=\eta(f_{1})+\eta(f_{2})=\eta^{\prime}(f_{1}+g_{1})+\eta^{\prime}(f_{2}+g_{2})
, since
g_{1}+g_{2}\in\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\prime})
, and for
a\in{{\mathbb{K}}}
,
\eta^{\prime}(a(f+g))=\eta^{\prime}(af+ag)=\eta(af)=a\eta(f)=a\eta^{\prime}(f+g)
, because
ag\in\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\prime})
.
Also,
\eta^{\prime}(1)=\eta(1)=1
, so
\eta^{\prime}\in V^{p}(\mu^{\prime})
.
In the last sentence, for
h\in C^{p}(X)
we get
\eta^{\prime}(h)=\eta(h)=h
.
∎

Proposition 4.13
.

Given a topological measure space
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
, we have that

V^{p}(\mathfrak{S},\mu)\not=\emptyset
if and only if

(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
satisfies the condition
(N_{C,\mu})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.4
, taking
X
to be
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
,
Y
to be
\mathcal{N}(\mu)
, and
C
to be
C^{p}(X)
. ∎

Remark 4.14
.

By Example
4.9
(a), there is a vector lifting for Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]
whose values are Baire class 1 functions.
In contrast, it is consistent with ZFC that there are no linear liftings for Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]
, or for any power
[0,1]^{\kappa}
, whose values are Borel functions (see
[
6
]
).

Remark 4.15
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
be a compact Radon probability space with
\mathop{\rm supp}(\mu)=X
and
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{S}}(\mu)=\emptyset
(see
[
14
]
453N). It follows by Remark
4.11
(i), that
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
satisfies condition
(N^{p}_{C,\mu})
, implying along with Proposition
4.13
that
V^{p}(\mathfrak{S},\mu)\not=\emptyset
. So, it is, in general, impossible to convert a given vector lifting
\eta\in{V}^{\infty}(\mu)
into a lifting
\rho
on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mu)
(in the sense of
[
18
]
Chapter III) by any existing method.

For a topological probability space
(X,\mathfrak{T},\varSigma,\mu)
, we might try to get a multiplicative selector
\rho
for the classes of nonnegative measurable functions
X\to[0,\infty]
which is strong (i.e., fixes the continuous functions). On the family of continuous functions itself, we will have
\rho(fg)=fg=\rho(f)\rho(g)
as long as
fg
is continuous, but continuity of
fg
can fail because of the discontinuity of multiplication on
[0,\infty]
. The next example shows that even for functions that are finite-valued except at one point, there might not be a multiplicative selector.

Example 4.16
.

Let
\lambda
be Lebesgue measure on
X:=[-1/2,1/2]
equipped with the usual topology. Fix
p\in(0,\infty)
. For
c=1,2
, define
f,g_{c}\colon X\to[0,\infty]
by
f(x):=|x|^{1/(2p)}
,
g_{c}(x):=c/f(x)

(x\not=0)
,
g_{c}(0):=\infty
. Note that
f
is bounded and
\int|g_{c}|^{p}\,d\lambda<\infty
. Also,
f
and
g_{c}
are continuous and
fg_{c}
is continuous except at
0
.

There is no function
\rho
into
[0,\infty]^{X}
defined on a family of functions including
f
,
g_{c}
and
fg_{c}

(c=1,2)
, such that
\rho(h)=h
for
h
continuous,
\rho(fg_{c})=\rho(f)\rho(g_{c})
and the following weak version of
(l2)
holds.

(l2)^{*}

f_{1}=f_{2}
except at one point implies
\rho(f_{1})=\rho(f_{2})
.

If
\rho
had all of the listed properties, then

c=\rho(c)=\rho(fg_{c})=\rho(f)\rho(g_{c})=fg_{c}
. Evaluating at
0
we get
c=0\cdot\infty
for
c=1,2
, a contradiction no matter how we define
0\cdot\infty
.

Removing the requirement that
\rho
be strong, we have the following example which modifies an example of J. von Neumann defined originally on
X={\mathbb{R}}
.

Example 4.17
.

(Cf. J. von Neumann
[
28
]
.) Let
\lambda
be Lebesgue measure on
X:=[-1/2,1/2]
. Suppose
\rho:\mathcal{L}^{0}(\lambda)\to\mathcal{L}^{0}(\lambda)
(here
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\lambda)=\mathcal{L}^{0}({\mathbb{R}},\lambda)
) is linear and satisfies
(l1)
and
(l2)^{*}
(see Example
4.16
). Define

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle:=x,\ \ e(x):=1\ \ (x\in X)

\displaystyle g_{t}(x)

\displaystyle:=1/(x-t)\ \ (x,t\in X,\,x\not=t),\ \ g_{t}(t):=0.

A) For
\lambda
-almost all
t\in X
, either
\rho(fg_{t})\not=\rho(f)\rho(g_{t})
or
\rho(eg_{t})\not=\rho(e)\rho(g_{t})
.
11
11

11

Of course
eg_{t}=g_{t}
but we are emphasizing the failure of the multiplicative property.

By
(l1)
,
S:=\{t\in X:\rho(f)(t)=t
and
\rho(e)(t)=1\}
has measure one. Fix
t\in S
. Notice first that we have
f(x)g_{t}(x)-te(x)g_{t}(x)=(f(x)-te(x))g_{t}(x)=e(x)
when
x\not=t
. By linearity of
\rho
and
(l2)^{*}
,
\rho(fg_{t})-t\rho(eg_{t})=\rho(e)
. If we had
\rho(fg_{t})=\rho(f)\rho(g_{t})
and
\rho(eg_{t})=\rho(e)\rho(g_{t})
we would get
\rho(f)\rho(g_{t})-t\rho(e)\rho(g_{t})=\rho(e)
. Evaluating at
t
and taking into account
t\in S
gives a contradiction.

B) If we require also that
\rho(e)=e
, then by A),
\rho(fg_{t})\not=\rho(f)\rho(g_{t})
for
\lambda
-almost all
t
.

Example 4.18
.

Given a measure space
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
with
\mu(X)>0
, let
\gamma:\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu)\to\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu)
be an
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-vector lifting existing by Proposition
4.7
and define

\gamma_{\flat}(A):=\{\gamma(\chi_{A})\geq 1\}\quad\mbox{for}\quad A\in\varSigma.

\gamma_{\flat}:\varSigma\to\varSigma
satisfies
(Lj)
for
j=1,2
,
(N)
, and
(E)
.

Since
\gamma(\chi_{A})+\gamma(\chi_{A^{c}})=1
for
x\in\gamma_{\flat}(A)
it follows
\gamma(\chi_{A^{c}})(x)\leq 0
, so
x\in[\gamma_{\flat}(A^{c})]^{c}
, i.e.
\gamma_{\flat}(A)\subseteq[\gamma_{\flat}(A^{c})]^{c}
, implying

\gamma_{\flat}(A)\cap\gamma_{\flat}(A^{c})=\emptyset
, i.e.

\gamma_{\flat}
satisfies
(V)
.

Remark 4.19
.

Write
(X,\mathcal{L},\lambda)
for the Lebesgue measure space on
X:=[-1/2,1/2]
. Let

\xi
be an
{{\mathbb{R}}}
-vector lifting for
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\lambda)
. There does not exist a
\gamma:\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{L}
satisfying
(N)
and
(E)
, such that
\gamma_{0}=\xi
, where
\gamma_{0}(f)(x)=\sup\{r\in{\mathbb{Q}}:x\in\gamma\{r<f\}\}
.
(See
[
5
]
for the basic properties of
\gamma_{0}
.)

Proof.

Assume, that there exists a
\gamma:\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{L}
satisfying
(N)
and
(E)
, such that
\gamma_{0}=\xi
and let
E,F\in\mathcal{L}
satisfy
E\subseteq F
. If
G:=F\setminus E
we get

\xi(\chi_{E})+\xi(\chi_{G})=\xi(\chi_{F})
and by
[
5
]
, Proposition 3.4 (ii) get
\chi_{\gamma(E)}+\chi_{\gamma(G)}=\chi_{\gamma(F)}
.

x\in\gamma(E)\cap\gamma(G)
would imply

2=\chi_{\gamma(E)}(x)+\chi_{\gamma(G)}(x)=\chi_{\gamma(F)}(x)=1
, contradiction, i.e.
\gamma(E)\cap\gamma(G)=\emptyset
, so

\gamma(E)\subseteq\gamma(F)
, i.e.
\gamma
satisfies
(M)
and
\xi=\gamma_{0}
satisfies
(m)
, that is, for every
f,g\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\lambda)
,
f\leq{g}
implies
\xi(f)\leq\xi(g)
, i.e.
\xi
is an order-preserving vector lifting for
\lambda
, contradicting Proposition
4.6
.
∎

5.
Product vector liftings

Definition 5.1
.

Given sets
X,Y
and functions
f:X\to{{\mathbb{K}}}
and
g:Y\to{{\mathbb{K}}}
, define

(f\otimes{g})(x,y):=f(x)\cdot g(y)
for
(x,y)\in X\times Y
.

For later reference, we make the following observation concerning the nature of
f\otimes g
when
f
and
g
are measurable real- or complex-valued functions on probability spaces.

Remark 5.2
.

Fix
p\in[0,\infty]
,
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
probability spaces,
f\in\mathbb{K}^{X}
,
g\in\mathbb{K}^{Y}
,
h:=f\otimes g
.

(i)

If
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
then

h\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu\otimes\nu)
.

(ii)

If
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
,
g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
,

f_{1}=_{\mu}f_{2}
and
g_{1}=_{\nu}g_{2}
then
f_{1}\otimes g_{1}=_{\mu\otimes\nu}f_{2}\otimes g_{2}
.

Let
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be a third probability space so that
[P_{0}]
,
[C]
and
[\overline{C}]
hold.

(iii)

If
h\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
then either
f=_{\widehat{\mu}}0
, or
g=_{\widehat{\nu}}0
, or
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
.

(iv)

If
h\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
then
h=_{\upsilon}0
if and only if
f=_{\widehat{\mu}}0
or
g=_{\widehat{\nu}}0
.

If
\upsilon=\mu\otimes\nu
then in (iii) we can write
f=0
,
g=0
instead of
f=_{\widehat{\mu}}0
,
g=_{\widehat{\nu}}0
using the proof below with the set
M
taken to be empty.

Proof.

(i) The measurability of
f\otimes g
is clear since functions of one variable are also
\mu\otimes\nu
-measurable as functions of two variables, and products of measurable functions are measurable. The statement thus holds for
p=0
.
For
p=\infty
, we have
a,b>0
,
N\in\varSigma_{0}
and
M\in T_{0}
such that
|f(x)|\leq a
,
|g(y)|\leq b
when
x\notin N
,
y\notin M
. Then
|f(x)g(y)|\leq ab
when
(x,y)\notin N\times Y\cup X\times M
, so
f\otimes g\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(X\times Y,\mu\otimes\nu)
.
For
0<p<\infty
, we have by the Tonelli Theorem,
\int|f(x)g(y)|^{p}\,d\mu(x)\,d\nu(y)=\int|f(x)|^{p}\,d\mu(x)\int|g(y)|^{p}\,d\nu(y)<\infty
, so
f\otimes g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(X\times Y,\mu\otimes\nu)
.

(ii) There exist
N\in\varSigma_{0}
and
M\in T_{0}
such that
f_{1}(x)=f_{2}(x)
when
x\notin N
and
g_{1}(y)=g_{2}(y)
when
y\notin M
. Then
f_{1}(x)g_{1}(y)=f_{2}(x)g_{2}(y)
when
(x,y)\notin N\times Y\cup X\times M
.

(iii) Suppose we have neither
f=_{\widehat{\mu}}0
nor
g=_{\widehat{\nu}}0
. By
[C]
, there is an
M\in\varSigma_{0}
such that
h_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
when
x\notin M
. Choose
x\notin M
such that
f(x)\not=0
. Then
g=(1/f(x))h_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
. Similarly, using
[\overline{C}]
we get
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
.

(iv)
(\Rightarrow)
We prove the contrapositive. Suppose we have neither
f=_{\widehat{\mu}}0
nor
g=_{\widehat{\nu}}0
. By (iii),
f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu)
and
g\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)
. Then the set
\{f\not=0\}\times\{g\not=0\}\in\varSigma\otimes T\subseteq\Upsilon
has positive
\mu\otimes\nu
-measure, and hence positive
\upsilon
-measure, so
h=f\otimes g=_{\upsilon}0
fails.

(\Leftarrow)
Say
f=_{\widehat{\mu}}0
. We have
f(x)=0
when
x\in M^{c}
for some
M\in\varSigma_{0}
. Then
h(x,y)=0
when
(x,y)\notin M\times Y
. By
[P_{0}]
,
M\times Y\in\Upsilon
, and by
[C]
,
\upsilon(M\times Y)=0
, so
h=_{\upsilon}0
.
∎

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
h\colon X\times Y\to\mathbb{K}
.
Write

{\mathcal{V}}=\operatorname{span}\{h_{x}:x\in X\},\ \ \mathcal{H}=\operatorname{span}\{h^{y}:y\in Y\},

the spans of the collections of vertical and horizontal sections of
h
, respectively. The following statements are equivalent.

(i)

h=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\otimes g_{i}
for some
f_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
,
g_{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
i=1,\dots,n
,

(ii)

h=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\otimes g_{i}
for some
f_{i}\in\mathbb{K}^{X}
,
g_{i}\in\mathbb{K}^{Y}
,
i=1,\dots,n
,

(iii)

{\mathcal{V}}
is finite-dimensional.

Note that by the symmetry of (a), we could say in (c) that
\mathcal{H}
is finite-dimensional. The equivalence of the two forms of (c) is basic linear algebra though since
\dim\mathcal{H}=\dim{\mathcal{V}}
when either of these dimensions is finite. This is essentially the standard fact that the row-rank and column-rank of a matrix are equal. (The equality can fail when the dimensions are not finite.)

Proof.

Clearly (a) implies (b), and if (b) holds then
{\mathcal{V}}
is a subspace of
\operatorname{span}\{g_{i}:i=1,\dots,n\}
, so (c) holds.

There remains to show that (c) implies (a). Let
\{g_{i}:i=1,\dots,n\}
be a basis for
{\mathcal{V}}
. Define functions
f_{i}
on
X
by writing for each
x
,

h_{x}(\,\cdot\,)=\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)g_{i}(\,\cdot\,)

Since the
g_{i}
are linearly independent, we can choose
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
such that the matrix with
(i,j)
-entry
g_{i}(y_{j})
is invertible.
Then the functions

h^{y_{j}}(\,\cdot\,)=\sum_{i}f_{i}(\,\cdot\,)g_{i}(y_{j}),\ \ j=1,\dots,n

all belong to
\mathcal{H}
, and hence so do the
f_{i}
since we can solve for them as linear combinations of the
h^{y_{j}}
.
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
X
and
Y
be topological spaces and assume that
\mathbb{K}
is equipped with a topology. Let
h\colon X\times Y\to\mathbb{K}

be separately continuous. The following statements are equivalent.

(i)

h=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\otimes g_{i}
for some
f_{i}\in\mathbb{K}^{X}
,
g_{i}\in\mathbb{K}^{Y}
,
i=1,\dots,n
.

(ii)

h=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\otimes g_{i}
for some
f_{i}\in C(X)
,
g_{i}\in C(Y)
.

Note that when
\mathbb{K}
is a topological ring and (b) holds,
h
is in fact jointly continuous since sums and products of continuous functions are then continuous.

Proof.

To show that (a) implies (b), apply the proposition to
h
. The spaces
{\mathcal{V}}
and
\mathcal{H}
contains continuous functions since
h
is separately continuous.
∎

Proposition 5.5
.

Suppose
\mathbb{K}={\mathbb{R}}
or
{\mathbb{C}}
and
0\leq p\leq\infty
.
Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,T,\nu)
be probability spaces with a product space
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
so that
[P_{0}]
,
[C]
and
[\overline{C}]
hold. Let
h\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
.
Write

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{span}\{h_{x}:h_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu),\,x\in X\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{span}\{h^{y}:h^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu),\,y\in Y\}

The following statements are equivalent.

(i)

h=_{\upsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\otimes g_{i}
for some
f_{i}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
,
g_{i}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
,
i=1,\dots,n
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

(ii)

h=_{\upsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\otimes g_{i}
for some
f_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
,
g_{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
i=1,\dots,n
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

We need to show that (a) implies (b). Let
f_{i}
,
g_{i}
be as in (a) and set
k:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\otimes g_{i}
. By Remark
5.2
(i),
k\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
. By
[C]
, for some
N\in\varSigma_{0}
we have
h_{x},k_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
and
h_{x}=_{\nu}k_{x}
when
x\notin N
.
By
[\overline{C}]
, for some
M\in T_{0}
we have that
h^{y},k^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
h^{y}=_{\mu}k^{y}
when
y\notin M
.

Apply Proposition
5.3
to
k\upharpoonright S
, where
S:=(X\setminus N)\times(Y\setminus M)
, to conclude that we can write
k\upharpoonright S=\sum_{j=1}^{m}u_{i}\otimes v_{j}
where

(i)

each
v_{j}
is a linear combination of vertical sections of
k\upharpoonright S
of the form

k_{x}\upharpoonright(Y\setminus M)=_{\nu}h_{x}\upharpoonright(Y\setminus M),\ \ x\in X\setminus N

and hence
v_{j}=_{\nu}\bar{g}_{j}\upharpoonright(Y\setminus M)
for some
\bar{g}_{j}\in{\mathcal{V}}
,

(ii)

and similarly each
u_{j}=_{\mu}\bar{f}_{j}\upharpoonright(X\setminus N)
for some
\bar{f}_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
.

Then (see Remark
5.2
(ii))

h\upharpoonright S=_{\upsilon}k\upharpoonright S=\sum_{j=1}^{m}u_{i}\otimes v_{j}=_{\upsilon}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\bar{f}_{i}\otimes\bar{g}_{j})\upharpoonright S

Since
\upsilon(S)=1
and the functions involved are measurable (Remark
5.2
(i)), it follows that
h=_{\upsilon}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\bar{f}_{j}\otimes\bar{g}_{j}
.
∎

Corollary 5.6
.

Suppose
\mathbb{K}={\mathbb{R}}
or
{\mathbb{C}}
and
0\leq p\leq\infty
. Let
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
be topological probability spaces with a product space
(X\times Y,\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
so that
[P_{0}]
,
[C]
and
[\overline{C}]
hold. Let
h\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
be separately continuous.
The following statements are equivalent.

(i)

h=_{\upsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\otimes g_{i}
for some
f_{i}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
,
g_{i}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
,
i=1,\dots,n
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

(ii)

h=_{\upsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\otimes g_{i}
for some
f_{i}\in C^{p}(X)
,
g_{i}\in C^{p}(Y)
,
i=1,\dots,n
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

The next propositions give an analog for sets of the results above.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
E\subseteq X\times Y
. Write

{\mathcal{V}}=\{E_{x}:x\in X\},\ \ \mathcal{H}=\{E^{y}:y\in Y\},

the collections of all vertical and horizontal sections of
E
, respectively. Write
\mathcal{H}^{*}=\{\bigcap\mathcal{H}^{\prime}:\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{H},\,\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\ \text{\rm finite}\}
. The following statements are equivalent.

(i)

E=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\times B_{i}
for some
A_{i}\in\mathcal{H}^{*}
,
B_{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
i=1,\dots,n
,

(ii)

E=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\times B_{i}
for some
A_{i}\subseteq X
,
B_{i}\subseteq Y
,
i=1,\dots,n
,

(iii)

\mathcal{H}
and
{\mathcal{V}}
are finite.

Proof.

Clearly (a) implies (b), and (b) implies (c).

There remains to show that (c) implies (a). For this, assuming (c) it suffices to check (a) with the
A_{i}\times B_{i}
ranging over all rectangles contained in
E
with
A_{i}\in\mathcal{H}^{*}
,
B_{i}\in{\mathcal{V}}
. We verify that these rectangles cover
E
.

Consider a point
(p,q)\in E
. We have
\{p\}\times E_{p}\subseteq E
, and if
y\in E_{p}
then
p\in E^{y}\in\mathcal{H}
. Thus,
H_{0}=\bigcap\{H\in\mathcal{H}:p\in H\}\in\mathcal{H}^{*}
satisfies
H_{0}\subseteq E^{y}
, and hence
H_{0}\times\{y\}\subseteq E
, for all
y\in E_{p}
. Thus,
(p,q)\in H_{0}\times E_{p}\subseteq E
.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
X
and
Y
be topological spaces. Let
U\subseteq X\times Y
have open vertical and horizontal sections. The following statements are equivalent.

(i)

U=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\times B_{i}
for some
A_{i}\subseteq X
,
B_{i}\subseteq Y
,
i=1,\dots,n
.

(ii)

U=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\times B_{i}
for some open sets
A_{i}\subseteq X
,
B_{i}\subseteq Y
,
i=1,\dots,n
.

Note that when (b) holds,
U
is in fact open in
X\times Y
.

Proof.

Follows from the proposition since the elements of
{\mathcal{V}}
and
\mathcal{H}^{*}
are open sets.
∎

We shall use the tensor product notation of the following definition.

Definition 5.9
.

(i)

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[P_{1}]
. If
V
and
W
are linear subspaces of
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
, respectively, we denote by
V\otimes_{\upsilon}W
, or just
V\otimes W
, the linear subspace of
{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
generated by
\{(f\otimes{g})^{\bullet}:f\in V,g\in W\}
.

(ii)

For
\gamma\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)\to{{\mathbb{K}}}^{X}
and
\eta\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})\to{{\mathbb{K}}}^{Y}
, let
\gamma\otimes_{p,\upsilon}\eta
, or just
\gamma\otimes\eta
, denote the set of maps
\pi:\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)\to{{\mathbb{K}}}^{X\times Y}
such that
\pi(f\otimes{g})=\gamma(f)\otimes\eta(g)
for all
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
,
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times{Y},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[P_{1}]
.
Let
V
and
W
be linear subspaces of
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
, respectively. If
\langle f_{i}^{\bullet}\rangle_{i\in I}
is a basis for
V^{\bullet}
and
\langle g_{j}^{\bullet}\rangle_{j\in J}
a basis for
W^{\bullet}
, then
\langle(f_{i}\otimes{g}_{j})^{\bullet}\rangle_{(i,j)\in I\times J}
is a basis for the linear subspace
V\otimes W
of
{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
.

Recall that
V^{\bullet}
denotes the image of
V
in
L^{p}(\mu)
under the quotient map
f\mapsto f^{\bullet}
and similarly for the other measures.

Remark 5.11
.

(a) When
\upsilon=\mu\otimes\nu
,
V=\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
,
W=\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
, and
p\geq 1
, the lemma describes the standard realization of a tensor product of the Banach spaces
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
.

(b) Lemma
5.10
shows that the bilinear map
V^{\bullet}\times W^{\bullet}\to V\otimes W
given by
(f^{\bullet},g^{\bullet})\mapsto(f\otimes g)^{\bullet}
is a tensor product of
V^{\bullet}
and
W^{\bullet}
. (Cf.
[
36
]
, pages 403–404. The map
(f^{\bullet},g^{\bullet})\mapsto(f\otimes g)^{\bullet}
is well-defined by Remark
5.2
(ii) and
[P_{1}]
and its bilinearity is clear.)

Proof.

We wish to show that
\langle(f_{i}\otimes{g}_{j})^{\bullet}\rangle_{(i,j)\in I\times J}
is a basis for
V\otimes W
.

For linear independence, suppose
\sum_{i,j}c_{i,j}(f_{i}\otimes{g}_{j})^{\bullet}=0^{\bullet}
, where
c_{i,j}\in\mathbb{K}
,
\langle c_{i,j}\rangle
of finite support.
The function
h:=\sum_{i,j}c_{i,j}(f_{i}\otimes g_{j})
is
\varSigma\otimes T
-measurable. Therefore,
M:=\{h\not=0\}\in\varSigma\otimes T
and by
[P_{1}]
,
\mu\otimes\nu(M)=\upsilon(M)=0
. By the Fubini theorem there exists a set
Q\in T_{0}
, such that
M^{y}\in\varSigma_{0}
for every
y\notin Q
.
When
y\notin Q
, set
a_{i}(y):=\sum_{j}c_{i,j}g_{j}(y)
. We have for
x\notin M^{y}\in\varSigma_{0}
,
\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)\cdot a_{i}(y)=0
implying
\sum_{i}a_{i}(y)f_{i}^{\bullet}=0^{\bullet}
.
Since
\langle f_{i}^{\bullet}\rangle
is a basis for
V^{\bullet}
we get
a_{i}(y)=0
for each
i
and
y\notin Q
, giving
\sum_{j}c_{i,j}g_{j}^{\bullet}=0^{\bullet}
for every
i
implying

c_{i,j}=0
for every
(i,j)
, since
\langle g_{j}^{\bullet}\rangle
is a basis for
W^{\bullet}
. This shows that
\langle(f_{i}\otimes{g}_{j})^{\bullet}\rangle
is linear independent.

To see that
\langle(f_{i}\otimes{g}_{j})^{\bullet}\rangle
generates
V\otimes W
, given
f\in V
and
g\in W
, write
f^{\bullet}=\sum_{i}a_{i}f_{i}^{\bullet}
, where
a_{i}\in{{\mathbb{K}}}
,
\langle a_{i}\rangle
of finite support, and write
g^{\bullet}=\sum_{j}b_{j}g_{j}^{\bullet}
, where
b_{i}\in{{\mathbb{K}}}
,
\langle b_{i}\rangle
of finite support. There exists sets
N\in\varSigma_{0}
,
P\in T_{0}
so that
f(x)=\sum_{i}a_{i}f_{i}(x)
when
x\notin N
, and
g(y)=\sum_{j}b_{j}g_{j}(y)
when
y\notin P
. Then

(f\otimes{g})(x,y)=f(x)g(y)=\sum_{i,j}a_{i}b_{j}f_{i}(x)g_{j}(y)=\sum_{i,j}a_{i}b_{j}(f_{i}\otimes{g}_{j})(x,y)

when
(x,y)\notin(N\times Y)\cup(X\times P)
and this set belongs to
\Upsilon_{0}
by
[P_{1}]
, so

(f\otimes{g})^{\bullet}=\sum_{i,j}a_{i}b_{j}(f_{i}\otimes{g}_{j})^{\bullet}
. Hence,

\langle(f_{i}\otimes{g}_{j})^{\bullet}\rangle
is a basis for the linear subspace
V\otimes{W}
.
∎

The next proposition is covered by
[
26
]
, Remark 2.9, when
p=\infty
, when
\mu
and
\nu
are complete and
\upsilon
is their completed product. Our proof adapts the proof given there to our present context. The proposition also follows directly from the upcoming Theorem
5.15
by taking
\mathfrak{S}=\{\emptyset,X\}
and
\mathfrak{T}=\{\emptyset,Y\}
. We present it anyway because its proof presents the idea for the proof of Theorem
5.15
in a simper setting.

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times{Y},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[P_{0}]
and
[C]
.
For every

\gamma\in V^{p}(\mu)
and
\eta\in V^{p}(\nu)
there exists a
\pi\in V^{p}(\upsilon)\cap(\gamma\otimes\eta)
satisfying
[\pi(f)]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for every
x\in X
.
If
[\overline{C}]
also holds, then we may require also that
[\pi(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
for every
y\in Y
.

Proof.

Choose linear bases

\langle f_{i}^{\bullet}\rangle_{i\in I}
for
L^{p}(\mu)
and

\langle g_{j}^{\bullet}\rangle_{j\in J}
for
L^{p}(\nu)
so that
\gamma(f_{i})=f_{i}
and
\eta(g_{j})=g_{j}
for all
i
and
j
. (See Proposition
4.3
.) By Lemma
5.10
the subspace

\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)\otimes\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
of
\mathcal{L}(\upsilon)

has the basis
\langle(f_{i}\otimes{g}_{j})^{\bullet}\rangle_{(i,j)\in I\times J}
.

Extend this to a basis
\langle h_{k}^{\bullet}\rangle_{k\in K}
for
L^{p}(\upsilon)
with
I\times J\subseteq K
and
h_{i,j}=f_{i}\otimes{g}_{j}
for
(i,j)\in I\times J
, where for
k\in K\setminus I\times J
by Corollary
2.21
we may choose
h_{k}
in such a way, that

[h_{k}]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for every
x\in X
. If
[\overline{C}]
holds we may also arrange

[h_{k}]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
for every
y\in Y
. For all
h_{i,j}=f_{i}\otimes g_{j}
,
(i,j)\in I\times J
, these section properties holds automatically, since
[f_{i}\otimes g_{j}]_{x}=f_{i}(x)g_{j}
and
[f_{i}\otimes g_{j}]^{y}=g_{j}(y)f_{i}
.

Let
\pi
be the vector lifting for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
such that
\pi(h_{k})=h_{k}
for all
k\in K
.
By Example
4.9
(b,c), for
h\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
we have
[\pi(h)]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for
x\in X
, and if
[\overline{C}]
holds,
[\pi(h)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
for
y\in Y
.

Claim 5.13
.

\pi(f\otimes{g})=\gamma(f)\otimes\eta(g)
for every
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
.

Proof.

Write
f^{\bullet}=\sum_{i}a_{i}f_{i}^{\bullet}
,
a_{i}\in{{\mathbb{K}}}
,
\langle a_{i}\rangle
of finite support, and write
g^{\bullet}=\sum_{j}b_{j}g_{j}^{\bullet}
,
b_{j}\in{{\mathbb{K}}}
,
\langle b_{j}\rangle
of finite support. Since

(f\otimes{g})^{\bullet}=\sum_{i,j}a_{i}b_{j}(f_{i}\otimes g_{j})^{\bullet}
for
(x,y)\in X\times Y
, we get

\displaystyle\textstyle\pi(f\otimes{g})(x,y)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\sum_{i,j}a_{i}b_{j}(f_{i}\otimes g_{j})(x,y)=\left(\sum_{i\phantom{j}}\!\!a_{i}f_{i}(x)\right)\left(\sum_{j}b_{j}g_{j}(y)\right)

\displaystyle=\gamma(f)(x)\cdot\eta(g)(y)=(\gamma(f)\otimes\eta(g))(x,y),

i.e.,
\pi(f\otimes g)=\gamma(f)\otimes\eta(g)
.
∎

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
be topological probability spaces satisfying
(N_{C,\mu})
and

(N_{C,\nu})
, respectively. For every topological probability space

(X\times Y,\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
satisfying
[C]
, we have
(N_{C,\upsilon})
.

Proof.

From
h\in C(X\times Y)
, it follows that
h_{x}\in C(Y)
for every
x\in X
and
h^{y}\in C(X)
for every
y\in Y
. If
h=_{\upsilon}0
then by
[C]
there is an
M\in\varSigma_{0}
with
h_{x}=_{\nu}0
for
x\notin M
. Then for all
x\notin M
,
h_{x}=0
by
(N_{C,\nu})
. Thus,
h^{y}=_{\mu}0
for all
y\in Y
and hence
h^{y}=0
for all
y\in Y
by
(N_{C,\mu})
. This gives
h=0
, so
(N_{C,\upsilon})
is satisfied.
∎

Theorem 5.15
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
and
(X\times{Y},\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be topological probability spaces satisfying
[P_{0}]
and
[C]
. Assume that
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
satisfy
(N_{C,\mu})
and
(N_{C,\nu})
, respectively. Then

(X\times Y,\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
satisfies
(N_{C,\upsilon})
, and for every

\gamma\in V^{p}(\mathfrak{S},\mu)
and
\eta\in V^{p}(\mathfrak{T},\nu)
there exists a

\pi\in(\gamma\otimes\eta)\cap V^{p}(\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\upsilon)
so that for
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
we have
[\pi(f)]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for all
x\in X
. If
[\overline{C}]
also holds, then we may require also that
[\pi(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
for every
y\in Y
.

Proof.

(X\times Y,\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
satisfies
(N_{C,\upsilon})
by Lemma
5.14
.

Note that
V^{p}(\mathfrak{S},\mu),V^{p}(\mathfrak{T},\nu)\not=\emptyset
by Proposition
4.13
. By the assumptions
(N_{C,\mu})
and
(N_{C,\nu})
,
C^{p}(X)
and
C^{p}(Y)
are linearly isomorphic to their images in
L^{p}(\mu)
and
L^{p}(\nu)
, respectively. Choose a basis
\langle f_{i}\rangle_{i\in K}
for
C^{p}(X)
and a basis
\langle g_{j}\rangle_{j\in L}
for
C^{p}(Y)
. Then
\gamma(f_{i})=f_{i}
and
\eta(g_{j})=g_{j}
for each
i\in K
,
j\in L
since
\gamma
and
\eta
are strong.

Extend
\langle f_{i}^{\bullet}\rangle_{i\in K}
to a basis
\langle f_{i}^{\bullet}\rangle_{i\in I}
for
L^{p}(\mu)
,
K\subseteq I
, and extend
\langle g_{j}^{\bullet}\rangle_{j\in L}
to a basis
\langle g_{j}^{\bullet}\rangle_{j\in J}
for
L^{p}(\nu)
,
L\subseteq J
, choosing the representative functions so that
\gamma(f_{i})=f_{i}
and
\eta(g_{j})=g_{j}
also for
i\in I\setminus K
,
j\in J\setminus L
.

By Lemma
5.10
,
\langle(f_{k}\otimes{g}_{l})^{\bullet}\rangle_{(k,l)\in K\times L}
is a linear basis for
{C}^{p}(X)\otimes C^{p}(Y)
.
Let
C:=C^{p}(X\times Y)^{\bullet}
. This is a linear subspace of
L^{p}(\upsilon)
which by
(N_{C,\upsilon})
is linearly isomorphic to
C^{p}(X\times Y)
(the isomorphism being
h^{\bullet}\mapsto h
for each
h\in C^{p}(X\times Y)
).

Claim 5.16
.

C\cap(\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)\otimes\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu))=C^{p}(X)\otimes C^{p}(Y)
.

Proof.

The inclusion from right to left is clear. For the other direction, suppose
h\in C^{p}(X\times Y)
and for some functions
f_{i}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(X)
and
g_{i}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(Y)
,
i=1,\dots,n
, we have
h=_{\upsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\otimes g_{i}
. By Corollary
5.6
, we can take
f_{i}\in C^{p}(X)
,
g_{i}\in C^{p}(Y)
, so
h^{\bullet}\in C^{p}(X)\otimes C^{p}(Y)
.
∎

Extend
\langle(f_{k}\otimes g_{l})^{\bullet}\rangle_{(k,l)\in K\times L}
to a basis
\langle h_{r}^{\bullet}\rangle_{r\in R}
for
C
with
K\times L\subseteq R
, each
h_{r}\in C^{p}(X\times Y)
, and
h_{k,l}=f_{k}\otimes{g}_{l}
for every
(k,l)\in{K}\times{L}
. Then
[h_{r}]_{x}\in C^{p}(Y)\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for
x\in X
and
[h_{r}]^{y}\in C^{p}(X)\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
for
y\in Y
. Also
\langle h_{r}\rangle_{r\in R}
is a basis for
C^{p}(X\times Y)
since the latter is isomorphic to
C
.

Again by Lemma
5.10
it follows that
\langle(f_{i}\otimes{g}_{j})^{\bullet}\rangle_{(i,j)\in{I}\times{J}}
is a basis for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)\otimes\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
.
Write
V
for the linear subspace of
L^{p}(\upsilon)
generated by
C
and
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)\otimes\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
. By Claim
5.16
,
V
has the basis
\langle(f_{i}\otimes{g}_{j})^{\bullet}\rangle_{(i,j)\in I\times{J}}\cup\langle h_{r}^{\bullet}\rangle_{r\in R}
.
Extend this to a basis
\langle h_{r}^{\bullet}\rangle_{r\in S}
for
L^{p}(\upsilon)
with
(I\times J)\cup R\subseteq S
, where
h_{i,j}^{\bullet}=(f_{i}\otimes{g}_{j})^{\bullet}
for
(i,j)\in I\times J
. By Corollary
2.21
, we can choose the new representatives
h_{r}
for
r\in S\setminus((I\times{J})\cup R)
to satisfy
[h_{r}]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for
x\in X
. If
[\overline{C}]
holds we may also arrange

[h_{r}]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
for every
y\in Y
. Together with our earlier choices, we get that these section properties now hold for every
r\in S
.

Let
\pi
be the unique vector lifting for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
such that
\pi(h_{r})=h_{r}
for each
r\in S
. By Example
4.9
(b,c), for
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
we have
[\pi(f)]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for
x\in X
, and if
[\overline{C}]
holds,
[\pi(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
for
y\in Y
.
By Example
4.9
(d),
\pi\in V(\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\upsilon)
since by construction
\pi
fixes each basis element of
C^{p}(X\times Y)
.
As in the proof of Claim
5.13
, we have
\pi(f\otimes{g})=\gamma(f)\otimes\eta(g)
for every
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
. This completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Corollary 5.17
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
be
\tau
-additive topological probability spaces with
\nu
complete, satisfying
(N_{C,\mu})
and
(N_{C,\nu})
, respectively. Their
\tau
-additive product

(X\times Y,\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
satisfies
(N_{C,\upsilon})
, and for every

\gamma\in V^{p}(\mathfrak{S},\mu)
and
\eta\in V^{p}(\mathfrak{T},\nu)
there exists a

\pi\in(\gamma\otimes\eta)\cap V^{p}(\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\upsilon)
for every
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
satisfying

[\pi(f)]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for
x\in X
. If
\mu
is also complete, then we can require also that
[\pi(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for
y\in Y
.

Proof.

The properties
[P_{0}]
and
[C]
(and
[\overline{C}]
if
\mu
is complete) for the spaces
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
and
(X\times{Y},\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
are consequences of
[
4
]
, Theorem 2.15, and so the desired result is immediate by Theorem
5.15

∎

Example 5.18
.

Let
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
be the hyperstonian space
(see
[
13
]
, 321K, where it is called the Stone space) of a probability space, and let
\sigma
be the canonical strong lifting for
\nu
which selects the unique clopen set from each measure class. By Proposition
4.13
the space
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
satisfies the property
(N_{C,\nu})
, and so by Corollary
5.17
the
\tau
-additive product
(Y\times{Y},\mathfrak{T}\times\mathfrak{T},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
of
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
by itself satisfies
(N_{C,\upsilon})
, and there exists a
\pi\in(\sigma\otimes\sigma)\cap{V}^{\infty}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathfrak{T}\times\mathfrak{T},\upsilon)
for every
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\upsilon)
satisfying
[\pi(f)]_{x},[\pi(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\nu)
for all
x,y\in{Y}
. Could
\pi
be converted into a strong (linear) lifting on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\upsilon)
?

Question 5.19
.

Given a family of probability spaces
(X_{i},\varSigma_{i},\mu_{i})
,
i\in I
, with product
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
, is there always a vector lifting for
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mu)
which respects coordinates in the sense of
[
13
]
346A?

6.
Marginals

Extending Definitions 4.2 and 4.4 of
[
5
]
(cf. Definitions 3.1 and 3.2 of
[
27
]
) in a natural way, we make the following definitions.

Definition 6.1
.

Fix sets
X
and
Y
. Also fix a map
\delta\colon U\to\mathcal{P}(Y)
, where
U\subseteq\mathcal{P}(Y)
, as well as a family of maps
\eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in X}
, where for each
x\in X
,
\eta_{x}:A_{x}\to{\mathbb{K}}^{Y}
for some
A_{x}\subseteq{\mathbb{K}}^{Y}
.

(a) Define
\delta_{\bullet}(E)\subseteq X\times Y
for
E\subseteq X\times Y
by

[\delta_{\bullet}(E)]_{x}=\delta(E_{x})\ \text{if $E_{x}\in U$},\ \text{and}\ [\delta_{\bullet}(E)]_{x}=\emptyset\ \text{otherwise}.

We call
\delta
a
2
-marginal
with respect to a family
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X\times Y)
, if
\delta_{\bullet}(E)\in\mathcal{E}
for every
E\in\mathcal{E}
.

Interchanging the roles of the two coordinates, we similarly define
\eta^{\bullet}(E)\subseteq X\times Y
for
\eta:V\to\mathcal{P}(X)
, where
V\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)
, by setting
[\eta^{\bullet}(E)]^{y}=\eta(E^{y})
when
E^{y}\in V
,
[\eta^{\bullet}(E)]^{y}=\emptyset
otherwise. Call
\eta
a
1
-marginal
with respect to
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X\times Y)
if
\delta^{\bullet}(E)\in\mathcal{E}
for every
E\in\mathcal{E}
.

(b) Define
\eta_{\bullet}(f):X\times{Y}\to{\mathbb{K}}
for
f:X\times{Y}\to{\mathbb{K}}
by

\bigl(\eta_{\bullet}(f)\bigr)(x,y):=\eta_{x}(f_{x})(y),\;\mbox{if}\;f_{x}\in A_{x}\;\mbox{and}\;\;\bigl(\eta_{\bullet}(f)\bigr)(x,y):=0\;\mbox{otherwise}.

We call
\eta
a
2
-marginal
with respect to a family
\mathcal{F}\subseteq{\mathbb{K}}^{X\times Y}
, if
\eta_{\bullet}(f)\in\mathcal{F}
for each
f\in\mathcal{F}
.

(c) Similarly, given a single map
\eta:A\to{\mathbb{K}}^{Y}
, where
A\subseteq{\mathbb{K}}^{Y}
, define
\eta_{\bullet}(f)
for
f:X\times{Y}\to{\mathbb{K}}
by

\bigl(\eta_{\bullet}(f)\bigr)(x,y):=\eta(f_{x})(y),\;\mbox{if}\;f_{x}\in A\;\mbox{and}\;\;\bigl(\eta_{\bullet}(f)\bigr)(x,y):=0\;\mbox{otherwise}.

In this latter setting, interchanging the roles of the two coordinates, we similarly define
\theta^{\bullet}(f):X\times{Y}\to{\mathbb{K}}
for
\theta:B\to{\mathbb{K}}^{X}
, where
B\subseteq{\mathbb{K}}^{X}
, by setting
\theta^{\bullet}(f)(x,y)=\theta(f^{y})(x)
when
f^{y}\in B
,
=0
otherwise. We call
\theta
a
1
-marginal
with respect to
\mathcal{F}\subseteq{\mathbb{K}}^{X\times Y}
if
\theta^{\bullet}(f)\in\mathcal{F}
for each
f\in\mathcal{F}
.

The next proposition is a stronger version of
[
5
]
, Remark 4.6 (e).

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
p\in[0,\infty]
. Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
, and
(X\times{Y},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[C]
and
\varSigma\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
. Let
T=(T_{x})_{x\in X}
,
\nu=(\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
. Define the four families

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon,\nu):=\{f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon):f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})\;\;\mbox{for all}\;\;x\in{X}\}

\displaystyle A_{3}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{L}^{0,p}_{\mu}(\Upsilon,\nu):=\{f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon):\text{for some}\ N\in\varSigma_{0},\ x\in N^{c}\ \text{implies}\ f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})\}

\displaystyle A_{4}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{L}^{0,p}(\Upsilon,\nu):=\{f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon):f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})\;\;\mbox{for all}\;\;x\in{X}\}

We also write
A_{3}
,
A_{4}
as
\mathcal{L}^{0,p}_{\mu}(\upsilon,\nu)
,
\mathcal{L}^{0,p}(\upsilon,\nu)
, respectively.
Let
\rho=(\rho_{x})_{x\in X}
, with
\rho_{x}\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
satisfying
(l1)
and
(l2)
. Suppose that one of the following completeness assumptions holds.

(\alpha)

(\varSigma_{0}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon
.

(\beta)

\rho_{x}(0)=0
for
x\in X
, and
\mu
is complete.

Then all implications among the conditions
\rho_{\bullet}(A_{i})\subseteq A_{j}
,
i,j=1,2,3,4
, hold, omitting, when
p\not=0
, those implications involving a condition where
i\in\{3,4\}
and
j\in\{1,2\}
.

Remark 6.3
.

(a) The omitted conditions in the last sentence all say that applying
\rho_{\bullet}
to a function with
\mathcal{L}^{p}
sections, will create an
\mathcal{L}^{p}
function on the product, which of course will not hold in general when
p\not=0
.

(b) The conclusion gives in particular that the conditions “
\rho
is a
2
-marginal with respect to
A_{i}
”,
i=1,2,3,4
, are all equivalent.

(c) If
(\alpha)
holds, then for any
N\in\varSigma_{0}
, the preimage of a Borel set under a function
\rho_{\bullet}(f)
intersects
N\times Y
in a member of
\Upsilon
since each
[\rho_{\bullet}(f)]_{x}
is either
0
or
\rho_{x}(f_{x})
and so is
T_{x}
-measurable.

Proof.

Write
H_{ij}
for the statement
\rho_{\bullet}(A_{i})\subseteq A_{j}
.

Since
A_{2}\subseteq A_{1}\subseteq A_{3}
(the second by
[C]
) and
A_{2}\subseteq A_{4}\subseteq A_{3}
, in a hypothesis
H_{ij}:\rho_{\bullet}(A_{i})\subseteq A_{j}
, we need consider only the case
i=2
,
j=3
, since that only weakens the hypothesis and this is not among the omitted conditions. When
p=0
, in a conclusion
H_{ij}
we similarly need consider only the case
i=3
,
j=2
since that only strengthens the conclusion. When
0<p\leq\infty
, avoiding the omitted conditions, we see that we need only consider in the conclusion the cases
(i,j)\in\{(1,2),(3,4)\}
.
Thus, we need only consider the following cases:

•

when
p=0
,
H_{23}\Rightarrow H_{32}
;

•

when
p\not=0
,
H_{23}\Rightarrow H_{12}
and
H_{23}\Rightarrow H_{34}
.

Observe that any function of the form
\rho_{\bullet}(f)
belongs to
A_{4}
as long as it belongs to
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon)
since for all
x\in X
,
[\rho_{\bullet}(f)]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
by definition of
\rho_{\bullet}
and by our assumption that
\rho_{x}
maps into
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
.
In particular,
\rho_{\bullet}(f)\in A_{3}
if and only if
\rho_{\bullet}(f)\in A_{4}
, and similarly
\rho_{\bullet}(f)\in A_{1}
if and only if
\rho_{\bullet}(f)\in A_{2}
.
Taking these into account, we see that we need only consider the following cases:

•

when
p=0
,
H_{24}\Rightarrow H_{31}
;

•

when
p\not=0
,
H_{24}\Rightarrow H_{11}
and
H_{24}\Rightarrow H_{33}
.

Case 1.
p=0
.

We need to show
H_{24}\Rightarrow H_{31}
.
Let
f\in A_{3}
, with
N
as in the definition. Note that when
p=0
,
A_{4}\subseteq A_{1}
.
Define
g\upharpoonright N^{c}\times Y=f\upharpoonright N^{c}\times Y
,
g\upharpoonright N\times Y\equiv 0
.
Since
\varSigma_{0}\dot{\times}Y\subseteq\Upsilon
,
g
is
\Upsilon
-measurable, and by the choice of
N
, we have
g\in A_{2}
. Thus, by
H_{24}
,
\rho_{\bullet}(g)\in A_{4}
.
We have
\rho_{\bullet}(f)\upharpoonright N^{c}\times Y=\rho_{\bullet}(g)\upharpoonright N^{c}\times Y
, so
\rho_{\bullet}(f)
is
\Upsilon
-measurable on
N^{c}\times Y
.
If
(\alpha)
holds, then it follows immediately that
\rho_{\bullet}(f)
is
\Upsilon
-measurable (see Remark
6.3
(c)) and therefore
\rho_{\bullet}(f)\in A_{1}
.
If
(\beta)
holds, then we can take
N=\{x\in X:f_{x}\notin\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)\}
and using
\rho_{x}(0)=0
for
x\in X
we get
\rho_{\bullet}(f)=\rho_{\bullet}(g)\in A_{4}\subseteq A_{1}
.

Case 2.
p=\infty
.

H_{24}\Rightarrow H_{11}
: Let
f\in A_{1}
.
Write
M:=\|f\|_{\infty}
. By
[C]
, there is an
N\in\varSigma_{0}
such that for
x\in N^{c}
,
\|f_{x}\|_{\infty}\leq M
. Define
g
as in Case 1. We get
g\in A_{2}
, so
\rho_{\bullet}(g)\in A_{3}
. As in Case 1, it follows that
\rho_{\bullet}(f)
is
\Upsilon
-measurable. Since each
\rho_{x}
satisfies
(l1)
, we have
\|\rho_{x}(f_{x})\|_{\infty}\leq M
for
x\in N^{c}
. By
[C]
and since
N\times Y\in\Upsilon_{0}
, it follows that
\|\rho_{\bullet}(f)\|_{\infty}\leq M
, so
\rho_{\bullet}(f)\in A_{1}
.

H_{24}\Rightarrow H_{33}
: Let
f\in A_{3}
, with
N
as in the definition.
Define
g
as in the argument for Case 1, getting that
g
is
\Upsilon
-measurable and
g_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\nu_{x})
for all
x\in X
.
For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, write
g_{n}=(-n)\vee(g\wedge n)
. Then
g_{n}\in A_{2}
, so
\rho_{\bullet}(g_{n})\in A_{4}
. Since for each
x
,
[g_{n}]_{x}=_{\nu_{x}}g_{x}
if
n
is large enough, and
\rho_{x}
satisfies
(l2)
, for each
x
we have
[\rho_{\bullet}(g)]_{x}=[\rho_{\bullet}(g_{n})]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\nu_{x})
when
n
is large enough. It follows that
\rho_{\bullet}(g)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\rho_{\bullet}(g_{n})
is
\Upsilon
-measurable. Then, as in Case 1, it follows that
\rho_{\bullet}(f)
is
\Upsilon
-measurable. By our observation, it then follows that
\rho_{\bullet}(f)\in A_{4}\subseteq A_{3}
.

Case 3.
0<p<\infty
.

H_{24}\Rightarrow H_{11}
: Let
f\in A_{1}
. By
[C]
, there is an
N\in\varSigma_{0}
such that for
x\in N^{c}
,
f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
.
Define
g
as in Case 1. We get
g\in A_{2}
, so
\rho_{\bullet}(g)\in A_{4}
. As in Case 1, it follows that
\rho_{\bullet}(f)
is
\Upsilon
-measurable. Since each
\rho_{x}
satisfies
(l1)
, we have
\rho_{x}(f_{x})=_{\nu_{x}}f_{x}
for
x\in N^{c}
. By
[C]
, it follows that
\rho_{\bullet}(f)\in A_{1}
.

H_{24}\Rightarrow H_{33}
: Let
f\in A_{3}
, with
N_{0}\in\varSigma_{0}
as in the definition, i.e.,
x\in N_{0}^{c}
implies
f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
.
By
[C]
, specifically Proposition
2.19
(i) applied to
h=|f|^{p}
, we can enlarge
N_{0}
to
N\in\varSigma_{0}
to get that on
N^{c}
, the function
x\mapsto\int|f_{x}|^{p}\,d\nu_{x}
is
\varSigma
-measurable.

Under
(\beta)
, we take specifically
N_{0}=\{x\in X:f_{x}\notin\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})\}
. We do not need to enlarge
N_{0}
since measurability on
N^{c}
implies measurability on
N_{0}^{c}
by completeness of
\mu
. So under
(\beta)
, we take
N=N_{0}=\{x\in X:f_{x}\notin\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})\}
.

The function
x\mapsto\int|f_{x}|^{p}\,d\nu_{x}
is finite-valued on
N^{c}
by the choice of
N
.
For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
E_{n}=\{x\in N^{c}:\int|f_{x}|^{p}\,d\nu_{x}\leq n\}\in\varSigma
and define
g_{n}(x,y)=f(x,y)
when
x\in E_{n}
,
g_{n}(x,y)=0
when
x\notin E_{n}
. Since
E_{n}\times Y\in\Upsilon
,
g_{n}
is
\Upsilon
-measurable. By
[C]
applied to
|g_{n}|^{p}
, we have
g_{n}\in A_{1}
and therefore
g_{n}\in A_{2}
. By
H_{24}
, we then get
\rho_{\bullet}(g_{n})\in A_{4}
. For
x\in E_{n}
, we have
[g_{n}]_{x}=f_{x}
and therefore
[\rho_{\bullet}(g_{n})]_{x}=[\rho_{\bullet}(f)]_{x}
. The set
N^{c}
is the union of the increasing sequence of the
E_{n}
. We therefore have
\rho_{\bullet}(f)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\rho_{\bullet}(g_{n})
on
N^{c}\times Y
, giving that
\rho_{\bullet}(f)
is
\Upsilon
-measurable on
N^{c}\times Y
. As in Case 1, it then follows that
\rho_{\bullet}(f)
is
\Upsilon
-measurable and hence belongs to
A_{4}\subseteq A_{3}
.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[P_{2}]
and
[C]
. Let
(Y,T,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
with
\mathcal{I}\cap T=T_{0}
. Then for every

f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon_{\varSigma_{0}\ltimes\mathcal{I}})
there exists a
g\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu\otimes\nu)
such that
f=_{\varSigma_{0}\ltimes\mathcal{I}}g
.

Proof.

Property
[C]
continues to hold if we extend
\nu
to make the triple
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T_{\mathcal{I}},\nu_{\mathcal{I}})
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
. Then by Proposition
3.28
(applied with
\varSigma_{0}
and
(Y,T_{\mathcal{I}},\nu_{\mathcal{I}})
in the place of
\mathcal{I}
and
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
),
\upsilon_{\varSigma_{0}\ltimes\mathcal{I}}
is defined.
By
[P_{2}]
and Corollary
3.11
,
\upsilon=\mu\otimes_{\Upsilon_{0}}\nu
.

Apply Lemma
3.1
(ii) twice, first getting a
\Upsilon
-measurable function
g_{0}\colon X\times Y\to{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying
f=_{\varSigma_{0}\ltimes\mathcal{I}}g_{0}
and then
getting a
\varSigma\otimes T
-measurable function
g\colon X\times Y\to{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying
g_{0}=_{\Upsilon_{0}}g
.
Noting that
\Upsilon_{0}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}\ltimes T_{0}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}\ltimes\mathcal{I}
, we get
f=_{\varSigma_{0}\ltimes\mathcal{I}}g
.
∎

Corollary 6.5
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[P_{2}]
and
[C]
. For every

f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}})
there exists a
g\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu\otimes\nu)
with
f=_{\mathfrak{N}}g
.

Proof.

Apply the lemma with
\mathcal{I}
being the completion of
T_{0}
.
∎

Corollary 6.6
.

Denote by
(X\times Y,\mathfrak{S}\times{\mathfrak{T}},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
the
\tau
-additive product of the
\tau
-additive topological probability spaces
(X,{\mathfrak{S}},\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
. For every

f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}})
there exists a
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu\otimes\nu)
with
f=_{\mathfrak{N}}g
.

Proof.

By
[
4
]
, Theorem 2.15 all assumptions of Corollary
6.5
are satisfied and so we may apply it to get the desired result.
∎

The next lemma is analogous to the fact that on complete measure spaces, functions equal almost everywhere to a measurable function are measurable. It provides a useful setting in which this works without completeness assumptions.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
X
be a set,
\mathcal{I}
an upwards directed family of subsets of
X
. Let

(Y,T_{x})_{x\in X}
be measurable spaces and for each
x\in X
, let
J_{x}
be an ideal of
T_{x}
. Write
T=(T_{x})_{x\in X}
,
J=(J_{x})_{x\in X}
.
Let
\Upsilon
be a
\sigma
-algebra on
X\times Y
such that
\Upsilon\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
.
Let
\mathcal{K}:=\mathcal{I}\ltimes J
.

(i)

For each
f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
, there is an
N\in\mathcal{I}
so that
f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(T_{x})
when
x\notin N
.

(ii)

If
h\colon X\times Y\to\mathbb{K}
satisfies that for some
N\in\mathcal{I}
and for all
x\in N^{c}
we have
h_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(T_{x})
and
h_{x}=_{J_{x}}0
then
h\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
and
h=_{\mathcal{K}}0
.

(iii)

If
f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
and
g\colon X\times Y\to\mathbb{K}
satisfies that for some
N\in\mathcal{I}
and for all
x\in N
,
f_{x}
,
g_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(T_{x})
, and
g_{x}=_{J_{x}}f_{x}
, then
g\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
and
g=_{\mathcal{K}}f
.

Proof.

Since
\Upsilon\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
and
\mathcal{K}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
, we have
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\mathcal{K})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
and thus
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}
is defined.

(i) From
\Upsilon\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
and
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
we get
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
. Apply Proposition
2.16
(i).

(ii) We may assume that
h
is real-valued. We have
\{h<r\}\in\mathcal{K}
when
r\leq 0
since
[\{h<r\}]_{x}=\{h_{x}<r\}\in J_{x}
for
x\in N^{c}
. When
r>0
,
\{h<r\}
is the complement of
\{h\geq r\}
which similarly belongs to
\mathcal{K}
.
Thus,
h\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
.
For
h=_{\mathcal{K}}0
, we are given
\{h_{x}\not=0\}\in J_{x}
when
x\in N^{c}
, so
\{h\not=0\}\in\mathcal{K}
and hence
h=_{\mathcal{K}}0
.

(iii) The function
h=g-f
satisfies the assumptions of (ii), so
h\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
and hence
g=f+h\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
, and since
h=_{\mathcal{K}}0
we get
g=_{\mathcal{K}}f
.
∎

It has been observed already in
[
27
]
, Theorem 5.1, that given a complete probability space
(Y,T,\nu)
not all liftings are
1
-marginals or
2
-marginals with respect the completed product
T\,\widehat{\otimes}\,T
of
T
by itself.
In addition, under the assumption of CH, it has been shown in
[
5
]
, Example 6.20, that there are complete topological probability spaces
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
and a lifting on the second space which is not 2-marginal with respect to the Radon product
\mu\otimes_{R}\nu
or even with respect to the product
\mu\otimes_{\mathfrak{N}_{2}}\nu
.
In the case of vector liftings, we have the same phenomenon that this does not happen if
\mathfrak{N}_{2}
is replaced by
\mathfrak{N}
, as shown in Examples
6.11
and
6.12
after the following lemmas.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
X
be a set,
\mathcal{I}
an upwards directed family of subsets of
X
. Let

(Y,T_{x})_{x\in X}
be measurable spaces and for each
x\in X
, let
J_{x}
be an ideal of
T_{x}
. Write
T=(T_{x})_{x\in X}
,
J=(J_{x})_{x\in X}
.
Let
\Upsilon
be a
\sigma
-algebra on
X\times Y
such that
\Upsilon\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
.
Let
\mathcal{K}:=\mathcal{I}\ltimes J
.
If
\eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in X}
, where each
\eta_{x}\colon\mathcal{L}^{0}(T_{x})\to\mathcal{L}^{0}(T_{x})
satisfies
(l1)
, then
\eta_{\bullet}
satisfies
(l1)
on
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
and hence
\eta
is a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
.

Proof.

Use Lemma
6.7
. By (i) of that lemma, for each
f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
, the function
g=\eta_{\bullet}(f)
satisfies the assumptions of (iii) of that lemma.
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})
for
x\in X
, and
(X\times{Y},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces. Write
T=(T_{x})_{x\in X}
,
T_{0}=(T_{x,0}:x\in X)
, where
T_{x,0}
is the null ideal of
\nu_{x}
, and
\nu=(\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
. Let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}
be a
\sigma
-ideal of
\varSigma
,
\mathcal{K}:=\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{0}
. Suppose that
\Upsilon\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
and
\mathcal{K}\cap\Upsilon\subseteq\Upsilon_{0}
.

Assume that
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
holds and
\varSigma_{0}\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})_{0}
. If
\eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in X}
, where each
\eta_{x}\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
satisfies
(l1)
, then

\eta_{\bullet}
satisfies
(l1)
on
\mathcal{L}^{0,p}_{\mu}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}},\nu)
.

Remark 6.10
.

(a) Since
\Upsilon\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
and
\mathcal{K}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
, we have
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\mathcal{K})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
. Since we also have
\mathcal{K}\cap\Upsilon\subseteq\Upsilon_{0}
,
\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}
is defined.

(b)
\eta
is a
2
-marginal with respect to both
\mathcal{L}^{0,p}_{\mu}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}},\nu)
and the smaller family
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
.

(c) When
\mathcal{I}=\varSigma_{0}
,

\varSigma_{0}\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})_{0}
holds automatically since
\varSigma_{0}\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{0}=\mathcal{K}\subseteq(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})_{0}
.

Proof.

Case 1.
p=0
. This case follows from Lemma
6.8
since
\mathcal{L}^{0,0}_{\mu}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}},\nu)\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
.

Case 2.
0<p<\infty
. Let
f\in\mathcal{L}^{0,p}_{\mu}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}},\nu)
. By Proposition
3.28
,
[C]_{\mathcal{I}}
holds for
\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}
. By Proposition
2.16
(i) and Proposition
2.19
(i), there is an
N\in\mathcal{I}
such that
f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu_{x})
for
x\in N^{c}
, and
x\mapsto\int|f_{x}|^{p}\,d\nu_{x}
is
\varSigma
-measurable on
N^{c}
. Since
f\in\mathcal{L}^{0,p}_{\mu}(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}},\nu)
, the set
A=\{x\in N^{c}:\int|f_{x}|^{p}\,d\nu_{x}=\infty\}
belongs to
\varSigma_{0}
.
Define
f_{1}(x,y)=0
when
x\in A
,
f_{1}(x,y)=f(x,y)
otherwise.
Then
f_{1}
is
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}
-measurable since
\varSigma_{0}\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}
.
For
x\in A
, we have
[\eta_{\bullet}(f_{1})]_{x}=0=(f_{1})_{x}
and
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]_{x}=0
. For
x\in N^{c}\setminus A
, we have
[\eta_{\bullet}(f_{1})]_{x}=\eta(f_{x})=_{\nu_{x}}f_{x}=(f_{1})_{x}
. Thus,
g=\eta_{\bullet}(f_{1})
satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
6.7
(iii) with respect to
f_{1}
and hence
\eta_{\bullet}(f_{1})
is
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}
-measurable. Since
\eta_{\bullet}(f)
and
\eta_{\bullet}(f_{1})
agree on
N^{c}\times Y
, and since
\mathcal{P}(N\times Y)\subseteq\mathcal{I}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{0}\subseteq\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}
, it follows that
\eta_{\bullet}(f)
is
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}
-measurable as well. By assumption,
A\times Y\in(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})_{0}
, so from
[C]
it follows that
\eta_{\bullet}(f)=_{\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}}f
.

Case 3.
p=\infty
. Proceed as in Case 2, replacing
\int|f_{x}|^{p}\,d\nu_{x}
by
\|f_{x}\|_{\infty}
, using Proposition
2.19
(ii) for the measurability of
x\mapsto\|f_{x}\|_{\infty}
.
∎

Example 6.11
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
, and
(X\times{Y},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[C]
. Write
\nu=(\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
.
If
\eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in X}
, where each
\eta_{x}\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
satisfies
(l1)
,
then
\eta
is a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{0,p}_{\mu}(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\nu)},\nu)
and hence also with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\nu)})
.

Proof.

Use Lemma
6.9
with
\mathcal{I}=\varSigma_{0}
. (See Remark
6.10
(c).)
∎

Example 6.12
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
be probability spaces and let
(X\times Y,\varSigma\otimes T,\mu\otimes\nu)
be the product space.
If
\eta\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
satisfies
(l1)
,
then
\eta
is a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{0,p}_{\mu}(\varSigma\otimes_{\{\emptyset\}\ltimes T_{0}}T,\nu)
and hence also with respect to

\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu\otimes_{\{\emptyset\}\ltimes T_{0}}\nu)
.

Proof.

Use Lemma
6.9
with
\mathcal{I}=\{\emptyset\}
.
∎

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
X
be a set,
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
probability spaces,
f\colon X\times Y\to{\mathbb{K}}
,
\eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in X}
with
\eta_{x}:\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})\rightarrow{\mathbb{K}}^{Y}
.

(i)

For
x\in X
,
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]_{x}
equals
\eta_{x}(f_{x})
if
f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
and equals
0
otherwise.

(ii)

If each
\eta_{x}
is homogenous and
f=g\otimes h
for some
g\in{\mathbb{K}}^{X}
and
h\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
, then
\eta_{\bullet}(f)(x,y)=g(x)\eta_{x}(h)(y)
for each
