(x,y)\in X\times Y
. In particular, if we have a single space
(Y,T,\nu)
and a single
\eta:\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)\rightarrow{\mathbb{K}}^{Y}
, where
\eta
is homogeneous, then
\eta_{\bullet}(f)=g\otimes\eta(h)
.
12
12

12

So in general,
\eta_{\bullet}
does not satisfy
(l2)
.

(iii)

If
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{R}}
, when
\eta_{x}(g)\geq{0}

(x\in X)
for all nonnegative
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
,
f\geq 0
implies
\eta_{\bullet}(f)\geq{0}
.

(iv)

If we also have
g\colon X\times Y\to{\mathbb{K}}
and
f_{x},g_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
for every
x\in X
then the following hold.

(a)

If each
\eta_{x}
is homogeneous then
\eta_{\bullet}(af)=a\eta_{\bullet}(f)

(a\in{\mathbb{K}})
.

(b)

If each
\eta_{x}
is additive then
\eta_{\bullet}(f+g)=\eta_{\bullet}(f)+\eta_{\bullet}(g)
.

(c)

If
p=0
or
\infty
and each
\eta_{x}
is multiplicative then

\eta_{\bullet}(fg)=\eta_{\bullet}(f)\eta_{\bullet}(g)
.

(d)

If
\eta_{x}(f_{x})=f_{x}
for all
x\in X
then

\eta_{\bullet}(f)=f
.

Proof.

(i) is true by definition of
\eta_{\bullet}
.

(ii) We have
f_{x}=[g\otimes h]_{x}=g(x)h\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for every
x\in X
, so by homogeneity of
\eta_{x}
,
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]_{x}=\eta(f_{x})=g(x)\eta_{x}(h)
giving
\eta_{\bullet}(f)(x,y)=g(x)\eta_{x}(h)(y)
.

(iii) holds by (i).

(iv) is clear from the definitions. In (c), that
p=0
or
\infty
ensures that each
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
is closed under multiplication.
∎

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[C]
, and let
\eta:\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)\rightarrow{\mathbb{K}}^{Y}
.
If
\eta
satisfies
(l2)
then for
f,g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
with
f=_{\upsilon}g
we have for all
y\in{Y}
the equality
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}=_{\widehat{\mu}}[\eta_{\bullet}(g)]^{y}
.

Proof.

By
[C]
, there is a set
N\in\varSigma_{0}
such that for
x\in N^{c}
we have
f_{x},g_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
and
f_{x}=_{\nu}g_{x}
.
By
(l2)
for
\eta
we get
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]_{x}=\eta(f_{x})=\eta(g_{x})=[\eta_{\bullet}(g)]_{x}
for
x\in N^{c}
. Thus,
\{[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\not=[\eta_{\bullet}(g)]^{y}\}\subseteq{N}
for all
y\in Y
, implying
\{[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\not=[\eta_{\bullet}(g)]^{y}\}\in\widehat{\varSigma}_{0}
for all
y\in{Y}
.
∎

Example 6.15
.

Denote by
(X\times Y,\mathfrak{S}\times{\mathfrak{T}},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
the
\tau
-additive product of the
\tau
-additive topological probability spaces
(X,{\mathfrak{S}},\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
, where
\nu
is complete. If
\eta\in{V}^{p}(\nu)
then Lemma
6.14
applies to
\eta
, and in Lemma
6.13
, clauses (ii) and (iv)(a,b,d) apply to
\eta
, and, when
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{R}}
, so does (iii) if additionally
\eta\in\mathcal{G}(\nu)
, i.e.,
\eta
is a linear lifting, and so does (iv)(c) if
\eta\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu)
.
(See
[
4
]
, Theorem 2.15, for the hypotheses of Lemma
6.14
.)

The next Lemma extends
[
27
]
, Proposition 3.1 as well as
[
3
]
, Lemma 4.4. Compare
[
5
]
, Lemma 4.7.

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
,
(X\times{Y},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces so that
[C]
holds. Let
\nu=(\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
and
\eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in X}
, where
\eta_{x}\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
. Fix a linear subspace
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{0,p}_{\mu}(\Upsilon,\nu)
.

(i)

If
\eta
is a
2
-marginal for
\mathcal{M}
and there is an
N\in\varSigma_{0}
such that
x\notin N
implies
\eta_{x}
satisfies
(l1)
on
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
, then
\eta_{\bullet}
satisfies
(l1)
on
\mathcal{M}
.

(ii)

If for each
x
,
\eta_{x}(0)=0
and
(l1)
,
(l2)
hold for
\eta_{x}
, then
\eta_{x}([\eta_{\bullet}(f)]_{x})=[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]_{x}
for any function
f
on
X\times Y
.

(iii)

If
\eta
is a
2
-marginal for
\mathcal{M}
, for each
x
,
\eta_{x}\in V^{p}(\nu_{x})
, and
\varSigma_{0}\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
, then there is a
\psi\in V(\mathcal{M})
such that
\eta_{x}([\psi(f)]_{x})=[\psi(f)]_{x}
for all
f\in\mathcal{M}
,
x\in X
.

(iv)

Suppose
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{R}}
and
p=\infty
. If
\eta
is a
2
-marginal for
\mathcal{M}
,
\eta_{x}\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu_{x})
for each
x
,
\varSigma_{0}\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
, and there exists a
\theta\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\upsilon\upharpoonright\Upsilon[T])
then there is a
\psi\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\upsilon)
such that
\eta_{x}([\psi(f)]_{x})=[\psi(f)]_{x}
for all
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\upsilon)
,
x\in X
.

Remark 6.17
.

Under CH, by a theorem of Mokobodzki (cf.
[
5
]
, Theorem 2.7), the assumption in (iv) that there is a lifting for
\upsilon\upharpoonright\Upsilon[T]
holds if the measure algebra of
\upsilon
has cardinality at most
\aleph_{2}
.

Proof.

(i) Let
f\in\mathcal{M}
. Then
f\in\mathcal{L}^{0,p}(\Upsilon,\nu)
, so there is an
N\in\varSigma_{0}
such that
x\notin N
implies
f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
and
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]_{x}=\eta_{x}(f_{x})=_{\nu_{x}}f_{x}
. By
[C]
,
\eta_{\bullet}(f)=_{\upsilon}f
.

(ii) If
f_{x}\notin\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
, then by definition
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]_{x}=0
and the conclusion is clear.
If
f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
, then by
(l1)
,
\eta_{x}(f_{x})=_{\nu_{x}}f_{x}
and then by
(l2)
,
\eta_{x}(\eta_{x}(f_{x}))=\eta_{x}(f_{x})
. The conclusion follows since by definition
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]_{x}=\eta_{x}(f_{x})
.

(iii) If
\eta_{x}\in V^{p}(\nu_{x})
, take any
\theta\in V(\mathcal{M})
whose values are in
\mathcal{L}^{0,p}(\Upsilon,\nu)
(cf. Example
4.9
(b)). Define

\psi(f):=\eta_{\bullet}(\theta(f))
for each
f\in\mathcal{M}
.
Since
\theta
and
\eta_{\bullet}
both satisfy
(l1)
, so does
\psi
, and

\psi
satisfies
(l2)
since
\theta
does.
Linearity of
\psi
follows from linearity of
\theta
and the
\eta_{x}
, and the fact that
\theta
takes values in
\mathcal{L}^{0,p}(\Upsilon,\nu)
(see Lemma
6.13
(iv) (a,b)). Since
\theta(1)=1
and
\eta_{\bullet}(1)=1
, we get
\psi(1)=1
, so
\psi\in V(\mathcal{M})
. Finally, for all
f\in\mathcal{M}
and
x\in{X}
we have
\eta_{x}([\psi(f)]_{x})=[\psi(f)]_{x}
by (ii).

(iv) If
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{R}}
,
p=\infty
,
\eta_{x}\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu_{x})
for each
x
, and we fix
\theta\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\upsilon\upharpoonright\Upsilon[T])
, then by Corollary
2.21
, the inclusion
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\upsilon\upharpoonright\Upsilon[T])\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\upsilon)
induces a bijection
L^{\infty}(\upsilon\upharpoonright\Upsilon[T])\to L^{\infty}(\upsilon)
which is clearly an algebra isomorphism, so it suffices to define
\psi
on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\upsilon\upharpoonright\Upsilon[T])
. Notice that
[\theta(f)]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\nu_{x})
when
x\in X
,
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\upsilon\upharpoonright\Upsilon[T])
since
[\theta(f)]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu_{x})
by Corollary
2.18
, and
|\theta(f)|\leq\|f\|_{\infty}
(
[
18
]
, (1) page 35). Thus,
\theta
takes values in
\mathcal{L}^{0,\infty}(\Upsilon,\nu)
.
Take
\psi(f):=\eta_{\bullet}(\theta(f))
,
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\upsilon)
, and proceed as in (iii) (using also Lemma
6.13
(iv)(c)).
∎

There is a converse of sorts to Lemma
6.16
(iii).

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
,
(X\times{Y},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[C]
. Let
\nu=(\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
and
\eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in X}
, where
\eta_{x}\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
satisfies
(l2)
for each
x
.
Let
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{0,p}_{\mu}(\Upsilon,\nu)
satisfy
\mathcal{M}+\mathcal{N}(\mu)=\mathcal{M}
.
Suppose
(\varSigma_{0}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon
and there exists a function
\psi\colon\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
satisfying
(l1)
so that

\eta_{x}([\psi(f)]_{x})=[\psi(f)]_{x},\ \text{for all $f\in\mathcal{M}$ with $x\notin N_{f}$, for some $N_{f}\in\varSigma_{0}$.}

(i)

Then
\eta
is a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{M}
and
\eta_{\bullet}
satisfies
(l1)
on
\mathcal{M}
.

(ii)

When we have a single space
(Y,T,\nu)
and a single map
\eta\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
, if
1\otimes g\in\mathcal{M}
for all
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
then
\eta
satisfies
(l1)
.

Remark 6.19
.

When
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
, the condition
X\,\dot{\times}\,T\subseteq\Upsilon
is sufficient to ensure that
1\otimes g\in\mathcal{M}
for all
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
.

Remark 6.20
.

Take
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{R}}
and suppose
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
,
(X\times Y,\varSigma\otimes T,\upsilon)
are probability spaces so that
(\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
is a product r.c.p. on
T
for
\upsilon
with respect to
\mu
.
According to
[
24
]
Theorem 3.11, for every equi-admissible family
(\tau_{x})_{x\in{X}}
of densities
\tau_{x}\in\vartheta(\nu_{x})
(see
[
24
]
for the definition) there exist a family
\eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in{X}}
of liftings
\eta_{x}\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\widehat{\nu}_{x})
admissibly generated by
\tau_{x}
(see
[
24
]
for the definition), and a lifting
\varphi:\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}})\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\upsilon})
such that

[\varphi(f)]_{x}=\eta_{x}\bigl([\varphi(f)]_{x}\bigr)\;\;\mbox{for every}\;\;f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}})\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;x\in{X}.

By Lemma
6.18
applied to
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\widehat{\nu}_{x})_{x\in X}
,
(X\times Y,\varSigma\,\widehat{\otimes}_{\upsilon}\,T,\widehat{\upsilon})
, where
\varSigma\,\widehat{\otimes}_{\upsilon}\,T
is the domain of
\widehat{\upsilon}
,
\eta
is a 2-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\upsilon})
.

Proof.

(i) Fix
f\in\mathcal{M}
. Since
\psi(f)=_{\upsilon}f
, using
[C]
and enlarging the given set
N_{f}\in\varSigma_{0}
if necessary, we can assume that
[\psi(f)]_{x},\,f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
and
[\psi(f)]_{x}=_{\nu_{x}}f_{x}
for every
x\notin N_{f}
. For
x\notin N_{f}
we have, using
(l2)
for
\eta_{x}
,

[\psi(f)]_{x}=\eta_{x}([\psi(f)]_{x})=\eta_{x}(f_{x})=[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]_{x},

so
\psi(f)\upharpoonright(N_{f}^{c}\times Y)=\eta_{\bullet}(f)\upharpoonright(N_{f}^{c}\times Y)
. Also
\eta_{\bullet}(f)\upharpoonright N_{f}\times Y
is
\Upsilon
-measurable since
(\varSigma_{0}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon_{0}
. Since
\psi(f)\in\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}+\mathcal{N}(\mu)=\mathcal{M}
, we have
\eta_{\bullet}(f)\in\mathcal{M}
and
\eta_{\bullet}(f)=_{\upsilon}\psi(f)=_{\upsilon}f
, so
\eta_{\bullet}
satisfies
(l1)
.

(ii) Now assume that we have a single space
(Y,T,\nu)
and a single map
\eta\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
, and
1\otimes g\in\mathcal{M}
for all
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
.
To see that
\eta
satisfies
(l1)
, fix
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
and let
f=1\otimes g
. Note that

\{\psi(1\otimes{g})\not=1\otimes{g}\}\in\Upsilon_{0}\subseteq\varSigma_{0}\ltimes T_{0}
. Choose
x\notin N_{f}
such that
\{[\psi(1\otimes{g})]_{x}\not=[1\otimes{g}]_{x}=g\}\in T_{0}
. Since
x\notin N_{f}
, as in (i) we have
[\psi(1\otimes{g})]_{x}=[\eta_{\bullet}(1\otimes{g})]_{x}=\eta(g)
, yielding
\{\eta(g)\not=g\}\in T_{0}
, or
\eta(g)=_{\nu}g
and hence
\eta
satisfies
(l1)
.
∎

The next example shows that, assuming sets of reals of cardinality less than
\mathfrak{c}
have measure zero, the restriction to having a single
\eta
cannot be removed in (ii).

Example 6.21
.

Let each of
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,T,\nu)
be
[0,1]
with Lebesgue measure, and let
F^{\bullet}
denote the class of
F\in T
in the measure algebra of
\nu
. Let
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be the Lebesgue measure space on
[0,1]^{2}
. There are
\eta_{x}\colon\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)\to\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)
,
x\in X
, satisfying
(l2)
so that
\eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in X}
is a
2
-marginal for
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
, and
\eta_{\bullet}
satisfies
(l1)
on
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
, but none of the maps
\eta_{x}
satisfy
(l1)
.

Let
E_{x}
,
x\in X
be non-null Borel sets so that for each Borel set
A\subseteq X\times Y
,
\{x\in X:(E_{x})^{\bullet}=(A_{x})^{\bullet}\}
has cardinality less than
\mathfrak{c}
.
13
13

13

List
[0,1]
bijectively as
\{x_{\alpha}:\alpha<\mathfrak{c}\}
. List the Borel subsets of
X\times Y
as
\{B_{\alpha}:\alpha<\mathfrak{c}\}
. Recursively define
E_{x_{\alpha}}=[0,t_{\alpha}]
so that at each stage,
(E_{x_{\alpha}})^{\bullet}
is different from any of the
<\mathfrak{c}
classes
([B_{\beta}]_{x_{\alpha}})^{\bullet}
,
\beta<\alpha
.

Claim 6.22
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
, there is an
N\in\varSigma_{0}
such that
\{x\in N^{c}:f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(T),\,f_{x}=_{\nu}\chi_{E_{x}}\}
has cardinality
<\mathfrak{c}
.

Proof.

Let
g\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
be Borel so that
f=_{\upsilon}g
. For some set
N\in\varSigma_{0}
,
x\in N^{c}
implies
f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)
,
f_{x}=_{\nu}g_{x}
. Let
A=g^{-1}(1)
. Then
S=\{x\in X:(E_{x})^{\bullet}=(A_{x})^{\bullet}\}
has cardinality less than
\mathfrak{c}
. If
x\notin N
and
f_{x}=_{\nu}\chi_{E_{x}}
, then
g_{x}=_{\nu}\chi_{E_{x}}
so
(A_{x})^{\bullet}=(E_{x})^{\bullet}
giving
x\in S
.
∎

Suppose that sets of size
<\mathfrak{c}
have measure zero, which is true for example if CH holds.
Fix a
\delta\colon\mathcal{L}^{0}(T)\to\mathcal{L}^{0}(T)
which is a
2
-marginal for
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
(for example, see Proposition 7.24 along with
[
3
]
Example 4.8) and let
\eta_{x}\colon\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)\to\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)
agree with
\delta
except on functions
h=_{\nu}\chi_{E_{x}}
, setting
\eta_{x}(h)=0
for such functions. If
f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
, then the set

N_{f}:=\{x\in X:f_{x}\notin\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu),\ \text{or}\ f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)\ \text{and}\ f_{x}=_{\nu}\chi_{E_{x}}\}

has measure zero by the claim.
Since
\eta_{\bullet}(f)
and
\delta_{\bullet}(f)
agree on
N_{f}^{c}\times Y
,
\eta_{\bullet}(f)\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
and
\eta_{\bullet}(f)=_{\upsilon}\delta_{\bullet}(f)=_{\upsilon}f
, so
(l1)
holds for
\eta_{\bullet}
. It follows that each
\eta_{x}
satisfies
(l2)
and
\eta
is a
2
-marginal for
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
but no
\eta_{x}
satisfies
(l1)
since
\eta_{x}(\chi_{E_{x}})=0
.

The following example extends that included in Remark 7.20.

Example 6.23
.

Let
(X,\varSigma)
,
(Y,\widetilde{T})
be measurable spaces. Let
\upsilon
be a probability measure on
\varSigma\otimes\widetilde{T}
, and
\mu
the marginal measure of
\upsilon
on
\varSigma
. Suppose that the marginal measure
\widetilde{\nu}
of
\upsilon
on
\widetilde{T}
is inner regular with respect to a countably compact class
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\widetilde{T}
(see
[
14
]
451A for definition) which is closed under finite unions and countable intersections. By
[
29
]
Theorem 3.5 (see also
[
14
]
Theorem 452M) there exists a family
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in{X}}
of complete probability spaces satisfying the property
[C]
for
\Upsilon=\varSigma\otimes\widetilde{T}
such that for every
x\in{X}
the inclusion
\mathcal{K}\subseteq{T}_{x}
holds and
\nu_{x}
is inner regular with respect to
\mathcal{K}
. So, we may apply
[
24
]
Theorem 3.8, in order to find a family

(\eta_{x})_{x\in{X}}
of liftings
\eta_{x}\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu_{x})
and a lifting
\varphi\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\widehat{\upsilon})
such that

[\varphi(f)]_{x}=\eta_{x}([\varphi(f)]_{x})\quad\mbox{for every}\quad x\in{X}\quad\mbox{and}\quad f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\varSigma\widehat{\otimes}_{\upsilon}\widetilde{T}).

By Lemma 7.18 applied to the probability spaces
(X,\varSigma,\mu),(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in{X}}
, and
(X\times{Y},\varSigma\,\widehat{\otimes}_{\upsilon}\,\widetilde{T},\widehat{\upsilon})
, the family
\eta:=\langle\eta_{x}\rangle_{x\in{X}}
is a 2-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\upsilon})
.

Note that the existence of a family
(\nu_{x})_{x\in{X}}
of probability measures
\nu_{x}
as above with domain
\widetilde{T}
is guaranteed, if either
\widetilde{T}
is the Baire
\sigma
-algebra with respect to a compact Hausdorf topology on
Y
, or
\widetilde{T}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra with respect to an analytic Hausdorff topology on
Y
, or
(Y,\widetilde{T})
is a standard Borel space (see
[
14
]
452N).

From Lemmas
6.16
and
6.18
, we easily get the following corollary.

Corollary 6.24
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T_{x},\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
,
(X\times{Y},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[C]
. Suppose that
(\varSigma_{0}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon
.
Let
\eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in X}
with each
\eta_{x}\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
satisfying
\eta_{x}(0)=0
,
(l1)
, and
(l2)
. The following statements are equivalent.

(i)

\eta
is
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
;

(ii)

\eta_{\bullet}(f)=_{\upsilon}f
for every
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
;

(iii)

there exists a map
\varphi:\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
satisfying
(l1)
, such that
\eta_{x}([\varphi(f)]_{x})=[\varphi(f)]_{x}
for all
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
and
x\in X
;

(iv)

there exists a map
\varphi:\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
satisfying
(l1)
, such that for each
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
there exists a set
N_{f}\in\varSigma_{0}
with
\eta_{x}([\varphi(f)]_{x})=[\varphi(f)]_{x}
for all
x\notin N_{f}
.

Proposition 6.25
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[C]
and
\widehat{\varSigma}_{0}\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
, and suppose that there is a
\theta\colon T\to T
satisfying
(L1)
which is a
2
-marginal for
\Upsilon
. Then there is a
\delta\colon\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)\to\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)
satisfying
(l1)
which is a
2
-marginal for
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
. If
Y
has a topology
\mathfrak{T}\subseteq T
with respect to which
\theta
is strong, then we may take
\delta
to be strong, i.e., to fix the elements of
C(Y,\mathfrak{T})
.

Proof.

We may assume that
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{R}}
. By
[C]
and
\widehat{\varSigma}_{0}\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
, it follows from Proposition 4.5 of
[
5
]
that
\theta_{0}\colon\mathcal{L}^{0}(\overline{{\mathbb{R}}},\nu)\to\mathcal{L}^{0}(\overline{{\mathbb{R}}},\nu)
is a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\overline{{\mathbb{R}}},\upsilon)
, where
\theta_{0}(f)(y)=\sup\{r\in{\mathbb{Q}}:y\in\theta\{r<f\}\}
. By Proposition 3.4 (iv) of
[
5
]
,
\theta_{0}
satisfies
(l1)
.
Let
\delta\colon\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)\to\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)
be the restriction of
\theta_{0}
to
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)
, with values modified to replace
\pm\infty
by
0
, i.e.,
\delta(f)(x)=\theta_{0}(f)(x)
when
\theta_{0}(f)(x)\not=\pm\infty
,
\delta(f)(x)=0
otherwise.

We check that
\delta
is a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
. If
f\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
then
(\theta_{0})_{\bullet}(f)
is
\Upsilon
-measurable. By Lemma
6.16
(i) (which works in the same way for functions into
\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}
),
(\theta_{0})_{\bullet}(f)=_{\upsilon}f
and hence
A=\{(\theta_{0})_{\bullet}(f)=\pm\infty\}\in\Upsilon_{0}
. We have

\delta_{\bullet}(f)\upharpoonright A^{c}=(\theta_{0})_{\bullet}(f)\upharpoonright A^{c}
and
\delta_{\bullet}(f)\upharpoonright A\equiv 0
, so
\delta_{\bullet}(f)
is
\Upsilon
-measurable.

For the last sentence, Proposition 3.6 of
[
5
]
implies that
\theta_{0}
is strong and hence
\delta
is strong.
∎

Question 6.26
.

Is there a vector lifting for Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]
which is a
2
-marginal for
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
, where
\upsilon
is Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]^{2}
?

In the next example, we show that it is possible that a primitive lifting for
\mathcal{L}^{p}
which is a
2
-marginal for
\mathcal{L}^{q}
for all
q>p
might not be a
2
-marginal for
\mathcal{L}^{p}
.

Example 6.27
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,T,\nu)
be the Lebesgue measure space on
(0,1)
, and let
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be the Lebesgue measure space on
(0,1)^{2}
.
For any
p\in[0,\infty)
, there is a primitive lifting for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
which is not a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
but whose restriction to
\mathcal{L}^{q}(\nu)
is a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{q}(\upsilon)
for each
q>p
.

Proof.

It suffices to do the case
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{R}}
. Since there are strong liftings for
\nu
which are
2
-marginals for
\Upsilon
by
[
3
]
Example 4.8, Proposition
6.25
gives a
\delta\colon\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)\to\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)
which is a strong
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
.

Fix a positive continuous function
h
on
(0,1)
which belongs to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
but not to
\mathcal{L}^{q}(\nu)
for any
q>p
. (One possibility: if
p=0
take
h(x)=e^{1/x}
; if
0<p<\infty
take
h(x)=[x\log^{2}(2/x)]^{-1/p}
.) Define
f(x,y)=xh(y)
. We have
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
. Note that the sections
f_{x}
are distinct. They are also continuous, so
\delta(f_{x})=f_{x}
since
\delta
is strong.
Fix a set
E\subseteq(0,1)^{2}
which is the graph of a function such that each section
E^{y}
has outer measure one. (See Example
3.24
.) Let
\rho\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
agree with
\delta
except for setting
\rho(f_{x})(y)=0
when
(x,y)\in E
. When
q>p
, for any function
g\in\mathcal{L}^{q}(\upsilon)
we have
\delta_{\bullet}(g)\in\mathcal{L}^{q}(\upsilon)
. For some
N\in\varSigma_{0}
,
g_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{q}(\nu)
, and hence
\rho(g_{x})=\delta(g_{x})
, for
x\in N^{c}
. Thus,
\rho_{\bullet}(g)
agrees with
\delta_{\bullet}(g)
on
N^{c}\times Y
and therefore
\rho_{\bullet}(g)\in\mathcal{L}^{q}(\upsilon)
. However, for
g=f
, the zero set of
\rho_{\bullet}(f)
is
E
and hence
\rho_{\bullet}(f)
is not
\Upsilon
-measurable.
By
[
5
]
, Proposition 3.4,
\delta
satisfies
(l2)
and fixes constant functions; hence
\delta
and
\rho
are primitive liftings for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
.
∎

The following lemma corresponds to
[
3
]
, Lemma 4.9.
14
14

14

The claim after the statement of Lemma 4.9 in
[
3
]
that the assumptions imply
\nu
is complete is incorrect. Completeness of
\nu
should be added to the hypothesis. Alternatively, write
\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\nu)
instead of
\mathfrak{N}
as we have done here.

Lemma 6.28
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisying
[P_{2}]
and
[C]
, and let
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\mathcal{I})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
with
\varSigma_{0}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y\subseteq\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{K}:=\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\nu)=\varSigma_{0}\ltimes T_{0}
.
If
\eta:\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)\rightarrow{\mathbb{K}}^{Y}
satisfies
(l2)
and
\eta_{\bullet}(g)\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}})
when
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu\otimes\nu)
, then

(i)

\eta_{\bullet}(f)\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}})
for each

f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
.

(ii)

\eta
is a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{J})
for any
J
with
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,J)\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq J\subseteq\mathcal{K}
.

Remark 6.29
.

(a)
\mathcal{K}
is an ideal of
\varSigma_{0}\ltimes T
and
\Upsilon\subseteq\varSigma_{0}\ltimes T
, so
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\mathcal{K})
belongs to
\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
.

(b)
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
are
\sigma
-ideals of the
\sigma
-algebras
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}
, respectively.

Proof.

For each ideal
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{I},\mathcal{K}
, and in (ii) also
J
, since
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
and
[C]
holds, the condition
\mathcal{H}\cap\Upsilon\subseteq\Upsilon_{0}
is satisfied, so
\upsilon_{\mathcal{H}}
is defined.

(i) Fix
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
. Choose
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu\otimes\nu)
such that
f=_{\mathcal{K}}g
by Lemma
6.4
. By
[C]
for
\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}
(Proposition
3.28
), fix
N\in\varSigma_{0}
such that
f_{x},g_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
and
\{f_{x}\not=g_{x}\}\in T_{0}
for all
x\in N^{c}
. For
x\in N^{c}
we have
f_{x}=_{\nu}g_{x}
, so
\eta(f_{x})=\eta(g_{x})
by
(l2)
. It follows that

\eta_{\bullet}(f)\upharpoonright(N^{c}\times Y)=\eta_{\bullet}(g)\upharpoonright(N^{c}\times Y)
.
By assumption,
\eta_{\bullet}(g)\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}})
. Then because
\varSigma_{0}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y\subseteq\mathcal{I}
it follows that
\eta_{\bullet}(f)\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}})
.

(ii) Follows from (i) since
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{I}})\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{J})\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
.
∎

Proposition 6.30
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[P_{0}]
and
[C]
. Let
\mathcal{K}:=\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\nu)=\varSigma_{0}\ltimes T_{0}
.
For every
\gamma\in V^{p}(\mu)
and
\eta\in V^{p}(\nu)
there exists a
\varphi\in V^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})\cap(\gamma\otimes\eta)
such that
\eta([\varphi(f)]_{x})=[\varphi(f)]_{x}
for every
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
and
x\in X
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.28
,
\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}
is defined and
[C]
holds for the triple
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon_{\mathcal{K}},\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
.
By Proposition
5.12
choose

\pi\in(\gamma\otimes\eta)\cap V^{p}(\upsilon)
such that for every
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
,

[\pi(f)]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for all
x\in X
. Define

\psi:=\eta_{\bullet}\circ\pi
, i.e.,

[\psi(f)]_{x}:=\eta([\pi(f)]_{x})
for
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
and
x\in X
.
Clearly
\psi(1)=1
, and

\eta([\psi(f)]_{x})=[\psi(f)]_{x}\;\mbox{for}\;f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)\;\mbox{and}\;x\in X.

By Lemma
6.8
the map
\eta
is a 2-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
, so
\psi(f)=\eta_{\bullet}(\pi(f))\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
and
\psi(f)=_{\mathcal{K}}\pi(f)=_{\upsilon}f
, implying
\psi(f)=_{\mathcal{K}}f
since
\Upsilon_{0}\subseteq{\mathcal{K}}
. Thus, we have

\psi\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}),\ \ \text{$\psi(f)=_{\mathcal{K}}f$.}

Since
\pi
satisfies
(l2)
, so does
\psi=\eta_{\bullet}\circ\pi
.
By Lemma
6.13
(iv)(a,b),
\psi
is linear since
\pi
is linear and
[\pi(f)]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for every
x\in X
.
For
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
,
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
, we have, using Lemma
6.13
(ii),

\psi(f\otimes{g})=\eta_{\bullet}(\pi(f\otimes{g}))=\eta_{\bullet}(\gamma(f)\otimes\eta(g))=\gamma(f)\otimes\eta(\eta(g))=\gamma(f)\otimes\eta(g),

i.e. we have
\psi\in\gamma\otimes\eta
.

Thus,
\psi
has all the desired properties of
\varphi
except that it is defined only on
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
rather than on all of
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
.
Using Lemma
3.1
(ii), we now readily extend the domain of
\psi
to all of
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
. Since
\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}
extends
\upsilon
, if
g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
satisfy
g_{1}=_{\mathcal{K}}g_{2}
then
g_{1}=_{\upsilon}g_{2}
. Hence, we have a linear isomorphism
i\colon L^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})\to L^{p}(\upsilon)
where for each
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
, we map
g^{\bullet}\in L^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
to
g^{\bullet}\in L^{p}(\upsilon)
. The composition

\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\longrightarrow}}L^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\longrightarrow}}L^{p}(\upsilon)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{\psi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)

is the desired extension
\varphi
of
\psi
, where
q(f)=f^{\bullet}
and
\overline{\psi}(g^{\bullet})=\psi(g)
. Clearly
\varphi(1)=1
, and
\varphi
is a composition of linear maps and hence linear. Since
q
satisfies
(l2)
, so does
\varphi
. Invariance of the sections under
\eta
holds since for
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
,
[\varphi(f)]_{x}=[\psi(g)]_{x}
for any
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
in the
\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}}
-class of
f
.
Also for such
f
and
g
,
\varphi(f)=\psi(g)=_{\mathcal{K}}g=_{\mathcal{K}}f
, so
(l1)
holds.
The property
\varphi\in\gamma\otimes\eta
holds since this property only deals with functions
f\otimes g
that belong to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
on which
\varphi
and
\psi
agree.
∎

Taking
\upsilon=\mu\otimes\nu
with
\nu
complete gives the following.

Corollary 6.31
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,T,\nu)
be probability spaces with
\nu
complete. For every

\gamma\in V^{p}(\mu)
and
\eta\in V^{p}(\nu)
there exists a
\varphi\in V^{p}(\mu\otimes_{\mathfrak{N}}\nu)\cap(\gamma\otimes\eta)
, such that
\eta([\varphi(f)]_{x})=[\varphi(f)]_{x}
for every
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu\otimes_{\mathfrak{N}}\nu)
and
x\in X
.

Theorem 6.32
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
and
(X\times Y,\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be topological probability spaces satisfying
[P_{0}]
and
[C]
. Let
\mathcal{K}:=\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\nu)=\varSigma_{0}\ltimes T_{0}
. Assume
(N_{C,\mu})
,
(N_{C,\nu})
hold, and

(6.33)

h_{x}\in C^{p}(Y)
for all
h\in C^{p}(X\times Y)
and
x\in X
.

For every
\gamma\in V^{p}(\mathfrak{S},\mu)
and
\eta\in V^{p}(\mathfrak{T},\nu)
there exists a
\varphi\in V^{p}(\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})\cap(\gamma\otimes\eta)
such that
\eta([\varphi(f)]_{x})=[\varphi(f)]_{x}
for every
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathcal{K}})
and
x\in X
.

Remark 6.34
.

Here are some conditions under which (
6.33
) holds:
(1)
p=0
;
(2)
p=\infty
(by Lemma
5.14
,
(N_{C,\upsilon})
holds, so if
h\in C(X\times Y)
and
\|h\|_{\infty}<\infty
, then
h
is bounded by Remark
4.11
(iii));
(3) any
p
, if
Y
is pseudocompact, i.e., the elements of
C(Y)
are all bounded.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.15
choose a
\pi\in(\gamma\otimes\eta)\cap{V}^{p}(\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\upsilon)
for every
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
satisfying
[\pi(f)]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
for all
x\in{X}
, and repeat the construction of
\varphi
from the proof of Proposition
6.30
using this
\pi
. From (
6.33
) and Lemma
6.13
(iv)(d) we have for
h\in C^{p}(X\times Y)
that
\eta_{\bullet}(h)=h
. Along with the fact that
\pi
is
C^{p}(X\times{Y})
-strong this implies that
\varphi(h)=\psi(h)=\eta_{\bullet}(\pi(h))=\eta_{\bullet}(h)=h
, i.e.
\varphi
is
C^{p}(X\times{Y})
-strong.
∎

Corollary 6.35
.

Let
(X\times Y,\mathfrak{S}\times{\mathfrak{T}},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be the

\tau
-additive product of the
\tau
-additive topological probability spaces
(X,{\mathfrak{S}},\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
with
\nu
complete. Assume that
(N_{C,\mu})
,
(N_{C,\nu})
, and
(
6.33
)
hold. For every
\gamma\in V^{p}({\mathfrak{S}},\mu)
,
\eta\in V^{p}({\mathfrak{T}},\nu)
there exists a
\varphi\in V^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}})\cap(\gamma\otimes\eta)
such that
\eta([\varphi(f)]_{x})=[\varphi(f)]_{x}
for every
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}})
and
x\in X
.

Proof.

Since the assumptions of Theorem
6.32
are satisfied by
[
4
]
, Theorem 2.15, we may apply Theorem
6.32
in order to get the result.
∎

7.
Measurable modifications of stochastic processes

In this section we prove that the existence of a measurable
p
-integrable vector lifting modification for stochastic processes is equivalent to the existence of a marginal vector lifting in one of the factors (see Theorem
7.5
) and, in case of modifications respecting continuous functions to the existence of strong marginals (see Corollary
7.7
). In Theorem
7.11
and Corollary
7.13
we provide a characterization of stochastic processes which have a measurable modification.

Because our main objects of study here are liftings, in this section we assume throughout that
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{R}}
. The results not mentioning liftings work for complex scalars as well (by the same arguments).
Throughout this section,
p\in[0,\infty]
and we fix a measurable space
(\Gamma,\mathcal{B})
, where
\Gamma
is a Tychonoff space and
\mathcal{B}
is its
\sigma
-algebra of Baire sets.

Given a measurable space
(X,\varSigma)
, write
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{\Gamma}=\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{\Gamma}(\varSigma)
for the space of all bounded
\varSigma
-
\mathcal{B}
-measurable maps from
X
into
\Gamma
, where we say
f\colon X\to\Gamma
is
bounded
if
f(X)\subseteq K
for some compact
K\subseteq\Gamma
.

Remark 7.1
.

Measurability of
f\colon X\to\Gamma
is equivalent to
\varSigma
-measurability of each composition
h\circ f
, for
h\in C(\Gamma)
,
since
\mathcal{B}
is the Baire
\sigma
-algebra.

Definition 7.2
.

Fix a probability space
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
and
\rho\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\mu)
. Then
\rho^{\prime}:\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{\Gamma}
, the lifting of
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{\Gamma}

associated to

\rho
, is the unique map satisfying

(a)

h\circ\rho^{\prime}(f)=\rho(h\circ f)
for
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{\Gamma}
and
h\in C(\Gamma)
,

(b)

\rho^{\prime}(f)=^{*}_{\mu}f
for
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{\Gamma}
,

(c)

\rho^{\prime}(f)=\rho^{\prime}(g)
if
f=^{*}_{\mu}{g}
for
f,g\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{\Gamma}
.

where
f=_{\mu}^{*}g
means
h\circ f=_{\mu}h\circ g
for all
h\in C(\Gamma)
.

Remark 7.3
.

(i)
It follows by
[
5
]
, Remark 6.5(a) that, property (a) already uniquely determines
\rho^{\prime}
and hence necessarily implies (b) and (c).

(ii)
For the proof that
\rho^{\prime}
exists, see
Theorem IV.7 p. 52 of
[
18
]
.

(iii)
Again by
[
5
]
, Remark 6.5(d), in (b) we cannot strengthen
=^{*}_{\mu}
to
=_{\mu}
.

Definition 7.4
.

Let
(Y,T)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon)
be measurable spaces. For (c)–(e) below, assume we also have a family of measures
\nu:=(\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
on
T
.

A family
(Q_{x})_{x\in X}
of
T
-
\mathcal{B}
-measurable functions
Q_{x}\colon Y\to\Gamma
will be called a
stochastic process
(or just a
process
)
over

(Y,T)

with values in

\Gamma
. We say that the function
Q\colon X\times Y\to\Gamma
given by
Q(x,y)=Q_{x}(y)
and the process
(Q_{x})_{x\in X}
are
associated
to each other. Conveniently,
(Q_{x})_{x\in X}
is the family of vertical sections of
Q
, so there is no conflict with our notation for vertical sections. The process is called

(a)

bounded
if
Q_{x}\in\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}^{\infty}(T)
for every
x\in X
,

(b)

\Upsilon
-
\mathcal{B}
-
measurable
if
Q
is
\Upsilon
-
\mathcal{B}
-measurable,

(c)

an
\mathcal{L}^{p}
-process
if
\Gamma={\mathbb{R}}
and
Q_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
for each
x\in X
,

(d)

a
modification
of
(R_{x})_{x\in X}
if
Q_{x}=_{\nu_{x}}{R}_{x}
for every
x\in X
,

(e)

a
weak modification
of
(R_{x})_{x\in X}
if
Q_{x}=^{*}_{\nu_{x}}R_{x}
for every
x\in X
,

where
(R_{x})_{x\in X}
is another stochastic process over
(Y,T)
with values in
\Gamma
.

The next theorem does for liftings for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
something analogous to what Theorem 6.10 of
[
5
]
did for liftings for the measure algebra of
\nu
.

Theorem 7.5
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
, and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[C]
,
\varSigma\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
. For
\rho:=(\rho_{x})_{x\in X}
with
\rho_{x}\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
satisfying
(l1)
and
(l2)
with either
(\alpha)

(\varSigma_{0}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon
or
(\beta)

\rho(0)=0
and
\mu
is complete, consider the following statements. In
(iii)
, assume
\rho_{x}\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu_{x})
for each
x
.

(i)

\rho
is a
2
-marginal with respect to

\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
.

(ii)

(\rho_{x}(Q_{x}))_{x\in X}
is a
\Upsilon
-measurable
\mathcal{L}^{p}
-process for every
\Upsilon
-measurable
\mathcal{L}^{p}
-process
(Q_{x})_{x\in X}
.

(iii)

(\rho^{\prime}_{x}(Q_{x}))_{x\in X}
is
\Upsilon
-
\mathcal{B}
-measurable for every bounded
\Upsilon
-
\mathcal{B}
-measurable process
(Q_{x})_{x\in X}
over
(Y,T)
with values in
\Gamma
, for any choice of
\Gamma
.

Then
(i)
is equivalent to
(ii)
. When
p=\infty
and
\rho_{x}\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu_{x})
for
x\in X
,
(i)
–
(iii)
are all equivalent.

Remark 7.6
.

(a) When
p\in(0,\infty)
, Theorem
7.5
applies to vector liftings
\rho_{x}\in V^{p}(\nu_{x})
but none of these are linear liftings (Proposition
4.6
).

(b) In (iii)
\bigl(\rho^{\prime}_{x}(Q_{x})\bigr)
is a process by definition of
\rho^{\prime}
, and it is a weak modification of
(Q_{x})
by Definition
7.2
(b). (It may not be a modification of
(Q_{x})
by
[
5
]
, Remark 6.11.)

(c) For the case
p=\infty
and
\rho_{x}\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu_{x})
, the proof of (ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii) below makes use of none of the assumptions on
\upsilon
.

Proof.

(ii) is equivalent to stating that
\rho
is a
2
-marginal for
\mathcal{L}^{0,p}(\upsilon,\nu)
, so the equivalence of (i) and (ii) follows from Proposition
6.2
.
For the rest of the proof, let
p=\infty
and
\rho_{x}\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu_{x})
for each
x
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) This part follows as for the proof of (i)
\Rightarrow
(iv) in Theorem 6.10 of
[
5
]
. We repeat the argument for completeness.
By (ii),
\rho
is a
2
-marginal with respect to

\mathcal{L}^{0,\infty}(\upsilon,\nu)
.
Let
Q\colon X\times Y\to\Gamma
be
\Upsilon
-
\mathcal{B}
-measurable with
Q_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{\Gamma}(T)
for all
x\in X
. Let
g\in C(\Gamma)
. Then
g\circ Q\colon X\times Y\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
\Upsilon
-measurable, and each section
[g\circ Q]_{x}=g\circ Q_{x}
belongs to
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\nu_{x})
since
Q_{x}
is bounded. Thus,
g\circ Q\in\mathcal{L}^{0,\infty}(\upsilon,\nu)
and hence
\rho_{\bullet}(g\circ Q)\in\mathcal{L}^{0,\infty}(\upsilon,\nu)
.
The map

\displaystyle(x,y)\mapsto{}

\displaystyle\rho_{\bullet}(g\circ Q)(x,y)=[\rho_{\bullet}(g\circ Q)]_{x}(y)

\displaystyle=\rho([g\circ Q]_{x})(y)=\rho(g\circ Q_{x})(y)=g(\rho^{\prime}(Q_{x})(y))

is therefore
\Upsilon
-measurable for all
g\in C(\Gamma)
, and therefore

(x,y)\mapsto\rho^{\prime}(Q_{x})(y)
is
\Upsilon
-
\mathcal{B}
-measurable.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii) We apply (iii) with
\Gamma={\mathbb{R}}
. For
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\nu_{x})
, by
[
18
]
, (2) page 35, we have
g\circ\rho(f)=\rho_{x}(g\circ{f})
for any
g\in C({\mathbb{R}})
. From Remark
7.3
(i), it then follows that in this case we have
\rho^{\prime}_{x}=\rho_{x}
.
Thus, when
p=\infty
, the only distinction between (ii) and (iii) with
\Gamma={\mathbb{R}}
is that in (ii), the
Q_{x}
are essentially bounded, whereas in (iii) they are bounded.

We want to show that
\rho
is a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{0,\infty}(\upsilon,\nu)
. Let
f\in\mathcal{L}^{0,\infty}(\upsilon,\nu)
. We use an idea from the proof of Proposition
6.2
. Define
f_{n}=(-n)\vee(f\wedge n)
for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then
f_{n}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\upsilon)
and each section
[f_{n}]_{x}
is bounded. By (iii), the process
(\rho_{x}([f_{n}]_{x}))_{x\in X}
is
\Upsilon
-measurable. For each
x
, when
n
is large enough we have
f_{x}=_{\nu_{x}}[f_{n}]_{x}
, so
[\rho_{\bullet}(f)]_{x}=[\rho_{\bullet}(f_{n})]_{x}
. Thus,
\rho_{\bullet}(f)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\rho_{\bullet}(f_{n})
is
\Upsilon
-measurable, yielding
\rho_{\bullet}(f)\in\mathcal{L}^{0,\infty}(\upsilon,\nu)
, as desired.
∎

Corollary 7.7
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
, and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[C]
,
\varSigma\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
, and
\varSigma_{0}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
. Suppose we have a topology
\mathfrak{T}\subseteq T
on
Y
with
X\,\dot{\times}\,\mathfrak{T}\subseteq\Upsilon
.
For
\rho:=(\rho_{x})_{x\in X}
with
\rho_{x}\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
satisfying
(l1)
and
(l2)
, consider the following statements. In
(iii)
, assume
\rho_{x}\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu_{x})
for each
x
.

(i)

\rho
is a
C^{p}(Y)
-strong
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
.

(ii)

(\rho_{x}(Q_{x}))_{x\in X}
is a
\Upsilon
-measurable
\mathcal{L}^{p}
-process for every
\Upsilon
-measurable
\mathcal{L}^{p}
-process
(Q_{x})_{x\in X}

and
Q_{x}=\rho_{x}(Q_{x})
when
Q_{x}
is continuous.

(iii)

(\rho^{\prime}_{x}(Q_{x}))_{x\in X}
is
\Upsilon
-
\mathcal{B}
-measurable for every bounded
\Upsilon
-
\mathcal{B}
-measurable process
(Q_{x})_{x\in X}
with values in
\Gamma
, for any choice of
\Gamma
, and
\rho^{\prime}_{x}(Q_{x})=Q_{x}
when
Q_{x}
is continuous.

Then
(i)
is equivalent to
(ii)
. If
p=\infty
and
\rho_{x}\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu_{x})
for
x\in X
,
(i)
–
(iii)
are all equivalent.

Proof.

The equivalence of the statements (i) and (ii) is immediate by Theorem
7.5
. The assumption
X\,\dot{\times}\,\mathfrak{T}\subseteq\Upsilon
ensures for (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) that if
g\in C^{p}(Y)
then
Q=1\otimes g
is the associated function of a
\Upsilon
-measurable
\mathcal{L}^{p}
-process.
Now assume
p=\infty
and
\rho_{x}\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu_{x})
for each
x
.

For (i)
\Rightarrow
(iii), by Theorem
7.5
we get that
(\rho_{x}(Q_{x}))_{x\in{X}}
is
\Upsilon
-measurable for every bounded
\Upsilon
-
\mathcal{B}
-measurable process
(Q_{x})_{x\in{X}}
with values in
\Gamma
. The equality
\rho^{\prime}_{x}(Q_{x})=Q_{x}
in (iii) follows from
g\circ\rho^{\prime}_{x}(Q_{x})=\rho_{x}(g\circ Q_{x})=g\circ Q_{x}
for
g\in C(\Gamma)
.
The converse implication (iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii) is clear by Theorem
7.5
, taking
\Gamma={\mathbb{R}}
, which as pointed out before makes
\rho^{\prime}_{x}=\rho_{x}
.
∎

Example 7.8
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,T,\nu)
be probability spaces.

(a) Assume that
\nu
is complete. Every
\rho\in A\mathcal{G}(\nu)
is a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mu\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\nu)
by
[
26
]
, Theorem 2.6 along with Corollary
6.24
. It follows that the clauses (i) and (ii) of Theorem
7.5
hold. See
[
21
]
for the definition of
A\mathcal{G}(\nu)
.

(b) If
\nu
is complete and
\rho\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu)
is an admissibly generated lifting (see
[
25
]
, page 291 for the definition), then we get the existence of a lifting
\pi\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\mu\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\nu)
with
\rho
-invariant sections:
[\pi(f)]_{x}=\rho([\pi(f)]_{x})
for each
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mu\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\nu)
and each
x\in{X}
, by
[
25
]
, Theorem 2.13. By Corollary
6.24
, this property implies that
\rho
is a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mu\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\nu)
, and so the clauses of Theorem
7.5
all hold for

(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\varSigma\,\widehat{\otimes}\,T,\mu\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\nu)
.

(c) Let
(X\times{Y},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be a probability space satisfying
[C]
and
\varSigma\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
.
If
\eta:\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
satisfies
(l1)
and
(l2)
then

\eta
is a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\nu)})
by Example
6.11
.
It follows that the clauses of Theorem
7.5
hold for the spaces
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\nu)},\upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\nu)})
which satisfy
[C]
by Proposition
3.28
and satisfy
\varSigma_{0}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y\subseteq\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\nu)\subseteq\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\nu)}
. (Clause (iii) requires
p=\infty
,
\eta\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu)
.)

For part (a) of the next example, cf.
[
5
]
, Example 6.14(a).

Example 7.9
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
be topological probability spaces.

(a) If
\mu,\nu
are
\tau
-additive then from
[
4
]
, Theorem 2.15 we get a
\tau
-additive product space
(X\times Y,\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\Upsilon,\upsilon)
.
By the proof of (v) of that theorem,
[C]
holds if we replace
(\Upsilon,\upsilon)
by
(\overline{\Upsilon},\overline{\upsilon})
, where
\overline{\Upsilon}:=\mathfrak{B}(X\times Y)
,
\overline{\upsilon}:=\upsilon\upharpoonright\overline{\Upsilon}
. Extending by the ideal
\mathfrak{N}
to get
\overline{\Upsilon}_{\mathfrak{N}},\overline{\upsilon}_{\mathfrak{N}}
, we preserve
[C]
by
Proposition
3.28
.
If
\rho\in{V}^{p}(\mathfrak{T},\nu)
, then similarly to what we did in Example
7.8
(c), we may apply Example
6.11
in order to get that
\rho
is a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\overline{\upsilon}_{\mathfrak{N}})
. Then clauses (i) and (ii) of Corollary
7.7
hold for
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\overline{\Upsilon}_{\mathfrak{N}},\overline{\upsilon}_{\mathfrak{N}})
. Clause (iii) also holds when
p=\infty
,
\rho\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu)
.

(b) If
\nu
is complete and
\rho\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu)
is a strong admissibly generated lifting,
then there exists a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T}
-strong lifting
\pi\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\mu\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\nu)
with
\rho
-invariant sections as in Example
7.8
(b), and such that the product topology
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T}
consists of measurable sets by
[
22
]
, Theorem 4.1.
15
15

15

Recall that for topological probability spaces
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu)
, in general we don’t have
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T}\subseteq\Sigma\otimes T
(see
[
13
]
, Lemma 346K and
[
15
]
).

We then get, as in Example
7.8
(b), that
\rho\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu)
is a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mu\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\nu)
. But since
\pi
is
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T}
-strong, it follows by
[
14
]
, 453C, that
\pi
is
C(X\times{Y})\cap\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mu\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\nu)
-strong. Thus, the clause (i) of Corollary
7.7
holds, and so the clauses of that Corollary all hold for
(X,\mathfrak{S},\varSigma,\mu),\ \ (Y,\mathfrak{T},T,\nu),\ \ (X\times Y,\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{T},\varSigma\,\widehat{\otimes}\,T,\mu\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\nu)
.

Cohn
[
8
]
, Theorem 3 (see also Chung and Doob
[
7
]
) has given a necessary and sufficient condition for a process to have a measurable modification. The result of Cohn has been extended in
[
5
]
, Theorem 8.8. Hoffmann-Jørgensen
[
17
]
has given another necessary and sufficient condition, which only depends on the 2-dimensional marginal distributions of the process. Musiał
[
24
]
, Proposition 4.1 states another characterization of processes possessing a measurable modification. The next theorem is inspired by that statement. See Corollary
7.13
for the specialization to probability measures, and Remark
7.16
for how our result relates to the one in
[
24
]
. We begin with a technical fact.

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
X
be a set,
\mathcal{I}
an upwards directed family of subsets of
X
. Let

(Y,T_{x})_{x\in X}
be measurable spaces and for each
x\in X
, let
J_{x}
be an ideal of
T_{x}
. Write
T=(T_{x})_{x\in X}
,
J=(J_{x})_{x\in X}
.
Let
\Upsilon
be a
\sigma
-algebra on
X\times Y
such that
\Upsilon\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
.

(i)

If
(\mathcal{I}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon
then
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes J}[T]=\Upsilon[T]_{\{\emptyset\}\ltimes J}
.

If
(Y,T_{x},K_{x})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
,
x\in X
, and
J_{x}=K_{x}\cap T_{x}
,
K=(K_{x})_{x\in X}
then

(ii)

\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes K}[T]=\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes J}[T]
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.3
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\mathcal{D})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
, and so
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{D}}
is defined, for
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{I}\ltimes J
,
\mathcal{I}\ltimes K
, and similarly
(X\times Y,\Upsilon[T],\{\emptyset\}\ltimes J)\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
so that
\Upsilon[T]_{\{\emptyset\}\ltimes J}
is defined.

(i) Since
\Upsilon[T]_{\{\emptyset\}\ltimes J}\subseteq\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes J}
and for any
E\in\{\emptyset\}\ltimes J
and
x\in X
we have
E_{x}\in J_{x}\subseteq T_{x}
, we have
\Upsilon[T]_{\{\emptyset\}\ltimes J}\subseteq\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes J}[T]
.
Conversely, let
A\in\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes J}[T]
. Then for some
B\in\Upsilon
and
E\in\mathcal{I}\ltimes J
, we have
A=B\,\triangle\,E
. For some
N\in\mathcal{I}
, we have for all
x\in N^{c}
that
B_{x}\in T
and
E_{x}\in J_{x}
. It follows that
E\cap(N^{c}\times Y)\in\{\emptyset\}\ltimes J
. We also have
B\cap(N^{c}\times Y)\in\Upsilon
since
N\times Y\in(\mathcal{I}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon
, and hence
B\cap(N^{c}\times Y)\in\Upsilon[T]
. Therefore,
A\cap(N^{c}\times Y)=(B\,\triangle\,E)\cap(N^{c}\times Y)\in\Upsilon[T]_{\{\emptyset\}\ltimes J}
.
By assumption, for all
x\in X
,
A_{x}\in T_{x}
, so

A\cap(N\times Y)\in(\mathcal{I}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon[T]\subseteq\Upsilon[T]_{\{\emptyset\}\ltimes J}
. Thus,
A\in\Upsilon[T]_{\{\emptyset\}\ltimes J}
.

(ii) Since
J_{x}\subseteq K_{x}
for all
x
, we have
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes J}[T]\subseteq\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes K}[T]
.
Conversely, let
A\in\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes K}[T]
. Then for some
B\in\Upsilon\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
and
E\in\mathcal{I}\ltimes K
, we have
A=B\,\triangle\,E
. For some
N\in\mathcal{I}
, we have for all
x\in N^{c}
that
B_{x}\in T_{x}
and
E_{x}\in K_{x}
. But we also have by assumption
A_{x}\in T_{x}
, so
E_{x}=A_{x}\,\triangle\,B_{x}\in T_{x}
and therefore
E_{x}\in J_{x}
. It follows that
E\in\mathcal{I}\ltimes J
and thus
A\in\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes J}
.
∎

Theorem 7.11
.

Let
X
be a set,
\mathcal{I}
an upwards directed family of subsets of
X
. For
x\in X
, let
(Y,T_{x},K_{x})\in\mathbf{T}_{\sigma}
,
J_{x}:=K_{x}\cap T_{x}
. Write

T=(T_{x})_{x\in X}
,
J=(J_{x})_{x\in X}
,
K=(K_{x})_{x\in X}
,
T_{K}=((T_{x})_{K_{x}})_{x\in X}
.

Let
\Upsilon
be a
\sigma
-algebra on
X\times Y
such that
\Upsilon\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{T}=\{f\in{\mathbb{R}}^{X\times Y}:f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(T_{x}),\,x\in X\}
. For
f\in\mathcal{F}_{T}
,
consider the following statements, where
\Omega
is a
\sigma
-algebra with
\Upsilon\subseteq\Omega\subseteq\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes K}
.

(a)

f_{x}=_{J_{x}}g_{x}
for all
x\in X
, for some
\Upsilon
-measurable
g\in\mathcal{F}_{T}
.

(b)

f
is
\Upsilon[T]_{\{\emptyset\}\ltimes T_{0}}
-measurable.

(c)

f
is
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes J}
-measurable.

(d)

f
is
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes K}
-measurable.

(e)

f_{x}=_{K_{x}}g_{x}
for all
x\in X
, for some
\Omega
-measurable
g\in\mathcal{F}_{T_{K}}
.

The following implications hold:
(a)

\Leftrightarrow

(b)

\Rightarrow

(c)

\Leftrightarrow

(d)
,
(a)

\Rightarrow

(e)

\Rightarrow

(d)
. If
(\mathcal{I}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon
, then
(c)

\Rightarrow

(b)
and therefore all statements are equivalent.

Proof.

The implications (b)
\Rightarrow
(c)
\Rightarrow
(d) are clear. For the converses of these, if (d) holds, then by Corollary
2.18
,
f
is
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes K}[T]
-measurable, and hence, by Lemma
7.10
(ii), is
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes J}[T]
-measurable, giving (c).
If
(\mathcal{I}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon
and (c) holds, then by Corollary
2.18
,
f
is
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes J}[T]
-measurable, and hence, by Lemma
7.10
(i), is
\Upsilon[T]_{\{\emptyset\}\ltimes T_{0}}
-measurable, giving (b). Next we check that (a) and (b) are equivalent.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b) Assume that
g\in\mathcal{F}_{T}
is
\Upsilon
-measurable and
f_{x}=_{J_{x}}g_{x}
for
x\in X
. By Lemma
6.7
(iii) (taking
\mathcal{I}
,
\Upsilon
there to be
\{\emptyset\}
,
\Upsilon[T]
, respectively),

f
is
\Upsilon[T]_{\{\emptyset\}\ltimes J}
-measurable.

(b)
\Rightarrow
(a)
Suppose
(f_{x})_{x\in X}
is a
\Upsilon[T]_{\{\emptyset\}\ltimes J}
-measurable process. By Lemma
3.1
(ii), there is a real-valued
\Upsilon[T]
-measurable function
g
on
X\times Y
such that
g=_{\{\emptyset\}\ltimes J}f
. For each
x\in X
,
g_{x}
is
T
-measurable by Proposition
2.16
(i) (with
\mathcal{I}=\{\emptyset\}
), giving
g\in\mathcal{F}_{T}
.
From
g=_{\{\emptyset\}\ltimes J}f
we get that for each
x\in X
,
g_{x}=_{J_{x}}f_{x}
, as required.

The implication (a)
\Rightarrow
(e) is obvious since
\Upsilon\subseteq\Omega
, so there remains to show (e)
\Rightarrow
(d). For this we apply (a)
\Rightarrow
(c) to the structures obtained by replacing
T_{x}
,
J_{x}
and
\Upsilon
with
(T_{x})_{K_{x}}
,
K_{x}
and
\Omega
, respectively. For the hypothesis corresponding to
\Upsilon\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T
, we have
\Omega\subseteq\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes K}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\ltimes T_{K}
. We have
f\in\mathcal{F}_{T}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{T_{K}}
, so (a)
\Rightarrow
(c) in this instance gives that (e) implies that
f
is
\Omega_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes K}
-measurable, and therefore is
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes K}
-measurable since
\Omega_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes K}\subseteq(\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes K})_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes K}=\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes K}
. Thus, (d) holds.
∎

Remark 7.12
.

Assume that
(\mathcal{I}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon
holds and there exists an
\eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in X}
, where each
\eta_{x}\colon\mathcal{L}^{0}(T_{x})\to\mathcal{L}^{0}(T_{x})
satisfies
(l1)
and
(l2)
(modulo
K_{x}
) and
\eta
is a
2
-marginal for
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon)
.
Then starting with
f\in\mathcal{F}_{T}
being
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes K}
-measurable as in (d), we directly get
g\in\mathcal{F}_{T}
as in (a) by taking
g=\eta_{\bullet}(f)
.

Proof.

By
(l1)
for the
\eta_{x}
,
g\in\mathcal{F}_{T}
and
f_{x}=_{K_{x}}g_{x}
for all
x\in X
. There remains to show
g
is
\Upsilon
-measurable.
By Lemma
3.1
(ii), there is an
h\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon)
such that
f=_{\mathcal{I}\ltimes K}h
.
There is an
N\in\mathcal{I}
such that for
x\in N^{c}
, we have
h_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(T)
and
f_{x}=_{K_{x}}h_{x}
. By redefining
h
to be zero on
N\times Y
, we get
h\in\mathcal{F}_{T}
. The function
\eta_{\bullet}(h)
is
\Upsilon
-measurable since
\eta
is a
2
-marginal for
\mathcal{L}^{0}(\Upsilon)
. By
(l2)
, when
x\in N^{c}
,
\eta_{x}(f_{x})=\eta_{x}(h_{x})
. Hence
\eta_{\bullet}(f)
and
\eta_{\bullet}(h)
have the same restriction to
N^{c}\times Y
. By
(\mathcal{I}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon
, it follows that
g=\eta_{\bullet}(f)
is
\Upsilon
-measurable.
∎

Corollary 7.13
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
, and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[C]
. Write
Consider the following statements.

(a)

(Q_{x})_{x\in{X}}
has a
\Upsilon
-measurable modification
(U_{x})_{x\in{X}}
over
(Y,T)
.

(b)

(Q_{x})_{x\in{X}}
is
\Upsilon[T]_{\{\emptyset\}\ltimes T_{0}}
-measurable.

(c)

(Q_{x})_{x\in{X}}
is
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\nu)}
-measurable.

(d)

(Q_{x})_{x\in{X}}
is
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}
-measurable.

(e)

Q_{x}=_{\widehat{\nu}_{x}}U_{x}
,
x\in X
, for some
\widehat{\Upsilon}
-measurable
U\colon X\times Y\to{\mathbb{R}}
, where
U_{x}
is
\widehat{\nu}_{x}
-measurable.

The following implications hold:
(a)

\Leftrightarrow

(b)

\Rightarrow

(c)

\Leftrightarrow

(d)
,
(a)

\Rightarrow

(e)

\Rightarrow

(d)
. If
(\varSigma_{0}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon
, then
(c)

\Rightarrow

(b)
and therefore all statements are equivalent.

Proof.

This follows immediately from the theorem, taking
\Omega=\widehat{\Upsilon}
.
∎

Remark 7.14
.

The equivalence of (d) and (e) Corollary
7.13
holds under the milder assumption that
\varSigma_{0}\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
. Since
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}=\widehat{\Upsilon}_{\mathfrak{N}}
, for the equivalence of (d) and (e), we can apply Theorem
7.11
to the spaces
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,\widehat{T}_{x},\widehat{\nu}_{x})_{x\in X}
,
(X\times Y,\widehat{\Upsilon},\widehat{\upsilon})
, where
\widehat{T}_{x}
is the domain of
\widehat{\nu}_{x}
. (
[C]
holds by Proposition
2.19
(v).) Now the assumption
(\mathcal{I}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon
holds since
\varSigma_{0}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y\subseteq\widehat{\Upsilon}
follows from
\varSigma_{0}\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
.
Some assumption is needed because if we take
(X,\varSigma,\mu)=(Y,T,\nu)=[0,1]
with Lebesgue measure,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)=
the trivial probability having
X\times Y
as its only nonempty measurable set then all the measures are complete. The function
Q=\chi_{\{0\}\times Y}
is
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}
-measurable since
\{0\}\times Y\in\mathfrak{N}
, but if
U_{x}=_{\nu}Q_{x}
for all
x
, then
U
must take both the values
0
and
1
and hence is not
\Upsilon
-measurable.

Remark 7.15
.

For
0\leq p\leq\infty
, we could stipulate in clause (a) of Corollary
7.13
that
(U_{x})_{x\in X}
is an
\mathcal{L}^{p}
-process and in clauses (b)–(d) that
(Q_{x})_{x\in X}
is an
\mathcal{L}^{p}
-process, since a modification of an
\mathcal{L}^{p}
-process is an
\mathcal{L}^{p}
-process. Then, similar to Remark
7.12
, if
(\varSigma_{0}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon
holds and there exists an
\eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in X}
, where each
\eta_{x}\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(T_{x})\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(T_{x})
satisfies
(l1)
and
(l2)
and
\eta
is a
2
-marginal for
\mathcal{L}^{0,p}(\Upsilon)
, then for any process
(Q_{x})_{x\in X}
which is
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}
-measurable, we get a
\Upsilon
-measurable modification
(U_{x})_{x\in X}
by taking
U_{x}=\eta_{\bullet}(Q_{x})
. In the proof of Proposition 4.1 of
[
24
]
, liftings for
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}
were used to show that (d) implies (e).

Remark 7.16
.

In Proposition 4.1 of
[
24
]
, (using our notation) a product r.c.p.
(\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
for a measure
\upsilon
is given (see Example
2.15
), and a process on
(Y,T)
is defined to be measurable if it is
\widehat{\upsilon}
-measurable.
The proof that a
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}
-measurable process has a measurable modification produces a family of variables
(U_{x})_{x\in X}
where
U_{x}
is
\widehat{\nu}_{x}
-measurable.
By our definition, this is not a process because the
U_{x}
are not all measurable with respect to the same
\sigma
-algebra. Nevertheless, it fits into the framework of Corollary
7.13
as statement (e) (see Remark
7.14
), and was the motivation for including statement (e).

Corollary 7.17
.

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu_{x})_{x\in X}
, and
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be probability spaces satisfying
[C]
. Let
(Q_{x})_{x\in{X}}
be a process over
(Y,T)
with values in
{\mathbb{R}}
.
The following statements are equivalent:

(i)

(Q_{x})_{x\in{X}}
has a
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}
-measurable modification
(U_{x})_{x\in{X}}
over
(Y,T)
.

(ii)

(Q_{x})_{x\in{X}}
is
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}
-measurable.

Proof.

For (i) implies (ii), let
U
be a
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}
-measurable modification of
(Q_{x})_{x\in{X}}
. Then
U
is
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}[T]
-measurable by
Corollary
2.18
. The restriction of
\upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}
to
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}[T]
satisfies
[C]
.
(By Proposition
3.28
(i),
\upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\nu)}
satisfies
[C]
, and hence so does its restriction to
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}(\mu,\nu)}[T]=\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}[T]
(Lemma
7.10
(ii)) which is the same as the restriction of
\upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}
to that
\sigma
-algebra.) We may therefore apply the theorem to that restriction in the place of
\upsilon
to conclude that

Q
is
(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}[T])_{\mathfrak{N}}
-measurable. Since
(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}[T])_{\mathfrak{N}}\subseteq(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}})_{\mathfrak{N}}=\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{N}}
, (ii) follows.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) is immediate by taking
U=Q
.
∎

8.
Fubini type products

Let
(X,\varSigma,\mu)
,
(Y,T,\nu)
,
(X\times Y,\Upsilon,\upsilon)
be fixed probability spaces, and let
p\in[0,\infty]
.
Also fix
\delta\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{X}
and
\eta\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{Y}
.
In this section we work with real scalars assuming throughout that
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{R}}
.

Definition 8.1
.

Define

\delta\odot\eta\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{X\times{Y}}
by the formula
\delta\odot\eta:=\delta^{\bullet}\circ\eta_{\bullet}
, i.e.,

(\delta\odot\eta)(f)(x,y):=\delta([\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y})(x)\;\;\mbox{if}\;[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)\;\mbox{and}\;(\gamma\odot\delta)(f)(x,y):=0\;\mbox{otherwise}

for all
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
and
(x,y)\in{X}\times{Y}
.

Remark 8.2
.

We can consider the composition
\eta\odot_{t}\delta:=\eta_{\bullet}\circ\delta^{\bullet}
instead of
\delta^{\bullet}\circ\eta_{\bullet}
.
It is easy to derive from results about
\delta\odot\eta
corresponding results for the product
\eta\odot_{t}\delta
. By
[
26
]
, Theorem
3.2, when
\upsilon=\mu\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\nu
we have
\delta\odot\eta\not=\eta\odot_{t}\delta
if the spaces

(X,\varSigma,\mu)
and
(Y,T,\nu)
are not purely atomic, i.e. practically in all
interesting cases.

Definition 8.3

(Cf.
[
27
]
, Definition 3.2)
.

We say that
\eta

generates
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
-sections
, if
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
for all
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
and
y\in Y
.
If
\eta
is a primitive lifting for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
which is a 2-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
and generates
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
-sections, then we say
\eta
is
\mu
-
smooth
.

The next result corresponds to
[
27
]
, Lemma 3.1, concerning primitive liftings for sets, and has a similar proof, which we enclose for completeness.

Lemma 8.4
.

Assume that
[C]
holds,
\mu
is complete,
(\varSigma_{0}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon
, and
\eta
is a primitive lifting for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
which is a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
. Then
\eta
is
\mu
-smooth if and only if
there exists a map
\varphi:\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
satisfying
(l1)
, such that for all
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
, there exists an
N_{f}\in\varSigma_{0}
such that for all
x\notin N_{f}
,
\eta([\varphi(f)]_{x})=[\varphi(f)]_{x}
, and
[\varphi(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
for every
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
and
y\in Y
. In this statement we can also take each
N_{f}
to be empty.

Proof.

Consider a map
\varphi\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)\to\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
satisfying
(l1)
and such that for all
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
there exists a set
N_{f}\in\varSigma_{0}
with
\eta([\varphi(f)]_{x})=[\varphi(f)]_{x}
for all
x\notin{N}_{f}
. There is such a map by Corollary
6.24
(and we can ask that
N_{f}=\emptyset
for all
f
). By
[C]
and
(l1)
, there is an
M_{f}\in\varSigma_{0}
such that
f_{x},[\varphi(f)]_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu_{x})
and
f_{x}=_{\nu}[\varphi(f)]_{x}
for all
x\notin M_{f}
. For
y\in{Y}
and
x\notin N_{f}\cup M_{f}
we get

[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}(x)=\eta(f_{x})(y)=\eta([\varphi(f)]_{x})(y)=[\varphi(f)]_{x}(y)=[\varphi(f)]^{y}(x).

Since
\mu
is complete, it follows that
\{[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\not=[\varphi(f)]^{y}\}\in\varSigma_{0}
. Thus,
[\varphi(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
if and only if
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
.
∎

Lemma 8.5
.

The map
\delta\odot\eta:\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{X\times{Y}}

has the following properties. In
(iii)
,
(v)
,
(vi)
, assume that
[C]
holds. In
(vi)
,
(vii)
, assume also that
\varSigma_{0}\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
holds.

(i)

\delta([\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y})=[(\delta\odot\eta)(f)]^{y}
for all
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
and all
y\in{Y}
with
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
.

(ii)

[(\delta\odot\eta)(f)]^{y}=\delta([(\delta\odot\eta)(f)]^{y})
for all
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
and all
y\in{Y}
.

(iii)

If
\mu
is complete,
\delta\odot\eta
satisfies
(l2)
.

(iv)

If
\delta
and
\eta
fix the constant function with value
k
on
X
and
Y
, respectively, then
\delta\odot\eta
fixes the constant function with value
k
on
X\times Y
.

(v)

If
\delta
and
\eta
are homogeneous then
\delta\odot\eta\in\delta\otimes\eta
.

(vi)

If
\delta\in V^{p}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mu)
,
\eta\in V^{p}_{\mathbb{R}}(\nu)
, and
\eta
generates
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
-sections, then
\delta\odot\eta
is linear:
(\delta\odot\eta)(af+g)=a(\delta\odot\eta)(f)+(\delta\odot\eta)(g)

for all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
f,g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
.

(vii)

If
\delta\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\mu)
,
\eta\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu)
, and
\eta
generates
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mu)
-sections, then
\delta\odot\eta
is multiplicative:
(\delta\odot\eta)(fg)=(\delta\odot\eta)(f)(\delta\odot\eta)(g)

for all
f,g\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\upsilon)
.

(viii)

If
\delta
and
\eta
are positive then so is
\delta\odot\eta
.

Recall from Definition
2.7
that positivity of a primitive lifting means that it takes nonnegative values on nonnegative functions.

Proof.

(i) follows by Definition
8.1
.

(ii) If
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
and
y\in{Y}
then

[(\delta\odot\eta)(f)]^{y}
equals either
\delta\left([\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\right)
if
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
, or

0
otherwise. Either way we get
[(\delta\odot\eta)(f)]^{y}=\delta\left([(\delta\odot\eta)(f)]^{y}\right)
.

(iii) By Lemma
6.14
, from
f=_{\upsilon}g
and completeness of
\mu
we get that for all
y
,
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}=_{\mu}[\eta_{\bullet}(g)]^{y}
, i.e.,
\mu([\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\,\triangle\,[\eta_{\bullet}(g)]^{y})=0
, but the sets
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}
,
[\eta_{\bullet}(g)]^{y}
might not be measurable. If these sets are measurable, it follows that
[(\delta\odot\eta)(f)]^{y}=\delta([\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y})=\delta([\eta_{\bullet}(g)]^{y})=[(\delta\odot\eta)(g)]^{y}
. Otherwise we have
[(\delta\odot\eta)(f)]^{y}=0=[(\delta\odot\eta)(g)]^{y}
.

(iv) Obvious from the definitions.

(v) If
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
h\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
, then

[\eta_{\bullet}(g\otimes h)]^{y}=[g\otimes\eta(h)]^{y}=g\eta(h)(y)\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
by Lemma
6.13
(ii), so using (i) we get

[(\delta\odot\eta)(g\otimes{h})]^{y}=\delta(\left[\eta_{\bullet}(g\otimes{h})\right]^{y})=\eta(h)(y)\delta(g)=[\delta(g)\otimes\eta(h)]^{y}
.

(vi) Let
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Since
\delta\odot\eta
satisfies
(l2)
by (iii), it follows from Proposition
2.19
(iv) and Corollary
2.21
(here we use
\varSigma_{0}\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
) that we may assume that
f_{x},g_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
for all
x\in X
.
Then from Lemma
6.13
(iv) it follows that
\eta_{\bullet}(af+g)=a\eta_{\bullet}(f)+\eta_{\bullet}(g)
. Since
\eta
generates
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
-sections, we then get for any
y\in Y
that
[(\delta\odot\eta)(af+g)]^{y}=\delta([\eta_{\bullet}(af+g)]^{y})=a\delta([\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y})+\delta([\eta_{\bullet}(g)]^{y})=a[(\delta\odot\eta)(f)]^{y}+[(\delta\odot\eta)(g)]^{y}
.

(vii) As in (vi), we may assume that
f_{x},g_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\nu)
for all
x\in X
. For all
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\upsilon)
and all
y\in Y
applying Lemma
6.13
(iv)(c) and the assumption that
\eta
generates
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mu)
-sections, we get

\displaystyle[(\delta\odot\eta)(fg)]^{y}

\displaystyle=[\delta([\eta_{\bullet}(fg)]^{y})=\delta([\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}[\eta_{\bullet}(g)]^{y})

\displaystyle=\delta([\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y})\delta([\eta_{\bullet}(g)]^{y})=[(\delta\odot\eta)(f)]^{y}[(\delta\odot\eta)(g)]^{y}

(viii) Let
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
with
f\geq{0}
and let
y\in{Y}
. Since
\eta
is positive, Lemma
6.13
(iii) gives
\eta_{\bullet}(f)\geq 0
. Then for
y\in{Y}
,
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\geq 0
, so if
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
we get
[(\delta\odot\eta)(f)]^{y}=\delta([\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y})\geq{0}
since
\delta
is positive. If
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\notin\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
then
[(\delta\odot\eta)(f)]^{y}=0
.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

There is now an obvious question, whether when
\delta
and
\eta
are primitive liftings for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
, respectively, the map
\delta\odot\eta
is a primitive lifting for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
. As it is shown in
[
27
]
, Remark 5.1, when
\upsilon=\mu\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\nu
, if
\delta\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\mu)
and
\eta\in\Lambda^{\infty}(\nu)
it may happen that

\delta\odot\eta
does not satisfy (l1).

Definition 8.7

(Cf.
[
27
]
, Definition 4.2)
.

The pair
(\delta,\eta)
is
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
-preserving
if
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
implies
(\delta\odot\eta)(f)\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
.

Proposition 8.8
.

If
\delta
is a
1
-marginal and
\eta
is a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
, then the pair
(\delta,\eta)
is
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
-preserving.

Proof.

Let
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
be arbitrary. Then
\eta_{\bullet}(f)\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
by the marginality of
\eta
and therefore

(\delta\odot\eta)(f)=\delta^{\bullet}(\eta_{\bullet}(f))\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
by the marginality of
\delta
.
∎

Definition 8.9

(Cf.
[
27
]
, Definition 3.2)
.

\eta
is a
weak
2
-marginal
with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
if for each
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
there is an
M_{f}\in T_{0}
such that
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y},f^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}=_{\mu}f^{y}
for all
y\notin M_{f}
.

Remark 8.10
.

When
[C]
and
[\overline{C}]
hold, and
(\varSigma_{0}\,\widehat{\times}\,Y)[T]\subseteq\Upsilon
, a
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
is also a weak
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
.

Proof.

By Corollary
6.24
,
\eta_{\bullet}
satisfies
(l1)
on
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
.
Then for each
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
we have
\eta_{\bullet}(f)\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
and
\eta_{\bullet}(f)=_{\upsilon}f
. Then by
[\overline{C}]
, there is an
M_{f}\in T_{0}
such that
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y},f^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}=_{\mu}f^{y}
for all
y\notin M_{f}
.
∎

Under some mild assumptions,
\delta\odot\eta
satisfies
(l1)
if and only if
(\delta,\eta)
is
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
-preserving and
\eta
is a weak
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
. The following proposition is modelled on Proposition 4.3 of
[
27
]
which establishes that equivalence in the context of liftings for sets with
\upsilon=\mu\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\nu
. As pointed out in the footnote to
[
3
]
, Remark 5.7 (b), the proof given there has a gap. We take the opportunity in Corollary
8.14
to fill that gap. The new proof also eliminates completeness assumptions on the measures.

Proposition 8.11
.

Assume
[\overline{C}]
holds,
\delta
is a primitive lifting for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
,
\eta\colon\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{Y}
.

(i)

If
(\delta,\eta)
is
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
-preserving and
\eta
is a weak
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
then
\delta\odot\eta
satisfies
(l1)
.

(ii)

Assume that
\varSigma\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
. Suppose that either
(a)

X
is not an atom, or
(b)

X
is an atom,
[C]
holds,
X\,\dot{\times}\,T\subseteq\Upsilon
, and
\eta
satisfies
(l2)
.
If
\delta\odot\eta
satisfies
(l1)
then
(\delta,\eta)
is
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
-preserving and
\eta
is a weak
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
.

Proof.

(i)
The argument in
[
27
]
adapts easily to the present setting.
Assume
(\delta,\eta)
is
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
-preserving and
\eta
is a weak
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
. Write
\varphi:=\delta\odot\eta
. Let
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
. Then
\varphi(f)\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
and for some
M\in T_{0}
,
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y},f^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}=_{\mu}f^{y}
when
y\notin M
.
Let
F=\{\varphi(f)\not=f\}
. We have
F\in\Upsilon
. When
y\notin M
,

[\varphi(f)]^{y}=\delta([\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y})=\delta(f^{y})=_{\mu}f^{y}
, where we used that
\delta
is a primitive lifting for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
, giving
\mu(F^{y})=\mu\{[\varphi(f)]^{y}\not=f^{y}\}=0
.
It follows from
[\overline{C}]
that
\upsilon(F)=0
. Thus,
\varphi(f)=_{\upsilon}f
and
(l1)
holds.

(ii) Assume that
(l1)
holds for
\varphi=\delta\odot\eta
. Then in particular the pair
(\delta,\eta)
is
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
-preserving.
To prove that
\eta
is a weak
2
-marginal for
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
, let
f\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
.

Claim 8.12
.

For each
N\in\varSigma_{0}
,
\nu^{*}(G_{N})=0
, where
G_{N}=\{y\in Y:[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\upharpoonright N\notin\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu\upharpoonright N)\}
.

Proof.

Let
N\in\varSigma_{0}
. Consider the function
g
which agrees with
f
on
N\times Y
and is identically equal to
1
on
N^{c}\times Y
. This function
g
belongs to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
since
\varSigma\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
.
By
[\overline{C}]
and
(l1)
for
\varphi
, let
M\in T_{0}
satisfy that
y\notin M
implies
[\varphi(g)]^{y},g^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
[\varphi(g)]^{y}=_{\mu}g^{y}=_{\mu}1
.

For
y\in G_{N}
, we have
[\eta_{\bullet}(g)]^{y}\upharpoonright N=[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\upharpoonright N\notin\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu\upharpoonright N)
implying that
[\eta_{\bullet}(g)]^{y}\notin\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and hence
[\varphi(g)]^{y}=0
, so that
y\in M
. Thus
G_{N}\subseteq M
and the claim follows.
∎

Claim 8.13
.

\nu^{*}(K)=0
, where
K=\{y\in Y:[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\notin\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)\}
.

Proof.

We treat two cases, corresponding to assumptions (a) and (b).

(a)
X
is not an atom.

This is similar to the proof of Claim
8.12
.
Write
X=A_{0}\cup A_{1}
where
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
are disjoint sets of positive measure. Then
K=K_{0}\cup K_{1}
, where for
i=0,1
,
K_{i}=\{y\in K:[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\upharpoonright A_{i}\notin\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu\upharpoonright A_{i})\}
.
It will suffice to show that
\nu^{*}(K_{i})=0
for
i=0,1
. We show
\nu^{*}(K_{0})=0
, the case
i=1
being similar. Consider the function
g
which agrees with
f
on
A_{0}\times Y
and is identically equal to
1
on
A_{1}\times Y
. We have
g\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
.
By
[\overline{C}]
and
(l1)
for
\varphi
, let
M\in T_{0}
satisfy that
y\notin M
implies
[\varphi(g)]^{y},g^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and
[\varphi(g)]^{y}=_{\mu}g^{y}
which is not
=_{\mu}0
since it equals
1
on
A_{1}
.

For
y\in K_{0}
,
[\eta_{\bullet}(g)]^{y}\upharpoonright A_{0}=[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\upharpoonright A_{0}\notin\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu\upharpoonright A_{0})
implying that
[\eta_{\bullet}(g)]^{y}\notin\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
and hence
[\varphi(g)]^{y}=0
. Thus,
K_{0}\subseteq M
and hence
\nu^{*}(K_{0})=0
, as desired.

(b)
X
is an atom,
[C]
holds,
X\,\dot{\times}\,T\subseteq\Upsilon
, and
\eta
satisfies
(l2)
.

By Example
3.4
, there is a
w\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\nu)
such that the
(X\,\dot{\times}\,T)
-measurable function
g\colon X\times Y\to{\mathbb{R}}
given by
g(x,y)=w(y)
satisfies
f=_{\upsilon}g
.
By
[C]
, there exists a set
P\in\varSigma_{0}
, such that
x\notin P
implies
f_{x}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
, and
f_{x}=_{\nu}g_{x}=w
. Since
\eta
satisfies
(l2)
,

[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]_{x}=\eta(w)
when
x\in P^{c}
, so that for any
y\in Y
,
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\upharpoonright P^{c}=\eta(w)(y)
is constant, in particular,
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\upharpoonright P^{c}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
. For
y\notin G_{P}
we have
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\upharpoonright P\in\mathcal{L}^{0}(\mu)
and hence
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
. Thus,
K\subseteq G_{P}
and Claim
8.13
follows by Claim
8.12
.
∎

With this claim, the proof proceeds by adapting the argument in
[
27
]
.
From
[\overline{C}]
and
(l1)
we get
M\in T_{0}
, such that
y\notin M
implies
[\varphi(f)]^{y},f^{y}\in\varSigma
, and
[\varphi(f)]^{y}=_{\mu}f^{y}
. By Claim
8.13
, we can also take
M
so that
y\notin M
implies
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}\in\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
.
For
y\in M^{c}
we have
[\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y}=_{\mu}\delta([\eta_{\bullet}(f)]^{y})=[\varphi(f)]^{y}=_{\mu}f^{y}
,
and therefore
\eta
is a weak
2
-marginal with respect to
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
.
∎

We similarly have the following corollary of the preceding proof, giving a version of the proposition for maps on the family of measurable sets instead of on
\mathcal{L}^{p}
. Except for the new clause (c) in (ii), it follows by a straightforward modification to the proof of Proposition
8.11
.

For set maps
\delta\in\mathcal{P}(X)^{\varSigma}
,
\eta\in\mathcal{P}(Y)^{T}
the notions in this section have meanings analogous to the ones given above. For
E\subseteq X\times Y
,
\delta^{\bullet}(E)=\{(x,y)\in X\times Y:x\in\delta(E^{y}),\,E^{y}\in\varSigma\}
and

\eta_{\bullet}(E)=\{(x,y)\in X\times Y:y\in\delta(E_{x}),\,E_{x}\in T\}
. Then the pair
(\delta,\eta)
is
\Upsilon
-preserving if
E\in\Upsilon
implies
(\delta\odot\eta)(f):=\delta^{\bullet}(\eta_{\bullet}(E))\in\Upsilon
.
We say
\eta
is a weak
2
-marginal with respect to
\Upsilon
if for each
E\in\Upsilon
there is an
M_{E}\in T_{0}
such that
[\eta_{\bullet}(E)]^{y},E^{y}\in\Upsilon
and
[\eta_{\bullet}(E)]^{y}=_{\mu}E^{y}
for all
y\notin M_{E}
.

Corollary 8.14
.

Assume
[\overline{C}]
holds,
\varSigma\,\dot{\times}\,Y\subseteq\Upsilon
,
\delta
is a primitive lifting for
\mu
, and
\eta\in\mathcal{P}(Y)^{T}
.

(i)

If
(\delta,\eta)
is
\Upsilon
-preserving and
\eta
is a weak
2
-marginal with respect to
\Upsilon
then
\delta\odot\eta
satisfies
(L1)
.

(ii)

Suppose that one of the following holds:
(a)

X
is not an atom;
(b)

X
is an atom,
[C]
holds,
X\,\dot{\times}\,T\subseteq\Upsilon
, and
\eta
satisfies
(L2)
;

(c)

X
is an atom,
X\,\dot{\times}\,T\subseteq\Upsilon
,
\eta
satisfies
(V)
and
\mu
is complete.
If
\delta\odot\eta
satisfies
(L1)
then
(\delta,\eta)
is
\Upsilon
-preserving and
\eta
is a weak
2
-marginal with respect to
\Upsilon
.

Proof.

Repeat the proof of Proposition
8.11
, making appropriate changes to the notation: in the whole proof, replace
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\upsilon)
by
\Upsilon
; in (i), replace
f
by a set
E\in\Upsilon
, and take
F=\varphi(E)\,\triangle\,E
; in (ii), fix a set
E\in\Upsilon
, replacing
f
by
E
in the rest of the proof, replace the restriction symbol
\upharpoonright
by the intersection symbol
\cap
, replace
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu)
,
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu\upharpoonright N)
and
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\mu\upharpoonright A_{i})
by
\varSigma
and
\mathcal{L}^{p}(\nu)
by
T
(for any exponent
p
), and replace the constant functions
0
and
1
by the sets
\emptyset
and
X
.
Claim
8.12
now reads as follows.

Claim 8.15
.

For each
N\in\varSigma_{0}
,
\nu^{*}(G_{N})=0
, where
G_{N}=\{y\in Y:[\eta_{\bullet}(E)]^{y}\cap N\notin\varSigma\}
.

In the proof of Claim
8.12
, replace
g
by
F=E\cup(N^{c}\times Y)
. Claim
8.13
now reads as follows.

Claim 8.16
.

\nu^{*}(K)=0
, where
K=\{y\in Y:[\eta_{\bullet}(E)]^{y}\notin\varSigma\}
.

In the proof of Claim
8.13
, in (a), replace
g
by
F=E\cup(A_{1}\times Y)
. In (b), and also for (c), the fact that
X
is an atom and
[C]
holds with
X\,\dot{\times}\,T\subseteq\Upsilon
, gives us (from Example
3.4
again)
an
A_{E}\in T
and an
N_{E}\in T_{0}
such that
E=_{\upsilon}F:=X\times A
(
F
replaces
g
) and
y\notin N_{E}
implies
E^{y}\in\varSigma
and
\mu(E^{y})=\chi_{A_{E}}(y)
.
The value
\eta(w)(y)
becomes
P^{c}
or
\emptyset
depending on whether
y\in\eta(A_{E})
.

That leaves the proof of Claim
8.16
under the assumptions in (c), namely that
X
is an atom,
\eta
satisfies
(V)
and
\mu
is complete. For that, we will make use of the following claim.

Claim 8.17
.

If
y\in A_{E}\setminus N_{E}
then
[\eta_{\bullet}(E)]^{y}\in\varSigma
and
\mu([\eta_{\bullet}(E)]^{y})=1
.

Proof.

When
y\in A_{E}\setminus N_{E}
,
[\varphi(E)]^{y}=_{\mu}E^{y}
, so
[\varphi(E)]^{y}
has measure one since
y\in A_{E}\setminus N_{E}
. It follows that
[\eta_{\bullet}(E)]^{y}\in\varSigma
and
\delta([\eta_{\bullet}(E)]^{y})=[\varphi(E)]^{y}
has measure one. Therefore
\mu([\eta_{\bullet}(E)]^{y})=1
.
∎

Since
\eta
satisfies
(V)
, so does
\eta_{\bullet}
, and then we get
[\eta_{\bullet}(E)]^{y}\cap[\eta_{\bullet}(E^{c})]^{y}=\emptyset
for all
y
. By Claim
8.17
applied to
E^{c}
,
y\in A_{E^{c}}\setminus N_{E^{c}}
implies
\mu([\eta_{\bullet}(E^{c})]^{y})=1
, and then by completeness of
\mu
we get
\mu([\eta_{\bullet}(E)]^{y})=0
, in particular
[\eta_{\bullet}(E)]^{y}\in\varSigma
.
We also have that

y\in A_{E}\setminus N_{E}
implies
[\eta_{\bullet}(E)]^{y}\in\varSigma
and
\mu([\eta_{\bullet}(E)]^{y})=1
, so it follows that
(A_{E}\setminus N_{E})\cap(A_{E^{c}}\setminus N_{E^{c}})=\emptyset
.
We have by
[\overline{C}]
that
\upsilon(E)=\upsilon(X\times A_{E})=\nu(A_{E})
and similarly for
E^{c}
, so the set
(A_{E}\setminus N_{E})\cup(A_{E^{c}}\setminus N_{E^{c}})
has
\nu
-measure one and is disjoint from
K
. Claim
8.16
follows.

Using Claim
8.16
, the proof is completed as in the proof of Proposition
8.11
, making appropriate changes as above.
∎

The following example shows that the assumption that
\eta
satisfies
(L2)
in (ii)(b) cannot be deleted.

Example 8.18
.

Let
X=Y=[0,1]
,
\mu=
Lebesgue measure restricted to Borel sets of measure
0
or
1
,
\nu=
Lebesgue measure,
\upsilon=\mu\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\nu
.

Note that each
\mu\otimes\nu
-measurable set
E
has the property that for some measure zero set
P\subseteq X
,
E\cap(P^{c}\times Y)=P^{c}\times A
for some measurable set
A
(since these sets form a
\sigma
-algebra containing the products
U\times V
, where
U
is
\mu
-measurable and
V
is
\nu
-measurable). The measure zero sets for
\upsilon
are therefore the sets which are contained in
(P\times Y)\cup(X\times Q)
for some measure zero sets
P,Q
.

Let
u\colon X\to[0,1/2)
and
h\colon X\to[1/2,1]
be functions such that
u
is one-to-one and has measure zero range, and each fiber
h^{-1}(y)
of
h
is a Bernstein subset of
X
,
y\in[1/2,1]
. Let
\delta
be the unique lifting for
\mu
, and let
\eta_{0}
